Meeting of the Board 
4 – 7 March 2024 
Kigali, Rwanda 
Provisional agenda item 10 
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
12 February 2024 
Consideration of funding proposals – 
Addendum X 
Funding proposal package for FP230 
Summary 
This addendum contains the following six parts: 
a) A funding proposal summary titled "Kuali Fund-GCF” submitted by Compañia 
Española de Financiación del Desarrollo S.A.S.M.E. (COFIDES);
b) No-objection letter(s) issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal 
point(s);
c) Environmental and Social report(s) disclosure;
d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment;
e) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's 
assessment; and
f) Gender documentation of the funding proposal.
These documents are presented as submitted by the accredited entity and the national designated 
authority(ies) or focal point(s), respectively. Pursuant to the Comprehensive Information Disclosure 
Policy of the Fund, the funding proposal titled “Kuali Fund-GCF” submitted by Compañia Española de 
Financiación del Desarrollo S.A. S.M.E. (COFIDES) is being circulated on a limited distribution basis 
only to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, 
legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template 
• 
Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-
reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. 
• 
Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details 
provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be 
reflected in all relevant documents.  
• 
The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not 
exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual 
service standard time. 
• 
The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  
• 
Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals 
will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, 
subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. 
Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.    
 
Please submit the completed proposal to: 
fundingproposal@gcfund.org 
Please use the following name convention for the file name: 
“FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]”

A.20. Executing Entity 
information 
GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC S.A., is a private limited liability firm specialized in impact investment 
management services. GAWA is registered as Alternative Investment Fund Manager (AIFM) and 
supervised by the Spanish Exchange Commission (CNMV). GAWA will act as fund and TAF manager. 
All the vehicles (i.e. Kuali Fund S.C.A. SICAR – EuSEF), GP (i.e. Kuali Fund Management Sarl) and 
manager (i.e. GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC S.A) will be the Executing Entities because of the role that 
each of these entities plays in the channelling of GCF proceeds and the making of investments. 
COFIDES will manage EUR 0.4 million from GCF. Therefore, COFIDES will be also an Executing 
Entity for such activities. 
A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

=== PAGE 8 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4 
 
Kuali Fund aims to contribute to the transition to low-carbon and resilient economies in selected countries in 
Latin American and the Caribbean (LAC) countries by fostering the systemic adoption of climate best practices 
by small financial institutions, including small banks (up to Tier 2), Microfinance Institutions (MFIs), 
cooperatives, other non-bank financial institutions and FinTech companies (denominated all as Financial 
Service Providers, or FSP). Kuali Fund will transform FSPs in their transition to become greener and more 
inclusive, so that they ultimately end up providing green and climate products 4 to Small and Medium 
Enterprises (SMEs) and to smallholder farmers (or smallholders). Kuali will also support companies with 
innovative climate solutions5 (denominated as innovative companies) to expand the number of available 
mitigation and adaptation solutions that could eventually be financed by FSP.  
Kuali Fund will invest at least 70% of its capital in 14 countries in Latin America and the Caribbean, as 
the region is particularly vulnerable to the negative effects of climate change. Some 14 million Latin Americans 
live in areas considered to be at high risk from climate change. Up to a maximum of 30% will be invested in 
India to share best practices in climate finance between India and Latin America on how to use technology 
to be more efficient and amplify impact. 
Kuali Fund will therefore be invested in 15 countries. However, as some of these countries have not issued 
the No Objection Letter, Kuali Fund will be divided into: i) the Funded Activity (hereinafter referred to as the 
Funded Activity or Kuali Fund-GCF) will only invest in the following seven countries: Mexico, Brazil, Colombia, 
El Salvador, Panama, Honduras and Guatemala; and ii) the Kuali Fund-nonGCF, which will invest in 15 
countries (India and 14 countries in Latin America and the Caribbean). This Funding Proposal has been 
designed according to the universe of seven countries, and even though Kuali Fund will cover a wider area, 
the figures and impacts for the Funded Activity (or Kuali Fund-GCF) are differentiated in this FP from the Kuali 
Fund as a whole. 
Kuali Fund-GCF is expected to directly support over 237,907 smallholders and 4,428 SMEs that will 
collectively avoid 2,707,455 expected tons of CO2 equivalent in 10 years expected lifetime6. 
According to the IFC7, the climate finance gap in developing countries is USD 23 trillion by 2030. This gap 
implies an increase in climate finance from USD 22 trillion to USD 44.5 trillion over this period. If this target is 
met, climate finance from financial institutions will account for 30% of total lending from financial institutions 
by 2030. So far, Financial Institutions’ (FI) lending has traditionally been channeled by large banks to 
finance large renewable energy projects, large industrial power plants and large efficient building 
construction companies.  
However, the smaller financial institutions targeted by Kuali Fund (FSPs) are key to mobilising climate 
finance in markets underserved by FIs (mainly referred to large banks). These small institutions are 
essential to channel climate finance to the two key segments that are the backbone of these recipient 
economies and that larger banks typically do not serve because of their perceived risk: (i) the smallholder 
farmers segment, which is one of the most vulnerable populations to climate change and will need to adapt 
rapidly to new climate conditions by adopting climate solutions, including ecosystem-based adaptation; and 
(ii) the SME segment, which accounts for 60%-70% of the industrial pollution and a significant share of global 
carbon emissions8. 
Kuali Fund will also work with the innovative companies - to increase the number of available adaptation and 
mitigation solutions (mitigation solutions for SMEs and adaptation solutions for farmers) - and Financial 
Service Providers that are seeking to implement more sustainable environmental practices. The FSPs will 
support the SME and smallholder farmer sector and promote the creation of partnerships and synergies 
between traditional FSPs and Fintech companies through open banking cooperation. The impact of the 
Funded Activity’s investments will be maximised with the support of a Technical Assistance Facility, which 
will assist the investees, inter alia, in implementing a green strategy, integrating green risks and opportunities 
into their systems, and launching and expanding green and climate-friendly products and services. 
Regarding the investments to be financed by Kuali Fund-GCF, from an SME perspective, the Funded Activity 
will finance assets or activities such as solar roof panels, high efficiency heat pumps, insulation, low emission 
transport, energy efficient machinery, methane digesters, among others; and the smallholder farmers may be

=== PAGE 9 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5 
 
supported in activities such as tree planting, composting, restoration of abandoned farmlands, on-farm 
irrigation systems, regenerative annual crops, silvopastoral systems or tree intercropping. In terms of 
innovative companies, Kuali Fund-GCF will invest in companies with innovative solutions for mitigation (such 
as electric transport, rural solar energy providers, bio-digester manufacturing companies, EV charging 
network) and in adaptation (farm-to-business mobility supply chain, smart farming, farming as a service 
platform or vertical farming). 
 
Kuali Fund’s structure includes the Kuali SICAR-EuSEF (the SICAR 9) and a Special Purpose Vehicle 
incorporated as a Securitisation Vehicle (SV) and is divided into two funding divisions: Kuali Fund-GCF and 
Kuali Fund-nonGCF10. The size of Kuali Fund-GCF for investments in NOL-countries is expected to reach 
EUR 119.5 million in total commitments to be used for investments and EUR 5.91 million for Technical 
Assistance. An EUR 0.4 million is requested from the GCF for Communication, Audit and Evaluation purposes 
and an additional EUR 2.4 million from the GCF for Monitoring and Evaluative Data Collection.  
 
4 Loan, participation in equity, insurance or any type of financial product denominated green because it will finance the 
acquisition of any SME or smallholder farmers’ asset that have mitigation impact (any improvement in processes that avoid 
CO2 tons, the acquisition of new equipment that reduce the carbon footprint, low emission transport, appliances, power 
generation, efficient vehicles, etc.) and/or adaptation impact (innovative seeds with better adaptation to new climate patterns, 
risk systems, green agriculture solutions, etc.). 
5 Companies that already offer products/services related with mitigation and adaptation, mainly “Agri-tech” and “Climate-tech” 
companies.

=== PAGE 10 ===
A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6 
 
Kuali Fund-GCF is expected to catalyse private and institutional investments at scale through a blended 
finance structure. The EU and GCF contributions to the first loss tranche of Kuali Fund-GCF11 (EU: EUR 1.26 
million and GCF: EUR 25 million) will be key to mobilising private investor participation by reducing their 
exposure to the perceived risk of the SME and smallholder farmer’s sector. The GCF, EU, FONPRODE and 
Kuali Fund itself will also contribute to the financing of the Technical Assistance (EUR 5.0 million from GCF; 
EUR 0.57 million from EU; EUR 0.38 million from FONPRODE and EUR 0.04 from Kuali Fund). 
As Kuali Fund-GCF will invest in 7 countries, 100% of the GCF resources will be allocated to NOL countries, 
and a 15% of the sum of both the EU first loss contribution and from the EU, FONPRODE and Kuali Fund 
contributions in TA resources will be allocated to NOL countries, leaving 85% of its resources to cover the 
non-NOL countries of Kuali Fund-nonGCF. 
The Securitisation Vehicle will issue notes that will be subscribed by the Spanish Agency for International 
Cooperation and Development 12 (AECID) through the FONPRODE 13 Sovereign Fund managed by the 
Spanish Ministry of Foreign Affairs, European Union and Cooperation for up to EUR 19.4 million for Kuali 
Fund-GCF, with a maturity corresponding to the duration of the Funded Activity (10-12 years). These notes 
will enhance the return to private investors.  
The remaining funding is expected to come from the private sector (EUR 72.8 million) and a potential 
contribution from GAWA Capital (EUR 1 million). 
The Subsidiary Agreements that COFIDES -as AE- will prepare for the implementation of this proposal are: i) 
for the equity component, the Private Placement Memorandum (PPM), the Commitment Agreement, and the 
Side Letter; ii) for the TA component, the Fiduciary Agreement. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will use part of these resources to transform both innovative companies and FSPs to 
implement climate finance strategies to help the SME and the agricultural sectors to reduce CO2 emissions 
and adapt to the impacts of climate change. The Funded Activity will select the instrument that (i) best meets 
the financing needs of the investee, (ii) where possible, provides the targeted risk-adjusted return and (iii) for 
equity investments, has a clear liquidity event/exit strategy. The following instruments are expected to be 
used: 
A. Debt-like investments: Approximately 60-70% of the portfolio, mainly invested in FSPs (including 
Fintech companies), but could also be invested in innovative companies. 
B. Equity: Approximately 30-40% of the portfolio for investment in innovative companies: 
i. Mezzanine investments, such as royalty financing/profit participation loans. 
ii. Growth-capital equity investments with a significant minority stake (less than 20%), a 
board representation and a clear and/or pre-negotiated exit. 
The investment horizon of the Funded Activity is 5 years, and most investments are expected to be made in 
the first 4 years, implying 3 to 6 investments per year. 
Ultimately, Kuali Fund-GCF must have the capacity not only to invest in innovative companies and FSPs, but 
also to transform them, in order to establish more sustainable business practices, while helping to reduce the 
current financing gap for SMEs and smallholders and the perceived risk of providing financial services to 
these final beneficiaries. Kuali Fund-GCF will transform financial institutions to adopt and implement

green strategies, following a progression that will be measured with the Green Index 3.014. This Green 
Index analyses the institutions according to their level of green strategy and their capacity to manage 
environmental risk and transform their model into a greener one. In this context, Kuali will invest 
approximately 40% in Green Investments and 60% in Super Green Investments. 
* Green Investments are those institutions that belong to Phases 1-3 according to the Green Index 3.0: 
• 
Phase 1: FSP has a green strategy and systems in place to implement it, including roles and 
responsibilities and how the institution monitors and reports on the implementation. 
• 
Phase 2: FSP identifies the vulnerability of its clients/portfolio and the institution itself, the negative 
environmental impacts generated on ecosystems caused by clients and the institution, and clients’ 
need and demand of clients for green and climate practices and technologies, as well as the 
opportunities for the institution itself. 
• 
Phase 3: The FSP has processes and tools in place to analyse and respond to the identified 
vulnerabilities, negative environmental impacts, and client needs and demands. The institutions 
translate the vulnerabilities, negative environmental impacts, demands and needs into actual risks and 
opportunities. 
These investments are financed at market rates. 
* Super Green Investments are investments in institutions that belong to Phase 4 according to the Green 
Index 3.0: FSPs offering financial and non-financial green products and services and companies with 
innovative climate solutions. In the case of innovative companies, which are all Super Green Investments, 
Kuali will use its investment and technical assistance to expand their operations, establish alliances with FSPs 
for the distribution of their products, strengthen their business model and social performance systems and 
increase their investor readiness to attract new investors in the subsequent fundraising rounds. These funds 
will aim to increase the FSP green and climate portfolio of FSPs at below market rates. 
 
Kuali Fund-GCF will also be a key instrument in the context of the COVID-19 pandemic and its subsequent 
economic crisis. The Funded Activity will contribute to securing financial support for innovative companies 
and FSPs, especially the latter focusing on SMEs and the smallholder farmers segment, which are particularly 
affected by the pandemic. Kuali Fund will provide FSPs (and eventually innovative companies too) with the 
necessary financing, which is essential to alleviate the current post-COVID liquidity crisis. This will in turn help 
FSPs to secure renewals of current funding or access to new funding.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION 
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
Main impacts of climate change  
The countries of Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) - especially in Central America and the Caribbean - are 
already severely affected by climate variability, higher temperatures, deforestation, sea-level rise, ocean 
acidification and the increased intensity and frequency of climate-related disasters, which particularly affect 
indigenous people15. 
Coastal areas, where most people of the Caribbean region live, are experiencing a dramatic increase in category-
5 hurricanes due to the rise in sea surface temperatures. More than 80 million people in LAC have inadequate 
access to clean water and basic sanitation, and some 14 million people in the region live in areas at high risk 
from climate change. Transitioning to a greener and climate-resilient economy is therefore crucial to ensure that 
LAC can reduce its CO2 emissions, better protect itself against climate risks and thrive in the long term.   
LAC accounts for less than 10% of total global greenhouse gas emissions but is particularly vulnerable to the 
adverse impacts of climate change due to direct exposure to climate risks, as well as a high sensitivity to such 
risks and relatively low adaptive capacity. Projections show an increase in mean temperatures of up to 4.5ºC by 
the end of this century compared to pre-industrial levels across LAC. Associated physical impacts include 
changes in precipitation regimes, strong increases in heat extremes, higher risks of drought and increased 
aridity.  
In addition, the average intensity of tropical cyclones and the frequency of the most intense storms are projected 
to increase while sea levels are expected to rise by *0.2–1.1 mm depending on the level of warming and the 
region. Global warming not only affects the right to health, food, water, culture, development and adequate 
housing, but also threatens people’s very survival and their right to life and physical integrity, particularly in the 
region. 
According to the United Nations16, temperatures will rise by at least 1.5°C by 2030 and 100 million people will 
be pushed into extreme poverty by climate change. Partner countries are responsible for 63% of current carbon 
emissions due to strong economic growth, increased access to energy and rapidly growing populations. Partner 
countries therefore have a key role to play in enabling the necessary transition to a low-carbon economy needed. 
In particular, SMEs make a significant contribution to growth, employment and innovation in emerging 
economies, but they also drive energy demand and CO2 emissions growth17. 
Climate change affects every country on every continent, but it does not do it equally. People already living in 
poverty are often the harshest hit (75% of poor people depend on agriculture and natural resources for their 
livelihoods, making them the most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change), while having the least capacity 
to cope with them. It is estimated that climate change will force 25 million people to migrate and will cause 
250,000 deaths per year between 2030 and 2050. From an economic perspective, 78% of SMEs expect climate 
change risks to have a significant impact on their business. The world’s poorest communities often live on the 
most fragile land, and they are often politically, socially, and economically marginalised, making them particularly 
vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. More frequent and intense storms, floods, droughts, and changes 
in rainfall patterns are already affecting these communities, making it harder for them to secure a decent 
livelihood.

=== PAGE 13 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 9 
 
 
 
 
 
18 Michelle Bachelet. 
As the UN High Commissioner for Human Rights18 says: “There must be action to ensure that all affected people 
can participate in designing and implementing climate solutions, and this includes specific assistance and 
protection for environmental human rights defenders”. 
Climate context of each target country 
A summary of the key features of the climate context of each target country is highlighted below. Please also 
refer to the Annex 2 for further description of this context, which includes the NDCs and national climate 
plans/targets of each country and how Kuali Fund-GCF is addressing their key objectives and the Annex 22, 
which includes the calculations and methodology for each target country: 
BRAZIL:   
 
The impacts of climate change in Brazil, which is home to nearly 60% of the Amazon, vary widely: higher 
temperatures can alter the range and distribution of temperature-sensitive species; increased severity of 
droughts can severely affect the Amazon’s freshwater ecosystems and the people who depend on them; 
changes in precipitation and temperature can affect the spread of disease; and sea level rise and storm 
surges will have significant impacts on the lowlands of the Amazon delta. Combined with deforestation 
and environmental degradation, these impacts pose a major threat to the Amazon’s natural resources 
and to tourism in the region. Climate variability and change also threaten Brazilian agriculture. If climate 
impacts reduce agricultural production, Brazil’s food insecurity could increase, as could the pressure of 
farmers on the Amazon’s land and resources. 
 
In recent years, the Brazilian government has implemented numerous policies to reduce deforestation 
rates, which fell to one-sixth of 2004 levels by 2014. However, the Amazon remains under threat of 
deforestation, with the rate of deforestation increasing by 29 per cent in 2016 compared to 2015. In 2008, 
Brazil adopted the National Climate Change Plan, which aims to address (1) reduction of greenhouse 
gas emissions; (2) vulnerability, impacts and adaptation; (3) research and development; and (4) capacity 
building and information dissemination. In 2016, Brazil prepared its third National Communication to the 
U.N Framework Convention on Climate Change, noting the progress the country has made and 
reaffirming its active participation in and support for the Convention. 
 
Brazil has set a relative target of a 37% reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2025 compared to 
2005 levels. In addition, the country estimates that it would achieve a 43% reduction in emissions by 
2030 compared to 2005 levels but notes that this is only a benchmark and should not be considered as 
part of its official contribution. 
COLOMBIA:   
 
Most Colombians live in the high Andes, where water scarcity and land degradation exist, and in the 
coastal areas, where sea level rise and flooding may affect human settlements and economic activities. 
Rising temperature and changes in rainfall patterns are likely to lead to water scarcity in Colombia. This 
could affect irrigated agriculture, human health, and sectors such as hydropower, which rely on a steady 
supply of water. Sea-level rise is likely to have localized effects, with significant impacts in tourism-
dependent cities such as Cartagena and may lead to the loss of key ecosystems such as coral reefs and 
fisheries, affecting important livelihoods. 
 
The Government of Colombia’s 2010-2014 National Development Plan identified climate adaptation as 
a priority and established a National Climate Change System to improve coordination between 
institutions. Since 2014, a National Climate Change Policy has focused on mitigation and adaptation 
actions by increasing resilience and achieving low-carbon development. 
 
Colombia has a relative reduction target of 20% of projected emissions in 2030 under a BAU scenario. 
An additional 10% reduction would be conditional on access to international financing.

B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 10 
 
 
 
 
MEXICO:   
 
Mexico’s location between two oceans and its complex topography increases the country’s exposure to 
extreme hydrometeorological events such as tropical cyclones, frosts, heat waves and floods. This 
geographical vulnerability is compounded by a significant wealth gap (53.2% of the population lives below 
the national poverty line) and urbanization. Aging transport, energy and water infrastructure is vulnerable 
to damage from floods and strong winds, especially in coastal areas. Coastal tourism, an important 
economic sector for Mexico, is also at risk, as is the destruction of diverse marine ecosystems. In rural 
areas, extreme temperatures and erratic rainfall are drastically affecting agricultural productivity, 
including both crops and livestock. Since 1990, agriculture has accounted for 80 per cent of the country’s 
weather-related financial losses. 
 
Mexico’s 2012 General Climate Change Law (GLCC) defines planning and policy instruments, including 
institutional arrangements and provides general guidelines for the implementation of national climate 
policy. Under the GLCC, the Mexican government, is mandated to formulate and guide national climate 
change policy. 
 
Mexico has a relative reduction target of 22% of projected emissions by 2030 in a BAU scenario. An 
additional 14% reduction would be conditional on access to international finance. In addition, the country 
has an unconditional black carbon reduction target of 30%, which could rise to 70% by 2030 conditional 
on external financing. 
PANAMA:   
 
Widespread poverty and inequality have a negative impact on the environment. Panama is considered 
to be one of the most biologically diverse countries in the world, with more than 12% of its land mass is 
protected. Nevertheless, poverty pressures have led many to exploit the natural resources of the 
Mesoamerican Biological Corridor in harmful ways. Deforestation is also a growing concern, as forests 
cover 40% of Panama’s territory. 
 
Soil erosion and deforestation are among Panama's most serious environmental problems. Soil erosion 
occurs at a rate of 2,000 tons per year. Between 1990 and 1995, the average annual rate of deforestation 
was 2.15%. Air pollution is also a problem in urban centres due to emissions from industry and transport. 
Only 79% of the rural population has access to clean drinking water. Pesticides, sewage, and pollution 
from the oil industry cause much of the pollution. The country's fish stocks are threatened by water 
pollution.  
 
Panama has sectoral mitigation goals. In the case of energy, 30% of the installed capacity of the electricity 
matrix should come from other types of renewable energy sources by 2050; and in the case of the 
USCUSS (Land Use, Land Use Change and Forestry Sector), the targets are reforestation of degraded 
areas, the unilateral contribution, increasing carbon sequestration capacity by 10% compared to the 
reference scenario for 2050. 
EL SALVADOR: 
 
El Salvador, along with Honduras and Guatemala, is one of the world’s most vulnerable countries to 
climate change. The reason for this problem is that they are all in the Dry corridor, a phenomenon that 
causes drought in an already dry area, which until now has had two very specific rainy seasons (in June 
and September) that have been disappearing for some decades ago due to climate change. The latest 
projections show that, between 2021 and 2050, annual rainfall will be reduced by between 10% and 20% 
and will become much more erratic, making it much more difficult to maintain their way of live, particularly 
for smallholder farmers and the local population dependent on rural areas. 
 
El Salvador commits itself to reducing the emissions by 819,000 equivalent tons of CO2 per year. The 
NDC document does not indicate what percentage of its total emissions this represents. El Salvador has 
set a renewable energy production target of around 86% of its electricity generation by 2030, an increase 
of 50% compared to the installed capacity in 2019.

19 Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Special Report: Warming of 1.5 degrees. 
20 Global Landscape of Climate Finance 2019, Climate Policy Initiative 
GUATEMALA: 
 
Guatemala is a multiethnic, pluricultural and highly biologically diverse country, but also one of the most 
vulnerable countries to climate change in the world. Like Ecuador and many other countries in the Central 
American Dry Corridor, it is regularly affected by El Niño events.  
 
Under these circumstances, Guatemala has published its Nationally Determined Contributions.  
Guatemala intends to commit to reducing GHG emissions by 22,6% below 2016 levels in 2030 under a 
BAU scenario, if the country receives international financial and technical assistance. Without this 
external support, the commitment is reduced to 11,2%. The 4 main axes on which Guatemala focuses 
through in the document are:  i) Land use change and forestry, ii) Energy, iii) Agriculture, iv) Residues. 
 
Guatemala has also prepared the National Action Plan of Climate Change. It is presented as a summary 
of the priority actions and precautions that citizens and institutions should take into account to face the 
risk inherent in climate change, using the IPCC report as a reference. It focuses on identifying 
opportunities for sustainable development to protect vulnerable people. It is in line with the objectives 
captured under the “Ley Marco para Regular la Reducción de la Vulnerabilidad, la Adaptación Obligatoria 
ante los Efectos del Cambio Climático y la Mitigación de Efecto Invernadero” (LMCC) a national law 
primarily aimed at combating climate change. 
HONDURAS: 
 
Located at the heart of the Central American isthmus, Honduras, like its neighbours, is constantly 
exposed to extreme meteorological events. In recent years, these events have become more frequent 
and damaging due to climate change. From droughts to floods and erratic climate. The poverty rate in 
the country is 48,3%. This is exacerbated in the rural areas, which are also among the most vulnerable 
sectors to climate change. 
 
Energy and the agriculture are two main sectors in which the country intends to reduce its emissions. 
The country has committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions by 16% by 2030, and almost 90% 
of the reduction will come from these two sectors. 
 
The Honduran government has also published a NAMA for a low-carbon livestock sector in 2018, in line 
with its national 2013 Climate Change Law, which demonstrates the importance of the rural sector in 
Honduras and the need for a rapid transition to a path of sustainability in order to stabilise the livelihoods 
of thousands of families in a sector that is facing more difficulties every year due to the erratic behaviour 
of the climate in the region. 
Limiting warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels19 will require changes in our economic system, including 
the way the energy is consumed, the way industries operate, and the way food is produced. The investment 
required to achieve this goal is huge, ranging from USD 1.6 trillion to USD 3.8 trillion per year between 2016 and 
2050. Current annual climate finance represents only one-third of the funding needed20. Financial institutions 
have a key role to play in mobilising the remaining two-thirds of the funding needed to close the climate finance 
gap. 
The IFC has estimated the climate finance gap in developing countries for this decade (to 2030) at USD 23 
trillion. If this target is met, climate finance will account for 30% of total lending by financial institutions by 2030. 
Lending by Financial Institutions has so far been channelled mainly by large banks to finance large renewable 
energy projects, large industrial power plants and large efficient building construction companies. However, 
smaller financial institutions are key to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large banks. 
These institutions are essential to channel climate finance to the two key segments that larger banks typically 
do not serve because of their perceived risk:

 
The SME segment accounts for 60%-70% of environmental impacts and a significant share of global 
carbon emissions21. This segment represents a large opportunity for mitigation as SMEs typically use 
energy-inefficient equipment and vehicles, and unreliable sources of electricity, which could be improved 
by solar solutions. If properly capitalised and trained, Financial Institutions could finance equipment to 
increase energy efficiency and reduce carbon emissions. 
 
Smallholder farmers, who are among the most vulnerable populations to climate change, need to adapt 
quickly to new climatic conditions by adopting climate-smart agriculture practices and equipment. It is 
estimated that 95% out of the 15 million smallholder farmers in LAC are already experiencing the negative 
impacts of rising temperatures, unpredictable rainfall and extreme weather events on crop yields, pest 
and disease outbreaks, declining incomes, and food security22. Smallholders can also reduce their carbon 
footprint by using new equipment such as biodigesters and reforesting degraded land. FSPs can play a 
key role in financing climate-smart agricultural equipment to enhance adaptation and promote mitigation. 
 
Climate adaptation for smallholder farmers 
The smallholder finance gap in the 
region is USD 20 billion 23  . This 
financing gap is explained by the

the right business model in terms

FIs, as the growth of their operations needs to come from their expansion into rural areas, as the penetration of

The potential of smallholder agriculture on climate change mitigation is important, but pales in comparison to the 
adaptation and resilience needs and impacts on the sector.  
Five out six farms in the world are less than two hectares in size, occupy around 12 per cent of the world’s 
agricultural land and produce about 35 per cent of the world’s food24, but the contribution of smallholder farmers 
to the food supply varies widely from country to countries. Farmers in developing countries are the most climate-
vulnerable population on the planet. Their livelihoods are entirely dependent on the environment and are 
extremely vulnerable to even small changes in temperature or rainfall. As a result, climate change threatens to 
make smallholders the most climate vulnerable population on the planet.  
The main practical risks faced by smallholder farmers are: 
- 
Drought: A temporary anomaly in which the availability of water is below the availability falls below the 
needs of a particular geographical area. There is not enough water to meet the needs of plants, animals 
and people. It is mainly caused by a lack of rainfall or precipitation. 
- 
Decrease in productivity: Decrease in the volume of the final product, whether agricultural or livestock, 
caused by sub-optimal production conditions.

=== PAGE 17 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 13 
 
 
 
 
 
25 OCDE Índice de Políticas PYME: América Latina y el Caribe 2019 
- 
Loss of production resources: Total or partial damage to the ecosystem in which production activities, 
whether agricultural or livestock, take place. 
- 
Increase in production costs:  The need to cover higher costs of purchasing inputs for agricultural and 
livestock activities. 
- 
Increase or occurrence of pests: Increase or occurrence of diseases in crops/animals of diseases in 
plants/animals, caused by external agents. Change in the phenological cycle of plants and/or the life 
cycle of animals. 
- 
Landslides: landslides or movement of land masses, caused by the instability of a slope. 
- 
Flooding: Occupation by water of areas that are normally free of water, due to overflowing of rivers, 
torrential rain, thaw, tidal waves above normal levels, avalanches, etc. 
- 
Fires: A large scale fire that burns or provokes and destroys vegetation or crops that are not intended to 
burn. 
- 
Erosion: Wear and tear and modelling of the earth's crust caused by the action of wind, rain, fluvial, 
marine and glacial processes, and by the action of living things. 
- 
Decreased availability (scarcity) of water: A decreases in the amount of water available, either due to 
less water being absorbed by the soil or a decrease in rainfall. 
- 
Reduced food security: The availability of food, people's access to food and the biological use of food. 
Building climate resilience for these farmers requires a massive improvement in agricultural value chains by 
adopting climate-smart practices, crops and assets. The improved value chains will expand smallholder access 
to quality assets, inputs, training, processing, and market opportunities for high-value and climate-resilient crops. 
These crops and practices will restore nutrients into depleted soils and enable farmers to achieve sustainable 
yields and profits, bolstering their resilience to climate change. Further, new trees planted, and organic matter 
generated will sequester CO2, helping to mitigate climate change. 
Early adoption of low-emissions agriculture in developing countries is an opportunity for more efficient use of 
land, fertilizer, energy, feed and water. However, substantial funding may be needed to adopt new technologies, 
given diverse and dispersed smallholders and costs of monitoring. 
Climate finance for SMEs 
SMEs facilitate economic inclusion and livelihood benefits for the world’s marginalised by offering employment 
as well as products and services to rural and urban communities, thereby contributing to socially inclusive, 
sustainable development. 
MSMEs account for about 99% of all enterprises and employ about 67% of all workers in Latin America25.  
However, due to a particular productivity gap, they account for only a quarter of the region’s total output value. 
MSMEs are distributed along all sector of the economy so precise emissions levels are difficult to stablish but 
are certainly in line with their importance in the economy. 
SMEs face several specific challenges regarding CO2 emissions and climate change like their difficulty to 
measure and manage the emissions they produce and increasing the efficiency of the resources they use. 
Left unchecked, climate change represents material risk to business cash flow and economic growth, by 
increasing operating costs, limiting resource availability, causing shifts in demand, disrupting supply chains, 
which in turn introduces new challenges to a fragile and vulnerable enterprise system. 
It is in the SMEs self-interest in finding ways to both mitigate emissions and to increase resilience – helps future 
proof business plans, meet the climate expectations of customers, retain market access and enhance their 
competitive edge and thereby make it a profitable business for them.

➢ Kuali Fund-GCF will work with smaller Financial Service Providers (FSPs) such as microfinance 
institutions, cooperatives, non-bank financial institutions, Tier 2 banks and Fintech companies aiming at 
deepening green and climate financial inclusion to SMEs and underserved farmers. 
Besides, on the technology market available for climate finance, there are innovative climate companies that 
already use technology solutions to prevent climate change, by offering climate mitigation and resilience 
solutions to reduce GHG emissions or addressing the impacts of global warming. These innovative companies 
that employ technology to get positive environmental effects on mitigation and adaptation to climate change 
include, among others, agri-tech companies, climate-tech companies, renewable companies, or ecosystem-
based adaptation companies. For instance, a company with operations in Mexico and Colombia, provides 
farmers with an easy-to-install-and-use prefabricated modular biodigester package that includes a full suite of 
biogas appliances and connections. The system takes organic waste and transforms it to renewable biogas and 
a powerful organic fertilizer that is beneficial to the soil and increases farmer's productivity. The biogas can be 
used as mechanical energy for farm productive activities or as a heating system for the farmer’s home. 
To meet the climate change mitigation goals of most emerging markets, there is an urgent need to finance 
innovative companies that use technology for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

PROGRAMME STRUCTURE: COMPONENTS AND ACTIVITIES 
The emissions reductions that need to occur before 2030 can largely be achieved in the 2020s by existing 
technologies such as solar, while the post-2030 emission reductions depend on innovative technologies. A report 
from the World Economic Forum26 notes that to successfully expand and deploy these climate solutions based 
on technologies in the 2030s, emerging climate technologies need to be validated at commercial scale in the 
2020s. According to a report from Boston Consulting Group27, it is expected that these innovative companies will 
require an estimated USD 100 trillion to USD 150 trillion over the next 30 years—equivalent to roughly USD 3 
trillion to USD 5 trillion annually- to fund their development and expansion. 
➢ Therefore, Kuali Fund-GCF’s investments in these innovative companies will be essential to meet the 
climate change mitigation goals in emerging economies. Additionally, In the context of COVID-19 
pandemic and its subsequent economic crisis, many of these innovative companies have suffered lack 
of funding putting at risk their operations. The Funded Activity investments will be key to secure their 
needed financial stability and to increase their investment readiness so that they can attract further 
funding.  
➢ Finally, Kuali Fund-GCF will foster alliances between FSPs and the innovative companies so that FSPs 
can finance the acquisition of the solution that the company implements. To achieve successful alliances, 
the Funded Activity will leverage its knowledge of FSPs to work with them and with the innovative 
company to understand their incentives, manage their expectations and reach a partnership which is 
beneficial for both. 
Climate Finance – best practices 
Countries, companies and especially the finance industry are under pressure to adopt climate finance best 
practices, driven both by regulation and by market dynamics, in order to reach the international commitment to 
greenhouse gases reduction. As financiers, FIs have the potential to substantially move the scale toward 
mitigation and adaptation to climate change.

=== PAGE 20 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 16 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
28 “Green Finance Latin America Survey” IFC, 2018. 
There are four main areas via which FIs can engage in more sustainable practices, as identified in IFC’s28 2018 
survey: 
 
Green strategic commitment: engaging with top management to implement green finance practices, 
cross-cutting functions, and processes. It implies making green finance a central pillar of core strategic 
decisions. In our methodology this corresponds to Green Index Phase 1. 
 
Environmental Risk Management system: adapting current risk management system to include 
environmental considerations. In our methodology this corresponds to Green Index Phases 2 and 3 
 
Green products and services: building a diversified set of products which incorporate environmental 
benefits (loans, deposits, insurance). In our methodology this corresponds to Phase 4. 
 
Eco-efficiency practices: reduce Fis’ and clients’ environmental footprint. In our methodology this 
corresponds to Green Index Phase 4. 
IFC’s survey highlighted that banks in LAC are taking steps to adopt more sustainable banking models, but still 
need support in the four areas highlighted above. Many banks mention that “sustainable finance models need 
access to new sources of financing, in particular, for growing responsible investors that exercise a strong demand 
for assets classified as green. In this context, institutional investors are catalysts for change, promoting green 
banking and actively influencing the decisions and strategies adopted by companies and banks on climate 
change”. Smaller financial institutions such as small banks, MFIs, cooperatives, other non-bank financial 
institutions and Fintechs (denominated all as FSPs) are key to serve SMEs and smallholder farmers, but they 
are lagging in the adoption of these sustainable practices. Capital offered by the Funded Activity is therefore 
essential to this transformation.  
Another key constraint the financial sector faces to serve the SMEs and smallholder farmers segments is 
performing an efficient detailed credit analysis of these final beneficiaries. Fintech sector is developing very 
interesting solutions to increase efficiency to serve this segment leveraging data and technology. For this reason, 
Fintech sector will be essential in Kuali Fund-GCF investments, by supporting green investments to SMEs and 
smallholder farmers and by supporting collaboration between traditional FSPs (which typically have larger 
balance sheets) and Fintech companies. Fintech represents not only an opportunity to deliver green financing 
to SMEs or farmers, but also an innovative channel to serve SMEs and smallholder farmers more efficiently and 
in underserved areas where only technology can reach them.

PROBLEM STATEMENT 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s target countries face serious difficulties in achieving greenhouse gas reduction goals in 
countries with permanent growing demographic expectations. The financial sector worldwide is under 
pressure to embrace climate finance best practices. Likewise, the reduction of activity due to the effects of 
the COVID crisis and its consequent lack of liquidity in the markets has affected economic growth. This lack 
of market liquidity is even more severe with regard to SMEs (USD 900 billion gap) and smallholders (USD 20 
billion gap) due to severe information disparities, large number of informal business practices, high costs to 
access the market in relation to available margins, heterogeneity of the market, impediments to SME and 
smallholder farmers growth and productivity and regulatory constraints. 
The gap between the financial needs and the supply of financial services is anticipated to remain significant 
in targeted countries, where, according to Global Findex29 in 2021, 54% of adults in average had an account 
in a financial institution or mobile money provider (98%-100% in high-income economies). Account ownership 
decreases to 45% and 50% for poor and women adults, respectively.  
Financial access is more challenging for women. In LAC countries, women are 7 percentage points less likely 
than men to have an account. A financing gap worth USD 5 billion has been identified for women-led micro-
business alone and an additional shortfall of USD 93 billion for women-led SMEs30. Female labor force 
participation is lower than men and many women are stuck in the informal sector, lacking access to financial, 
human, and social capital which are key constraints for the growth of their business. These limitations are 
exacerbated by weaker levels of financial literacy, a shortage of available financing options and the issue of 
accessing this finance without legal ownership of assets and guarantees.  Participants in the financial system 
need also to be aware of and understand how gender norms limit women’s access and use of financial 
services31. 
Finally, despite their socioeconomic importance, smallholder farmers, the hardest hit by climate change, are 
the most underserved in financial services.  Women in agriculture have less access to key assets, fewer land 
rights and carry out a greater amount of unpaid work within the family and the community. On top of that, 
women are more vulnerable to the effects of climate change. 
The vulnerability to environmental risks and climate change of SMEs, smallholder farmers, women and poor 
households in general is linked to their reliance on expensive, unreliable, polluting, and potentially harmful 
energy sources for their daily needs and productive activities. Farmers are limited in their capacity to invest 
in climate smart technologies and practices to reduce emissions, gain resilience and increase their yields. 
SHIFT IN THE DEVELOPMENT PATHWAY 
Financial inclusion and environmental protection are critical issues for people living in poverty and key levers 
to achieve inclusive and sustainable economic growth. Financial services are central to the lives of everyone, 
allowing people to participate in the economy, access services, seize green opportunities and build resilience. 
When people participate in the financial system, they are more capable to start and expand businesses, 
invest in mitigation and adaptation, education and manage risks. When the financial sector manages 
environmental risks and fosters green and climate products, it can improve clients’ resilience to climate 
change.

=== PAGE 23 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 19 
 
 
 
 
There are Financial Service Providers (FSPs) deepening financial services to SMEs and people underserved 
by traditional banks. Besides, there are technology solutions and climate smart practices available for 
implementation, offered by Innovative Companies (ICs) to prevent climate change.   
ICs together with FSPs have the opportunity to participate to offset the vulnerability of poor segments and 
support reliable climate solutions to SMEs and farmers, thanks to the provision of dedicated financing and 
technical support. The proximity of microfinance institutions to their clients and their expertise in credit 
provision could give them a comparative advantage. To perform in green/climate inclusive finance, FSPs and 
ICs need to implement appropriate environmental strategies, risk management systems and design, provide 
in turn, green and climate financial and non-financial services to SMEs and smallholder farmers. 
IF Kuali  Fund-GCF succeeds in leveraging private capital investment and establishing sustainable business 
practices in green Innovative Companies (ICs) and Financial service providers THEN due to the reduced 
financing gap and perceived risk of lending, SMEs and smallholder farmers will have access to green 
finance BECAUSE improved green financial ecosystem will allow SMEs and smallholder farmers to invest in 
mitigation and adaptation technologies, increasing their adaptability to climatic threats while lowering GHG 
emissions 
Provided that key external conditions in target countries (assumptions) are not significantly deteriorated, 
i.e., that the regulatory framework remains conducive for Green Financial Inclusion, facilitating  private sector 
participation, that the political situation does not deteriorate significantly and that there are no sudden 
situations of over-indebtedness that make it impossible to support the green transition through financial 
services; Kuali Fund and its associated TA Facility can contribute to mitigating the risks for Green Climate 
Finance and overcoming existing barriers. This is especially the case with respect to the risks related to the 
FSPs’ failure to create a short to medium term project pipeline; the low financial appetite for SMEs and 
farmers in Private Sector; the FSPs’ failure to implement the right business model generating lack of demand 
from SMEs and farmers; the Climatic hazards or the risk of an agriculture sector non conducive for Climate 
Smart Agriculture practices. At the same time, it relates to the barriers such as the business model of FSPs 
which are not ready for the new environment with potential higher credit cost, disruption of operations, etc.; 
the gap between the financial needs of smallholders and the supply of financial services; the gender barriers 
in financial inclusion (economic, eligibility and physical barriers); the limited access and use of low-carbon 
technologies and practices at an affordable cost for SMEs and smallholders to manage climate risks; the 
limited preparedness, understanding and use of climate information; the scarce quality and reliable vendors 
of low-carbon technologies; the limited regional cooperation, knowledge management and, finally, the fact 
that most promising current climate technologies are still at prototype phase. 
Once capital from private investors is raised by Kuali Fund in addition to public funding, debt and equity 
investments into FSPs and ICs will be materialized (activities) - i.e., Kuali fund will be capitalized and 
operational (output)-and SMEs and Smallholder farmers will receive, in turn, green financial services from 
FSPs (activity). 
In parallel, other activities such as the setup of the governance structure and management of the associated 
Kuali’s Technical Assistance will entail the launching of the tendering processes to contract the right service 
providers to carry out the TA projects, which will imply that the Kuali’s TA Facility will be fully operational 
(output). TA Projects will be devoted to Green Enhancement for SMEs and smallholder farmers; Green 
Readiness with FSPs; Social Performance and Environmental Risks Management Systems for FSPs and 
ICs; FSPs‘ General Business Improvement; ICs’ Business Model Improvement; Eco-systemic interventions, 
stakeholder engagement and gender actions and, finally, Knowledge Sharing activities 
On the investment side, Kuali’s financing will result (outputs) in greater availability of green financial services 
adapted to the need of SMEs smallholder farmers. Besides, thanks to the TA Projects capacities for Climate

34 International Finance Corporation. 2017. MSME Finance Gap: Assessment of the Shortfalls and Opportunities in Financing Micro, Small, and Medium 
Enterprises in Emerging Markets. 
35 SEED (2020). Translating Climate Finance into Climate Action on the Ground. Leveraging the Potential for Small and Medium-sized Enterprises (SMEs). 
Smart Agriculture and adoption of Climate Solutions will be enhanced in smallholder farmers and financial 
ecosystem actors’ capacities will be strengthened, enabling green and inclusive finance. 
As a result, it will be achieved (outcomes) the Adoption of climate solutions and improvement management 
of agricultural lands contributing to reducing GHG emissions, the Improved resilience in vulnerable people & 
communities and the Enhanced green & inclusive financial ecosystem (technology and market development, 
as well as knowledge generation). 
Greater availability of finance together with capacity building will also drive co-benefits such as Improved 
Financial Inclusion and gender outreach; Employment preservation and labor women inclusion (the latter two 
specially fueled by the gender action plan) and efficient Technology adoption by SMEs together with CSA 
practices embraced by farmers. 
BARRIERS AND RISKS 
The poor mitigation and adaptation results from the AFOLU and MSME/SME sector in the past has been 
caused by several barriers.  
A. Financial Barrier 
 A large gap exists between the actual finance provided and the need by the AFOLU sector to realize its 
mitigation potential. This gap is particularly important for small-scale agriculture, where climate finance is 
approximately USD 10 billion per year, but where financing needs are estimated at USD 240 billion per year32. 
Besides, most of the finance directed toward the sector in general comes from public sources (84% from 
multilateral and national DFIs alone)33 and it is usually channeled through large banks, public institutions and 
companies towards large agroforestry projects.  
Regarding SMEs, in the developing economies, the finance gap from formal MSMEs is valued at $5.2 trillion, 
which is equivalent to 19 percent of the gross domestic product (GDP). This in turn amounts to 1.4 times the 
current level of MSME lending in these countries34. Approximately 40 percent of all enterprises are credit 
constrained. 
A review of multilateral climate finance funds reveals that the explicit integration of SMEs within multilateral 
climate fund projects still remains marginal and that climate finance flows have only marginally enlisted local 
Fis (microfinance institutions (MFIs) and banks) to deliver capital to SMEs, thus discounting the importance 
of these financial actors for mainstreaming climate finance in the financial sector35. 
FSP are key to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large banks, companies and even public 
institutions. These institutions are essential to channel climate finance towards two key segments that larger 
banks do not usually serve because of their perceived risk (smallholders and SMEs). However, FSPs struggle 
to understand and respond to the financing needs of climate-smart SMEs with tailored financial products due 
to lack of assessment frameworks for green-climate tech & business models.  
This finance gap for SMEs hinders the capacity of the whole SME sector to realize its contributions to inclusive 
economic development and achieve climate as well as social impacts at scale.  
Kuali’s innovative blended finance structure and target investees can effectively channel climate finance and 
drive investment into sustainable AFOLU and MSMEs, mobilizing important quantity of private resources. 
Regarding women, specific barriers limit their access to finance, such as physical, economic and eligibility 
barriers and FSPs will implement activities to address them to the possible extent.

=== PAGE 25 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 
 
 
 
 
36 IEA, Net Zero by 2050, 2021 https://www.iea.org/reports/net-zero-by-2050 
37 PWC State of Climate Tech 2021 
B. Knowledge Barrier 
Financial Services Providers must play a major role in catalyzing clients’ plans to mitigate and increase their 
resilience by providing adequate finance for the required assets and investments. Additionally, FSPs will also 
need to adapt to this new environment with potential higher credit cost, disruption of operations, etc.  
However, their business model is not ready for it as FSPs will need to adapt its risk management systems 
and business model to address these new challenges (i.e., climate maps, more resilient operations, better 
diversified portfolios). 
The main barriers that FSP face are the lack of knowledge about climate change (mitigation and adaptation) 
and their potential impacts in their portfolio and the opportunities and risks that it represents. The is also a 
lack of data, tools and methodologies that can manage climate risks, in the form of management systems 
and “green and climate lending”. The regulators and the ecosystem in general are on their way to create a 
comprehensive framework but there is little external support to individual FSP. 
Kuali through its Technical Assistance Facility will strengthen their FSP investees green and climate products 
design & offering, processes & risk management, technology, and employee capabilities.  
C.  Innovation Barrier  
The so-called Climate Technologies are technologies that are explicitly focused on reducing GHG emissions 
or addressing the impacts of climate change. Climate tech applications can directly mitigate or remove 
emissions, help us adapt to the impacts of climate change and enhance our understanding of the climate. 
It is estimated that by 2050, almost half of global CO2 emissions reductions will come from technologies that 
are currently only at the demonstration or prototype phase36. Significant and sustained investment is required 
across the innovation life cycle to ensure that commercially ready solutions are able to scale within this next 
decade. Besides, commercially ready technologies also need important levels of funding to scale.  
Likewise, only 4% of the funding is directed towards companies outside of North America, Europe or China 
and is also disproportionated directed toward the Mobility and Transport sector receiving 61% of all 
investment37. Areas related to the diverse energy efficient equipment, industry or food, agriculture and land 
use are lagging behind in funding.   
 
PROGRAMME STRUCTURE: COMPONENTS AND ACTIVITIES 
Kuali Fund-GCF investment strategy aims at supporting smaller Financial Institutions in overcoming these 
barriers by offering: 
a. Capital in a form of debt to finance FSP expansion as they transition from Phase 1 of the 
Green Index to Phase 4 so that they become ready to build new green and climate portfolio or 
to promote or foster the green and climate portfolio if it already exists. In the case of innovative 
companies, finance will be used to support their expansion so that their climate solutions can 
reach new clients and markets. 
b. Technical assistance to implement projects that help FSPs to become greener and 
eventually to build state-of-the-art climate finance SME and smallholder farmers business 
model. In the case of innovative companies, technical assistance will be used to strengthen 
their social performance systems, their business model and increase their investor readiness. 
The Theory of Change of Kuali Fund includes the transformation of FSPs business models to become 
greener, scale up green and climate lending for SMEs and smallholder farmers. Kuali Fund-GCF investments 
are expected to be transformational in the following areas:

=== PAGE 26 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
 
 
 
a. Implementation of new processes & technologies: such as efficient client origination 
models, credit processes or portfolio risk management systems. Establishing risk mitigation 
systems to monitor and mitigate agriculture and SME/smallholder farmers related risks 
following the “Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosure” recommendations. Using 
of data analytics and client segmentation as tools to reduce credit costs. 
b. Training people on climate finance, including product specialists, risk officers, loan officers 
and managers so that the institution can make strategic commitments of green and climate 
lending figures and environmental mitigation goals. 
c. Implementation of Environmental Risk Mitigation Systems (both internal and external) to 
reduce the institution’s carbon footprint (reduction in waste, paper, water or energy 
consumption) and to include environmental risk as a factor of the activities financed and on 
the credit approval process.  
d. Implementation of green and climate products directed to the SME and smallholder 
farmers such as loans to finance more efficient new machinery or equipment or smart 
agriculture loans. 
After this transformation process, the ICs and FSPs invested by the Funded Activity will be greener and more 
inclusive so that they can continue with their environmental and social impact even after the investment is 
exited and possibly crowding in more Financial Institutions that have not been investees to launch new climate 
finance initiatives. Kuali Fund-GCF will support this transformation by providing capital and Technical 
Assistance to FSPs and innovative companies that will strengthen their green and climate SME and 
smallholder farmers product design & offering, processes & risk management, technology, and employee 
capabilities:   
- 
Processes: reflects the policies, tools, mechanisms, procedures and frameworks that innovative 
companies and FSPs (Fintechs included) use to identify, assess and manage environmental risks. 
Kuali expects to use part of the Technical Assistant facility to support innovative companies and FSPs 
implementing environmental risk management systems to monitor and manage their own ecological 
footprint and their clients’ portfolio, including implementing changes in the loan approval process to 
include the environmental risk level of the clients. 
 
- 
Products:  the ICs and FSPs progress towards sustainability begins in many cases with the adoption 
of eco-efficiency practices, but Kuali Fund-GCF’s purpose is to make them reach a strategic green 
commitment, including offering solutions that incorporate environmental benefits, which are 
intentionally and differentially marketed as green and climate to the FSP’s client base. Climate 
financing products include loans to finance energy efficient equipment, solar panels, hybrid vehicles 
or loans to finance other environmental-friendly activities of the SMEs and smallholder farmers such 
as   ecosystem-based adaptation solutions, recycling, waste management or climatic microinsurance 
products that contribute to help these beneficiaries become more resilient to environmental shocks 
and climate change. 
 
- 
Governance/ People: environmental lending products are potentially more complex in their appraisal 
and reporting. Staff will often require training to recognize potential customer opportunities, 
communicate benefits to customers, ensure that loan applications meet eligibility criteria and to report 
on key indicators. Support will be provided in the form of Technical Assistance that allows innovative 
companies and FSPs to mitigate technical risks through the support of external consultants or by the 
creation of in-house specialist teams. Over time, these skills can be mainstreamed into the 
organization. Technical assistance will also be used to design and launch training programs to SMEs 
and smallholder farmers about environmentally friendly practices.

=== PAGE 27 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23 
 
 
 
 
 
38 Desigualdad horizontal y discriminación étnica en cuatro países latinoamericanos, Alicia Puyana Mutis, October 2018. 
- 
Technology: recent advances in digital technologies and innovative business models represent a 
game changer that can help close the SME and smallholder farmers finance gap by enabling smaller 
firms to tap alternative sources of funding, thanks to the data generated by their digital footprint. The 
COVID-19 global pandemic has led to a devastating impact on SMEs and smallholder farmers, with 
millions of small businesses being at risk of closing permanently if the crisis persists, while others are 
suffering massive losses and laying off many of their staff. The situation is more dire in emerging 
market countries where there are limited public resources to help small businesses. The use of digital 
technologies is proving to be critical to help SMEs and smallholder farmers in crisis contexts. The 
delivery of digital financial services can be done through several players such as financial technology 
(FinTech) firms, which generally focus on a particular financial product or service and utilize new 
technologies and ways to do business to serve customers in a more efficient and transparent manner, 
or through financial institutions that adopt these new products and services or may partner with 
FinTech companies to deliver these products. These players are transforming the traditional lending 
process by automating the underwriting and loan servicing tasks making it significantly cheaper, 
faster, and easier to provide financing to SMEs and smallholder farmers. In addition, the use of 
alternative data and advanced data analytics is helping eliminate the information asymmetry and 
provide additional information to assess the credit risk of SMEs and smallholder farmers. The data 
from digital transactions can help innovative companies and FSPs better understand their customer 
needs and patterns, thereby helping create new products and services. 
These strategies aim to transform the FSPs’ operations to increase SME and smallholder farmers’ access to 
more affordable lending for climate-related loans. It benefits both levels of beneficiaries FSPs (diversification 
of their portfolio and improvement of their image and reputation) and SMEs and smallholder farmers (greater 
liquidity, more resilient, reduction of their energy costs, or increase access to global value chains). These 
mitigation and adaptation activities undertaken by Kuali Fund will result in three co-benefits: (i) Improved 
Financial Inclusion and gender outreach; (ii) Employment preservation and labor women inclusion (the latter 
two specially fueled by the gender action plan) and (iii) efficient Technology adoption by SMEs together with 
CSA practices embraced by farmers. 
The Funded Activity will provide loans, equity, and grants (through the Technical Assistance Facility) with a 
gender approach as well as increase the availability of inclusive financial products for women led SMEs and 
women farmers and will positively impact indigenous woman as the majority of indigenous communities in 
the region works as smallholder farmers38. 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s commitment in environmental and social fields is reflected in the Portfolio Impact Score 
(PIS), a tool designed by the Fund Manager that has already been successfully employed in previous funds. 
Its main purpose is measuring the socio-economic impact of each investment in order to register the specific 
impacts generated. The methodology of this tool consists of applying a score to the performance of each 
project based on social aspects -including gender- and environmental objectives previously set. The Impact 
Score will be measured at the beginning of each investment during the social and environmental due 
diligence. It will be measured and monitored on a quarterly basis. After the exit of each investment, the 
Investors Advisory Committee will select an independent social auditor that will measure the Audited Impact 
Score. A Kuali Fund-GCF’s Audited Impact Score will be obtained as the average of all the investee’s Audited 
Impact Score. This Audited Impact Score will determine half of the potential carried interest of the managers. 
The PIS supports the Impact Management process ant it is the weighted average of two main dimensions: (i) 
Outcome/Output metrics, measured at investee level, and summed to give an aggregate score at the 
Funded Activity level; and (ii) Transformative metrics, aimed at measuring to what extent the investee is

=== PAGE 28 ===

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 24 
 
 
 
 
 
39 The Green Index 3.0 was developed in order to generate a coordinated response among different stakeholders around 
inclusive finance. The main goals are enhancing cooperation, improving learning from different inclusive finance actors, and 
developing tools to standardize the inclusive finance sector to attract more international attention into the sector from both 
the public and the private side. It is used to assess many different FSPs helping them to develop better action plans regarding 
green inclusive finance. SPI4-Alinus complements the Green Index 3.0 by providing investors indicators to improve their 
Due Diligence processes with the FSPs regarding their social outcomes: https://cerise-spm.org/es/alinus/ 
40 https://www.e-mfp.eu/resources/green-index-innovative-tool-assess-green-inclusive-finance-performance-fsps  
developing its policies/ business model to better serve smallholder farmers or SME’s and these metrics are 
measured and scored at investee level and applied as a weighted average to give the Funded Activity’s score. 
Transformation metrics are made up of two set of metrics: (1) Green Index39 – which measure how well an 
FSP currently performs in inclusive green finance through the Green Index 3.0 conceptualized by the e-MFP 
Green Inclusive and Climate Smart Finance Action Group (GICSF-AG)40 and (2) Social Performance Score 
-– which measures how effectively an organization achieves its stated social and environmental goals and 
creates value for the population it aims to reach through the SPI4-Alinus tool.  
Outcome/Output metrics are made up of two set of metrics for a FSPs: (1) Outcome/Output Map – which is 
designed to measure impact, defined as the change in an outcome/output caused by Kuali activities and (2) 
Investee Customized Outcome/Output -– which is designed to measure a specific impact, defined as the 
change in an outcome/output, at the investee level that is not being capture by the Funded Activity level 
preestablished metrics.   
The score is calculated as a weighted average (by investment value) of the Transformative Metric Score and 
Outcome/Output Metric Scores for FSPs and Innovative Companies. All scores range between 0 and 1.  
The chart below summarizes the process to calculate the Portfolio Impact Score: 
 
The result will be established within a range of 0-10 and will be analyzed and justified by external auditors. 
The carried interest will be linked to this green and climate performance, so if the global portfolio score is 
positive, the Fund Manager will benefit from a better carried interest. In this way, the Manager’s interests are 
aligned with the results achieved in environmental, social and climate issues.

It should be noticed that the PIS is not used during the identification phase. This phase relates to pipeline 
construction and the preliminary investment committee. At those stages, more macro level variables are being 
taken into account (e.g. financial ratios, overall green portfolio, average loan, social mission and targets, etc.). 
Then, the preliminary IC will approve/reject the mandatory on site due diligence that precedes the final 
investment committee. During this Due Diligence, an initial PIS is constructed to serve as a baseline and also 
to evaluate in detail the social and impact performance of the investee and potential contribution to Kuali 
Fund-GCF’s targets. At this stage, the PIS is used to make an investment decision and if favorable to establish 
a base line for monitoring

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) 
Kuali Fund aims to support the transformation of the LAC (minimum of 70% of Kuali Fund resources) and 
Indian (up to 30% of the total resources) financial sector to become more inclusive and greener by providing 
climate finance for SMEs and smallholder farmers, fostering systemic adoption of climate finance best 
practices. Since the Paris Agreement, countries, companies, and the population itself have made a 
commitment to reduce greenhouse gases and to adopt best climate practices. Kuali Fund will invest in 15 
countries in LAC and India. Nevertheless, since the No Objection Letters from the whole universe of countries 
have not been reached, the resources from GCF will only be allocated in seven countries: Brazil, Mexico, 
Colombia, Panama, El Salvador, Honduras, and Guatemala. The Funded Activity (or Kuali Fund-GCF) will 
invest in those NOL countries jointly with part of the EU resources, the private resources, and the SV 
resources from Kuali Fund. Therefore, as there will be separate contributions, there will be also separate 
cash flows for the different financiers. 
The size of Kuali Fund-GCF for investments in NOL-countries41 is expected to reach EUR 119.5 million in 
total commitments to be used for investments and EUR 5.91 million in Technical Assistance. An additional 
EUR 0.4 million is requested to GCF for Communication, Audit and Evaluation purposes and EUR 2.4 million 
for Monitoring and Evaluative Data Collection. Kuali Fund-GCF is expected to catalyze private and 
institutional investment at scale through a blended finance structure. The EU and GCF contributions to the 
first-loss tranche of Kuali Fund-GCF42 (EU: EUR 1.26 million43 and GCF: EUR 25 million) will be key to 
mobilize private investors’ participation by reducing their exposure to the perceived risk of the SME and 
smallholder farmer’s sector. AECID’s contribution44 to Kuali Fund-GCF (up to EUR 17.6 million) plus EUR 1.8 
million from the SICAR itself would be channeled through a Securitization Vehicle (SV) pari passu with private 
investors.  This SV will be a separate legal entity from the Funded Activity, yet applying the same investment 
strategy, and it will be managed by the same General Partner. AECID contribution to the SV will be in Senior 
notes and the SICAR contribution to the SV will be in subordinated notes to protect the contribution from 
FONPRODE, that enhance the return of the private investors. The remaining funding is expected to come 
from GAWA Capital (1% of the Kuali Fund-GCF commitments) and from private investors (approximately 
EUR 73.8 million), which will be raised in collaboration with top private banking institutions. COFIDES will 
also invest as a private investor (approximately EUR 5 million). 
The GCF, EU, FONPRODE and Kuali Fund itself will also contribute to the funding of the Technical 
Assistance (EUR 5.0 million from GCF; EUR 0.57 million from EU; EUR 0.3 million from FONPRODE and 
EUR 0.04 from Kuali Fund). Since Kuali Fund will invest in 15 countries, 100% of GCF resources will be 
allocated in NOL countries, and a 15% of the sum of EU’s contribution in first loss and EU, FONPRODE and

45 MRA 1 (Energy generation and access); MRA 2 (Low-emission transport); MRA 3 (Buildings, cities, industries and appliances); MRA 4 
(Forestry and land use).
