Kuali Fund contributions in TA resources will be allocated in NOL countries (Kuali Fund-GCF), keeping 85% 
of their resources to cover the no-NOL countries of Kuali Fund. 
The objective of Kuali Fund-GCF is to transform FSPs in their transition to become greener and more 
inclusive so that they eventually provide green and climate products to SMEs and smallholder farmers. The 
Funded Activity will also support companies with innovative solutions (denominated as innovative companies) 
to expand the number of available mitigation and adaptation solutions that could eventually be financed by 
FSP. The Funded Activity will be contributing to the economic growth and job creation/support of these 
countries from a social and environmentally sustainable perspective. This objective is expected to be met by 
setting up a EUR 119.5 million investment fund (Component I) and accompanied by Technical 
Assistance (TA) support of EUR 5.91 million (Component II).  An EUR 0.4 million is requested to GCF for 
Communication, audit and evaluation purposes and an additional request to GCF of 2.4 MM EUR for 
Monitoring and Evaluative Data Collection. According to the IRMF, the Funded Activity will have an impact 
on four Mitigation Results Areas45 and two Adaptation Results Areas46, both with their respective core project 
chain results and indicators.  
Both Components I and II will support the same beneficiaries (namely, FSPs, including the SMEs and the 
smallholders financed by the FSPs) and Innovative Companies. In order to be eligible for TA support, 
investees must have been previously selected under Component I, implying the provision of funding. 
Therefore, all beneficiaries of Component I will be offered TA support related to TA focus areas, however in 
rare circumstances the beneficiaries could reject the support offered or show little interest, capacity, and 
commitment. The TA Manager will make its best efforts as that all can develop TA projects 52. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will undertake debt and/or equity investments into FSPs and ICs (Activity I.1.1.2) to build  
climate  portfolios and provide or implement climate products and services and therefore  contributing to the 
adoption of climate solutions and improvement management of agricultural  lands and reducing GHG 
emissions  (Outcome 1); improve resilience in vulnerable people and communities (especially in smallholder 
farmers) (Outcome 2);  and  improve Financial Inclusion and gender outreach (Co-benefit 1); Preserve 
employment and labor women inclusion (specially fueled by the gender action plan) (Co-benefit 2); and 
adopting efficient technologies by SMEs and embracing CSA practices by farmers (Co-benefit 3). 
The Funded Activity will invest in Financial Service Providers (Tier 2 banks, MFIs, cooperatives, non-bank 
financial institutions and Fintech companies) to drive transformational change that will generate a positive 
environmental impact and to improve access to finance by channeling capital to specific underserved target 
segments (SMEs and smallholder farmers), supporting its investees in their green transition to develop  
climate lending SME portfolios with high mitigation and adaptation impact. A special effort will be made to 
build collaboration and synergies between these traditional FSPs and in line with emerging Open Banking 
trends in Europe and in Emerging Markets. Kuali Fund-GCF will transform these institutions to adopt and 
implement green strategies following a progression that we measure with the Green Index 3.0. This Index 
divides the Investments into 4 Phases: 
• 
Phase 1:  FSP has a green strategy and systems to implement it.  
o 1.1–- Definition of the green strategy: 
▪ 
Detailed goals, mission, documented strategy. 
▪ 
Compliance with applicable standards and regulation). 
o 1.2–- Implementation of the green strategy: 
▪ 
Management, governance, and processes. 
▪ 
Monitoring of environmental impacts. 
• 
Phase 2:  FSP identifies environmental risks and opportunities. 
o 2.1 -- Identification of direct risks & opportunities

FSPs in Phases 1-3 are considered “Green Investments” and FSPs and innovative companies48 in Phase 4 
are considered “Super Green Investments”: 
• 
Green Investments will be financed at market rates and the environmental impact generated by 
mitigation of these FSPs’ own carbon footprint and the integration of the environmental risk 
management will be analyzed (including indicators on vulnerability of clients to climate change and 
identification of green financing opportunities according to the clients’ profile).  
• 
Super Green Investments (institutions and companies with innovative climate solutions in Phase 4) 
will be supported to with below-market rates to increase the green portfolio.

With respect to Component I and II, COFIDES as Accredited Entity will be the Executing Entity for all activities 
relating to evaluative data collection and communication audit. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will invest approximately 60% of the capital in FSPs (either Green or Super Green 
Investments) and will provide Technical Assistance to foster their transition into a green strategy and 
eventually offering financing for mitigation and adaptation solutions to its clients, an attractive and 
underpenetrated market opportunity. Kuali Fund-GCF will invest the remaining capital (approximately 30%) 
in Innovative Companies (Super Green Companies) which offer products/services related with mitigation and 
adaptation, including “Agri-tech” and “Climate-tech” companies. Kuali will support the growth of these Super 
Green Investments by establishing partnerships with the Funded Activity’s investments in traditional FSPs or 
with its clients, both SMEs and smallholder farmers. Kuali Fund will invest 60-70 % in debt and 30-40 % in 
equity in growth capital opportunities. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will invest in FSPs which, in turn, might finance SMEs in assets or activities such as solar 
roof-top panels, high-efficiency heat pumps, insulation, low-emission transport,  energy-efficient machinery, 
methane digesters, among others; and the smallholder farmers may be supported in activities such as tree 
plantation, composting, abandoned farmland restoration, farm irrigation systems, regenerative annual 
cropping, silvopastoral systems or tree intercropping. As regards to innovative companies, Kuali Fund-GCF 
will invest in companies with innovative solutions in mitigation (such as electric transport, rural solar energy 
provider, biodigester manufacturer companies, EV charging network) and in adaptation (farm-to-business 
mobility supply chain, smart farming, Farming as a service platform or vertical farmings). 
Kuali Fund-GCF expects to build a portfolio of about 12-18 investees. Each investment is expected to range 
between EUR 5 and 20 million, with an average of EUR 8 million. As mentioned above, debt-like investments 
might have an automatic renewal in the case that certain financial and environmental milestones are met. 
• 
Eligibility and investment criteria 
The potential beneficiaries of the Kuali Fund-GCF are defined as follows: 
• 
Potential investees: 
o Financial Service Providers (FPS): An institution focused on the provision of a diverse set of 
financial services to micro and small entrepreneurs or to micro, small and medium enterprises 
including savings, loans, insurance, leasing, and factoring products. These institutions are 
oriented towards providing mainly enterprise financing (versus consumer financing which is 
dominant in institutions operating in developed countries) through basic and transparent 
banking and a close lender-borrower relationship. The institution can take the form of a variety 
of legal entities, including banks, NGOs, microfinance institutions, cooperatives, finance 
companies and other private types of institutions.  
o Innovative Companies: Companies that provide or implement products and services that have 
a direct positive effect on mitigation and adaptation & resilience to climate change as well as 
mitigation. This relation will be established at the beginning of each investment in the 
Investment Memorandum and the Social Due Diligence.  
• 
Final beneficiaries: 
o MSME/SME: enterprise qualifies as a micro, small or medium enterprise if it meets two out of 
three criteria of the IFC MSME Definition (employees, assets, and sales), OR if the loan to it 
falls within the relevant MSME loan size proxy.

o Smallholder farmers (IRIS+ definition): marginal and sub-marginal farm households 
constrained in size and resources. Some sources define smallholder farmers as those who 
cultivate between 1 hectare to 10 hectares (for more semi-arid areas) of land. While they will 
vary in farm size and crop/livestock distribution according to their activity and region, common 
characteristics of smallholder farmers are that they have low access to technology, limited 
resources in terms of capital, skills, and risk management, depend on family labor for most 
activities, and have limited capacity in terms of storage, marketing, and processing. The 
definition of smallholder might differ from investment to investment, depending on the 
investees’ location, country, and regional area. This definition will be established at the 
beginning of each investment in the Social Due Diligence Memorandum.  
 
Kuali Fund-GCF will originate potential investing opportunities according to the following criteria: 
1. Social and Environmental Impact Criteria: Kuali Fund-GCF will assess the potential social or 
environmental impact of its investees through a tool called Portfolio Impact Score (PIS). This tool 
applies a score to the performance of each project based on social aspects -gender included- and 
environmental objectives previously set. Further details and methodology are provided in Section B2 
(a). 
 
2. Financial criteria: The Funded Activity’s financial eligibility criteria will be further defined in the Policies 
and Procedures Manual that will be developed in the following phase. In particular, in general terms 
and subject to the specific conditions of the country, potential investees need to meet the following 
financial investment criteria: 
• 
For Financial Service Providers: 
o Minimum total assets of EUR 20MM.  
o A positive Return On Assets in 2021 after the COVID-19 impact.  
o Capital adequacy ratio higher or around 15%.  
o Portfolio At Risk > 30 days + rescheduled loans less or around 10%.  
o Write-off ratio less or around 2%.  
o Operational self-sufficiency higher or around 100%.  
o At least 2 years of audited statements and 3 years of operations.  
• 
For companies with innovative climate solutions: 
o Positive equity. 
o EBITDA breakeven or projection for EBITDA breakeven in following 12 months with 
review of debt-to-equity and debt-service coverage ratios.

=== PAGE 37 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33 
 
 
 
 
o Sustainable and scalable sources of revenues (> EUR 500k per year). 
o At least 2 years of audited statements and 3 years of operations. 
Finally, according to the investment selection process, Kuali will follow the next phases for any deal: 
• 
A preliminary report is developed to check the initial criteria and needs. 
• 
This report will be presented to a preliminary internal committee. 
• 
If the Committee agrees to continue with the process, then a Due Diligence will be addressed. 
• 
Once dully finalized, a final report of the operation will be developed. 
• 
This final report and complementary information will pass through a final internal committee to 
decide whether the operation is subject to be financed or not. 
• 
If the committee has agreed to move forward, then the legal and financial documentation must 
be negotiated between Kuali Fund and the beneficiaries. 
• 
Signature of the contracts. 
• 
Disbursement. 
3. Selection of the investment instrument:  Kuali Fund-GCF will select the instrument that (i) best fits the 
financing needs of the investee, (ii) provides, in most cases, the expected risk-adjusted returns and 
(iii) in case of equity, has a clear liquidity event.  More specifically, it is expected to invest through the 
following instruments: 
• 
Debt investments usually with a maturity of 2-3+2-3 years, with automatic renewal in the case 
certain financial or social milestones (such as advancing in the investee's green strategy) are 
met; all investments will be in local currency and any foreign exchange risk will be offset 
through swaps. The typical debt investee will be a FSP. 
• 
Equity or quasi-equity investments including common or convertible equity, royalty 
financing and other mezzanine schemes in FSP or innovative companies. Common or 
convertible equity will be used when a liquidity event for the investee (IPO, secondary sales, 
etc) is feasible in the future and liquidity rights are negotiated (such as right-along rights, drag-
along, mandated sale or right to sale on a capital expansion).  Mezzanine investments can be 
structured as loans where the interest rate has a variable component which depends on 
certain Key Performance Indicators of the investee such as revenues or net income, which 
are proxy for how well the company is doing. The advantage of mezzanine instruments is that 
they can capture equity upsides while also providing a clear exit option. This instrument is 
expected to be used for innovative companies where the best financing option would be equity 
but where exit might be an issue as liquidity events could be difficult. 
Kuali will originate potential investing opportunities according to the following criteria: 
• 
Fit of basic investment criteria (size, stage of development, minimum/ maximum amount of the 
investment, country). In the case of FSPs, investee management must have expressed 
interest in advancing towards green and climate opportunities and products. In the case of 
innovative companies, the company must have a clear social and environmental strategy and 
plans to strengthen and expand its operations. 
• 
Basic ratio analysis assessing profitability (ROA or Contribution/EBITDA Margins), 
Capitalization (Capital adequacy ratio), Portfolio quality and revenue growth rate. 
• 
Quality of management team 
• 
Quality of co-lenders or co-investors 
• 
In FSPs, there are certain Social Performance criteria that can directly exclude an opportunity, 
such as high interest rates (relative to sector), elevated levels of profitability or excessively 
aggressive business plans.

=== PAGE 38 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34 
 
 
 
 
• 
In the case of an equity/ mezzanine transaction, consideration is given of the growth potential, 
quality of shareholders and exit opportunities. 
 
4. Besides all the above criteria, Kuali investment team will ensure that every investment will also have 
an adequate management team with a track record and knowledge of FSPs and appropriate 
Management Systems and Credit Policies. Also, the investments in FSPs need to comply with the 
Anti-Money Laundering and Financing Terrorism (AML-FT) process, have an updated and agreed list 
of exclusion and are not involved in OFAC official and ultimate beneficiary lists. Climatic 
Requirements: Kuali Fund-GCF will be able to support certain beneficiaries with no Climate track 
record in their green transition by incorporating these solutions into their processes and business, 
with the ultimately target of assisting on its transformation. The investment portfolio will balance a mix 
of entities where the majority of their activities are green and entities that are still not advanced in their 
green transition.  
 
• 
Use of funds 
The Funded Activity guidelines for the use of GCF funds are as follow: 
Kuali Fund-GCF Portfolio 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s portfolio will be built during the investment period (5 years from the first closing) complying 
with the following milestones: 
• 
Kuali Fund will use GCF funding for investments in eligible Innovative Companies or for lending to 
FSPs. The FSPs will undertake to use GCF funding only for building Climate Portfolio following the 
eligibility criteria. FSPs will be allowed a period of 3 years to build a Climate Portfolio. If an FSP is not 
able to build this Climate Portfolio by the end of Year 3, then the AE/Kuali Fund will request the FSP 
the total repayment of the stake of the loan provided using GCF proceeds.  
• 
GCF funds will not be used by Kuali Fund/ FSPs for financing assets or projects other than the climate 
projects as per eligibility criteria. 
• 
On an aggregate basis, Kuali Fund commits on a commercially reasonable best effort basis to build 
an aggregate Climate Portfolio equal to one hundred percent of the aggregated amount disbursed by 
co-investors within the Kuali Fund-GCF Funded Activity by the end of the life of the investments. 
• 
Ramp-up period for building Climate Portfolio from co-financing: in 3+3, 50% of Climate Portfolio by 
end of Year 3 and 100% by end of the following 3 years. In 2+2+2, 30% by end of Year 2, 65% by 
end of the following 2 years and 100% by the end of the following 2 years. 
Equity Investments  
GCF funds will be exclusively used for equity investment in Innovative Companies that are companies that 
manufacture or market products and services that have a direct positive effect on mitigation and adaptation 
and resilience to climate change.  
These Companies will not have any other relevant business lines and therefore the funds will exclusively 
support the advancement of climate solutions and impact.  
The sectorial scope is wide-ranging, but it should relate to the sectors, technologies and specific criteria 
depicted in the technology list.

=== PAGE 39 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35 
 
 
 
 
Equity investments in FSP and other Innovative Companies with mixed climatic and non-climatic portfolios 
and solutions will remain an alternative for the rest of investors. Even in these cases, the funds will support 
the advancement of climate solutions and impact. 
All the investment tickets made available to these companies can be considered fully climate finance.  
Debt Investments  
The overall goal of Kuali Fund-GCF resources is to transform FSPs to start developing products and a 
loan portfolio directed towards climate finance. In this regard, Kuali Fund-GCF commits on a commercially 
reasonable best effort basis that by the exit of the investments 100% of its investees shall have expanded 
their climate finance portfolio (if they already have a climate finance portfolio) or at least will have piloted 
climate finance products for their clients. 
FSP will start with different climate-readiness levels, a few with no climate lending yet, however we expect 
that FSPs Climate Finance portfolio will increase over time as they adapt their business model in terms of 
products, process and people to finance assets related with climate finance. 
Kuali Fund-GCF commits to building a portfolio of debt investments in FSPs. The companies in this portfolio 
will have the obligation to increase their total aggregated Climate Portfolio during the life of the 
investments. 
In order to ensure an adequate path towards relevant climate products and portfolios at the investee level, 
Kuali Fund-GCF will include covenants in the loan agreements that will prevent the renewal of the deals if 
a clear path toward climate finance products has not been achieved. 
The climate lending scope is wide-ranging, but it should relate to the sectors, technologies and specific criteria 
for adaptation and mitigation depicted in the technology list. Analog technologies will be eligible. 
The specific sector and technologies climatic criteria are shown in the Investment Criteria and Governance 
Annex. The technology pool displayed does not present carbon lock in risks of long-lived, high-emission 
infrastructure.  
Requirements 
• 
Climate lending relate to the sectors, technologies and specific criteria for adaptation and mitigation 
depicted in the technology list attached as Figure I and II of the Term Sheet. 
• 
The Kuali fund will not finance fossil fuel assets. 
• 
The Kuali fund will not finance energy efficiency investments that extend the lifetime of fossil fuel 
assets. 
• 
The Kuali fund has selected a wide-reaching list of technological solutions that can be frequently 
implemented by the prototypical clients of the FSP and Innovative Companies in the developing 
countries that Kuali will serve. The solutions list proposed by the Kuali fund is as extensive as possible, 
however, the number of agricultural practices, technologies, etc, that potentially represent a climate 
solution (mitigation and adaptation) are countless. During the life of the fund new solutions are 
expected to be added as robust data and peer-reviewed research becomes more available and 
accessible. 
• 
The Exclusion List (attached in Annex 3 of the Term sheet) shall apply to investments under the 
Funded Activity. Based on updated baseline studies, the exclusion list must be revised in consultation 
with GCF.

B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 36 
 
 
 
 
 
49 Investees’ names are confidential in the FP but are available in Annex CO2 Calculations. 
• 
Expected target returns and other terms 
Equity or quasi-equity investments target returns: Kuali Fund-GCF will seek in its equity or quasi-equity 
investments a relevant minority stake (usually between 15 to 25% ownership) and a board seat and a clear 
and/or pre-negotiated exit reflected in the Shareholders Agreement’s liquidity rights. Typical multiples range 
between 2x – 3x Price to Book Value or 7x –- 10x EBITDA, depending on the opportunity and on the country. 
Kuali targets returns exceeding 15-20% p.a. in EUR. 
Debt investments target returns: the Funded Activity targets debt rates to FSPs between 4 and 8% in Euro 
after cost of hedging which vary from country to country. To the maximum extent possible loans will be done 
in local currency and FX fully swapped to EUR with the use of swaps or forwards. 
• 
Expected holding periods 
According to holding period, the senior or subordinated debt with a tenor of either 2+2 or 3+3 years, where 
an option for early repayment will be negotiated so that at year 2 or 3 the investment would be repaid if certain 
financial or environmental milestones are not met (including the commitment to advance in the green 
strategy). The Fund could also consider 2-3 years tenor, with the commitment to extend by 2 or 3 more years 
if the investee is moving forward with the commitment to advance in its green strategy and the financial 
performance remains solid. 
• 
Role in financing rounds: will the Funded Activity act as a lead, co-lead, follower or sole 
investor 
In debt investments, Kuali Fund-GCF normally acts as lead or co-lead lender, investing in companies where 
other large international impact investing firms have already invested in. In equity investments, the Funded 
Activity prefers making co-investments with other carefully chosen investors sharing the same impact 
principles and vision.

India plays an important role in Kuali Fund because the country shows a robust development steadily 
maintained through the last years in the FinTech sector even during the pandemic context. The country has 
the highest FinTech adoption rate; 87%, being the world average a 64%. This development was achieved 
due to an enabling policy environment, a sizeable talent pool, a large unbanked population eager to have 
financial services at disposal, an improving internet access tendency combined with a digital literacy increase 
and a quick smartphone penetration in a growing economy.  Fintechs are critical to transform and improve 
the financial sector, both broadening its potential client range and increasing its efficiency by lowering costs 
as physical presence is not as frequent as in traditional banking. The sector accomplishes this improvement 
by providing services to people historically unattended by the sector because of social and geographical 
conditions (places which are tricky to access, remote rural areas populated by groups at risk from social 
exclusion). 
Latin America and the Caribbean would benefit from the Indian sector´s maturity avoiding overlooking risks 
such as data security, privacy leak or consumer protection issues. India´s experience in adapting its 
regulation to a new and changeable Fintech context come handily in a developing market such as the 
American. Either too lax or too restrictive regulation can imply cost-related challenges. Avoiding them is 
extremely valuable and will generate synergies (south-south cooperation: knowledge transference) which will 
enhance Latin America and the Caribbean to break their endogenous barriers. The American continent has 
a better structured and developed traditional banking sector, whose reluctance to embrace this new 
technology acts as a barrier, and the slow data transmission speed in some areas.

will have a positive impact in the environment resulting in the reduction of 6,796,959 tons CO2.The attribution 
to Kuali Fund-GCF is 2,707,455 tons CO2.

Taking into account the abovementioned solutions and the climate change problems the area faces, here 
below some match examples have been identified between problems and solutions in specific areas and 
sectors: 
Hydrological changes: 
• 
Glacier retreat: Colombia will suffer from water reservoir decreases due to the reduction of glacier 
formations. 
Consequently, the river flow will decrease generating potential water services disruption, distortions 
on the water cycle and a decrease in the hydroelectric power plants generation. To improve water 
efficiency and alternative renewable electricity production, Kuali proposes the following solutions: 
Farm irrigation efficiency, conservation agriculture, distributed solar photovoltaics and high efficiency 
heat pumps.

=== PAGE 44 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 40 
 
 
 
 
 
50 “SME and smallholder farmers Finance Gap. Assessment of the shortfalls and opportunities in financing small and medium 
enterprises in emerging markets”. IFC, Washington DC, 2017. 
• 
Irregular rainfall patterns: Caribbean countries as well as part of Colombia will suffer changes in 
rainfall seasons due to “El Niño” phenomenon. Kuali Fund-GCF will encourage tree plantation and 
intercropping as one of its solutions to achieve rain and soil retention as well as resilient agriculture 
techniques. 
Impact on coastal and marine zones: 
According to the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) the countries suffering 
the sea level rise the most (by number of hectares) would be mostly Brazil and Mexico. However, percentage 
wise, Honduras would be strongly affected by this phenomenon too. It would affect the population living in 
coastal villages and cities, coastal biodiversity, infrastructures (mostly roads and ports) and coastal economic 
activity. Kuali Fund-GCF would be able to reduce the negative effects by the “Tree plantation” solution. 
Specifically, mangroves are quite effective in absorbing potential wave impacts while at the same time 
generating a rich ecosystem and capturing CO2. 
 
Extreme events in coastal zones: 
The Caribbean countries are the main ones affected by this type of catastrophes due to water warming in the 
Caribbean Sea and Mexican golf. In top of the mangroves´ solution, Kuali Fund-GCF can help vulnerable 
people and SMEs living in coastal areas by providing a reliable source of electricity in areas in which the grid 
gets frequently damaged by this kind of events. Off-grid solar photovoltaic combined with high efficiency heat 
pumps or other electric devices can provide a reliable source of electricity and a cost reduction in the medium 
and long term. 
Impacts on agriculture: 
This sector will be affected in many different ways depending on the socio-economic and geographical reality 
of each country as shown previously. Climate change effects (temperature increase, rainfall pattern changes, 
etc.) can produce several effects such as soil moisture, soil loss or reduction of yields. Kuali Fund-GCF 
proposes several solutions for both SMEs and farmers such as: Conservation agriculture, Regenerative 
annual cropping, nutrient management, or farm irrigation efficiency. These solutions generate a resilience 
improvement which helps locals to increase their adaptation capacity to the mentioned natural events.

Traditionally larger Financial Institutions have not served the segments of SMEs and smallholder farmers 
and, as a result, SMEs in LAC region are facing a finance gap of 900 billion USD50. The gap increases by 
additional USD 745 billion if considering demand from informal SMEs. About 31% of formal SMEs in LAC 
have unmet financing needs. The existing funding gap arises from FIs’ limited understanding of the SME 
sector, lack of information or disparities, the large proportion of informal business practices, high market entry

=== PAGE 45 ===

51 https://www.e-mfp.eu/resources/green-index-innovative-tool-assess-green-inclusive-finance-performance-fsps  
52 An enterprise qualifies as a woman-owned enterprise if it meets the following criterium: (A) ≥ 51% owned by woman/women; OR (B) ≥ 
20% owned by woman/women; AND (i) has ≥ 1 woman as CEO/COO/President/Vice President; AND (ii) has ≥ 30% of the board of 
directors composed of women, where a board exists. 
(https://www.ifc.org/wps/wcm/connect/industry_ext_content/ifc_external_corporate_site/financial+institutions/priorities/ifcs+definitions+of+tar
geted+sectors) 
barriers in relation to available margins, heterogeneity of the market, hurdles to SME growth and productivity 
and regulatory limitations.  Serving efficiently the SME segment requires the adaptation of FIs’ business 
models, as the credit assessment, risk management, marketing strategies and products are different from 
more traditional retail and corporate segments. 
Based on Cerise SPI4 “Green Index”, Kuali Fund-GCF will analyze the “green readiness” of the different 
innovative companies and FSPs and will identify gaps that its investees will need to improve if they wish to 
transform to a more sustainable business model. GAWA Capital’s Co-CEOs are certified SPI4 social and 
environmental auditors. 
The Green Index 3.0 has been conceptualized by the e-MFP Green Inclusive and Climate Smart Finance 
Action Group (GICSF-AG) 51 and it is moreover aligned with the new Environmental Dimension of the 
Universal Standards for Social and Environmental Performance Management (USSEPM), developed by the 
joint work of the GICSF AG, SPTF and CERISE.  
This tool will allow Kuali Fund-GCF: 
• 
To assess and understand the current environmental performance of the investees. 
• 
To compare its performance with its peers. 
• 
To identify environmental policies, actions and strategies that could be implemented in the investee. 
• 
To track progress on environmental performance over time. 
• 
To communicate on their environmental performance to Kuali investors. 
The Green Index will not be directly used to determine which FSP/Companies will be financed.  The 
FSP/Companies that will be financed are determined by their capacity to deliver impact (mitigation, 
adaptation) over the investment period. Through this “green readiness” process, potential improvements to 
be implemented through TA projects will be identified.  
Kuali Fund-GCF will ensure systematic and consistent application of the ESMS framework to its investment 
and its adherence to E&S requirements (Annex 6). Potential investees’ financing activities included in the 
IFC’s Exclusion List or with a record of E&S issues will not be considered for financing. 
Besides, Kuali Fund-GCF will categorize the investments according to their existing and potential portfolio 
activities. The Funded Activity will require all its investees to develop and operate an ESMS commensurate 
with the level of E&S risk. In the case of investees financing activities with medium and high E&S (e.g. small 
hydropower, agriculture, forestry, etc.), it will be required to establish an Internal Environmental and Social 
Management System that assesses and ranks all individual loans according to size, loan amount, economic 
activity, and location. The higher the risk, the more exhaustive the evaluation is needed, requiring the entities 
to conduct formal impact analysis before approval. During the Due Diligence process, the General Partner 
team will also conduct an Environmental and Social Due Diligence that ensures that such procedures are in 
place in the potential investees and designs an Action Plans to solve any minor issues. 
Additionally, Kuali Fund-GCF will tackle the SME and the smallholder farmers segments funding gap in LAC 
from three different angles: 
• 
Address financial constraints by investing in and transforming small Financial Service Providers to 
efficiently serve the SME and the smallholder farmers segments -including women-owned SMEs52

B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 42 
 
 
 
 
 
53 During the investment period, the SICAR will also make a one-off contribution of EUR 250.000. 
54 It is common practice that the custodian Bank of the SICAR is also the fiduciary of the TAF. 
and women farmers, expected to represent 30% - and to support collaboration between FSPs and 
Fintechs, leveraging an increasing deployment of open banking solutions by banks and regulators. 
SME clients will benefit from such collaboration because they will have access to wider and more 
innovative products offered by the Fintech companies on banking platforms. TA will be used to adapt 
innovative companies and FSPs’ products, risk management systems, processes, people and 
technology to adequately prepare to serve SME and smallholder clients with green and climate 
products by overcoming main challenges in lending to these two segments (including credit risk, 
engagement of clients and technology adoption, capacity building and financial literacy). TA will also 
be used to improve Social Performance Management and Environmental systems of innovative 
companies and FSPs. 
• 
Productivity/Digitalization: investments in Fintech companies focused on SME and smallholder 
farmers lending, improving the productivity and digitalization processes of these beneficiaries while 
providing financial solutions with a green perspective. Potential collaborations between Fintech 
investees could be established for FSPs and also innovative companies to improve their risk 
assessment by leveraging on-line SME and smallholder farmers data, while Fintechs can broaden 
their marketplace strategies. TA will be used for capacity building and technology adoption in SME 
and smallholder farmers clients, preparing them to access financing from Fintechs and the rest of 
FSPs. TA will also be used to improve Social Performance Management and Environmental systems 
of innovative companies and FSPs. 
• 
Increasing the access to green and climate finance, so that SMEs and smallholder farmers can 
embrace sustainable production and efficient energy use, key elements in achieving a greener 
economy and meeting sustainability standards (required by larger firms integrated in global value 
chains). TA will be used to provide educational programs through innovative companies/FSPs and to 
train also SMEs and smallholder farmers in the economic and financial aspects of renewable energy 
and energy efficiency projects and to train smallholders to adopt climate smart agricultural practices. 
 
Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) 
Structure and Governance 
The TAF (Component II) will be established in parallel with the Funded Activity to provide institution building 
support to investees in enhancing outreach and reducing risk. It will be set up in parallel to the Funded Activity 
base in a Fiduciary Agreement. It shall be managed by the TAF Manager (TAFM), GAWA Capital Partners 
SGEIC, S.A. and oversee by the Technical Assistance Steering Committee (the “TASC”, see below). The 
TASC will have a strategic role and will provide overarching guidance to the TAFM. The TAFM shall operate 
within the guidelines and procedures approved by the TASC and in accordance with a TA Management 
Agreement.  
The Technical Assistance resources shall be made available through a Fiduciary Arrangement (fiducie) 
referred to as the “"Technical Assistance Facility”", “TAF” or “TA Facility”. Therefore, the TA Facility will be 
governed by a Fiduciary Agreement between: (i) the Donors/Contributors to the TAF (including COFIDES on 
behalf of GFC and the EU, AECID and the SICAR)53; (ii) the fiduciary Bank (the fiduciary) and (iii) GAWA 
Capital Partners (the TAFM).  The Luxembourg Trust and Fiduciary Contracts Act 2003 provides the legal 
basis for the creation of this type of contracts.  
The contributions and/or donations of assets made to the TAF are referred as the Fiduciary Assets. The 
Fiduciary Assets shall be credited to the fiduciary account established with a reputable bank in Luxembourg 
(the Fiduciary Bank)54 which will act as the holder of the Fiduciary Assets in its capacity as Fiduciary.

The TAF funds are expected to reach a size of EUR 5.9 million for Kuali Fund-GCF (a total of EUR 12.25 for 
Kuali Fund55) from GCF (EUR 5 million), EU (EUR 0.57 million), FONPRODE (EUR 0.3 million) and Kuali 
Fund (EUR 0.04 million). The TAF contribution provided will be ultimately deployed to address key barriers 
that prevent SMEs and smallholder farmers to adopt mitigation as well as adaptation practices and achieve 
the expected outcomes. GAWA Capital will manage the TAF.  
The TAFM shall apply the contributions in accordance with guidelines (the TA Guidelines) drafted by the 
TAFM and approved by the TASC The TA Guidelines include at least a TA Operations Manual (see initial 
draft in Annex 21), that will refer to TA Policy and TA Procurement Policy, and the TA Annual Plan (TA Annual 
Operational Plan Budget). 
The link between the TAF and Kuali Fund-GCF will be guaranteed by: (i) the innovative companies and FSPs 
supported by the Funded Activity will be also a target for the TAF and (ii) the TAFM will be GAWA Capital, 
which is also the manager of Kuali Fund-GCF. 
 
The TASC shall at any time be composed of at least three (3) members and a maximum of five (5) members, 
including at all times one representative per contributor (GCF or AE in representation of GCF) and one 
independent member (“the Initial Members), and others (e.g. COFIDES), with expertise in SMEs/ smallholder 
farmers finance and TA Funds. This composition shall allow the TAF to benefit from the expertise of relevant 
actors. The Fund manager can attend the TASC meetings but will have no voting rights. The TASC will hold 
at least 3 meetings a year and will have a strategic role (approval of TA Guidelines). However, the TASC will 
also approve all agreements exceeding EUR 200,00056. Decisions among the TASC shall be adopted by the 
simple majority of its members.  
Day-to-day management decisions on TAF deployment will be made by the TAFM. The TAFM will be 
responsible, inter alia, for (see Annex 21-TA Operations Manual): structuring the TA services, managing 
tender procedures, negotiating, and signing agreements with service providers, coordinating and monitoring 
TA Projects, drafting monitoring reports and preparing the TA Guidelines 
Key barriers 
At the level of innovative companies and FSPs, the following key barriers are expected to be addressed: 
• 
Understanding of the addressable market: Kuali Fund-GCF expects to conduct specific country 
market research to identify key opportunities/ clients’ needs for innovative companies and FSPs to 
address profitably.  
• 
Designing financial products that meet clients’ demand: supporting innovative companies and 
FSPs in identifying key assets to finance, engage and build partnership with manufacturers, 
understanding mitigation and adaptation potential. 
• 
FSPs staff able to properly serve SMEs and smallholder farmers segments by providing effective 
training. Effective training will be provided also for innovative companies. 
• 
Ensure that creditworthy clients are able to receive a loan at affordable rates by  
o Adapting credit process to SMEs and smallholder farmers segments by using of data analytics 
and client segmentation as tools to reduce credit costs. 
o Supporting open-banking solutions and Identifying synergies between FSPs in Kuali’s 
portfolio. 
o Leveraging digitalization. 
At SMEs and smallholder farmers level, the following key barriers are expected be addressed with TA:

=== PAGE 48 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 44 
 
 
 
 
• 
Increase awareness about the potential to increase income and reduce CO2 emissions through 
new energy efficient equipment by running workshops/ preparing marketing material. 
• 
Run ad-hoc environmental checkups of individual businesses by suggesting opportunities to 
reduce emissions. 
• 
Support digitalization of processes and streamlining value chains to make them energy efficient. 
• 
Support the adoption of climate smart agricultural practices by running workshops delivered by 
aggregators. 
• 
Support the implementation of climate smart agricultural practices at a farmer level with specific 
training. 
Areas of intervention 
The TAF will support the implementation of new processes that reduce innovative companies and FSPs 
environmental footprint. As a result, approximately 55% of this amount is expected to be delivered to 
FSPs/innovative companies; about 10% to SMEs and smallholder farmers (the final beneficiaries); and other 
10% to ecosystemic projects. The remaining amount will be allocated to TA management (for further 
information, please see Annex 10). 
The TAF will implement  Green Enhancement projects with SMEs and smallholder farmers (Activity II.2.1.1); 
Green Readiness projects with FSPs (Activity II.3.1.1);  Social Performance and Environmental Risks 
Management Systems projects (including gender and SEAH actions) with FSPs and ICs (Activity II.3.1.2.); 
General Business Improvement projects with FSPs (Activity II.3.1.3.),  Business Model  Improvement projects 
with Innovative Companies (Activity II.3.1.4);  Ecosystemic Projects, stakeholder engagement and gender 
actions (Activity II.3.2.1.) The estimated cost breakdown among different activities is provided on Annex 4 
Budget Plan and the TA Operation Manual. 
All these activities together contribute mostly to the Outcomes 2 (Improved resilience in vulnerable people & 
communities) and 3 (Enhanced green & inclusive financial ecosystem).  
Innovative Companies and FSPs will receive TA assistance to improve their Environmental Risk Management 
System (Activity II.3.1.2.); to monitor and mitigate climate and agriculture related risks, aligning it to TCFD 
and reducing innovative companies and FSPs’ environmental footprint. TA will also be used to improve 
innovative companies and FSP’s Social Performance Management System: projects aiming at 
improving the ability of investees to achieve their social mission and adequately manage Social Risks by 
following the Social Performance Task Force Universal Standards (SPTF) and the SPI4 tool of CERISE.  
The Green Index 3.0 tool used during the due diligence will also be used under the TAF. The tool will perform 
a “green-readiness” analysis of the innovative companies and FSPs and identify gaps that these 
institutions need to improve in order to transform to a more sustainable business model.  
This tool includes the following parts, to provide a full picture of the environmental engagement of the potential 
investee: 
• 
Qualitative questions to understand the global environmental performance of the potential investee. 
This will provide a graphic representation of the results, which will help to benchmark the potential 
investee against other financial institutions and identify areas of improvement in the potential 
investee’s green-readiness in the following dimensions: environmental strategy of the investee, 
internal/external environmental risk management, and specific actions being taken by the investee to 
foster green opportunities. 
• 
A quantitative part with detailed data on energy, water, paper, gasoline consumption; number and 
volume of green energy loans disbursed and number of borrowers with active climatic microinsurance 
products. This will help keep track of the progress in the investees’ green activities. 
 
Furthermore, TA will be allocated to innovative companies and FSPs in order to improve their processes and 
provide a comprehensive product offering to SMEs and smallholder farmers as well as supporting these

=== PAGE 49 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 45 
 
 
 
 
beneficiaries in digitalization and implementing better health, safety and environmental protocols. TA will offer 
a general business improvement: projects aiming at improving the financial performance and the risk 
management of the FSPs and ICs, with a special focus on risk assessment for the COVID-19 crisis and 
recovery. 
In addition, TA funding will consider the importance of addressing the consequences of the COVID-19 
crisis in the countries. Therefore, activities such as the creation of new lines of business, financial and 
capacity building education for SMEs and smallholder farmers, new green opportunities in the economic 
recovery or the enhanced development of digital ecosystem will also be supported.  
The investments from Kuali Fund-GCF are also expected to improve opportunities and have a beneficial 
impact on women, given their significant participation in entrepreneurial and farming activities in LAC. The 
objective is to foster an enabling environment by identifying specific areas where the Funded Activity may be 
able to promote women economic empowerment and equality of opportunities. Specific gender interventions 
have been designed to increase women’s participation: i) development of gender policies and action plans in 
innovative companies and FSPs; ii) training and support for innovative companies and FSPs to implement a 
ESMS including gender and SEAH considerations; iii) participation in TA activities; iv) business skills training 
and financial literacy programs and v) financial products adapted to women’s needs and realities. External 
consultants will be hired to carry out country assessments that will serve as the basis for activities included 
in Gender Action Plan (including SEAH). 
 
Eligibility Criteria 
Main eligibility criteria, guidelines, policies and procedures for the application of the TAF /Fiduciary Assets 
will be defined by the TAFM and submitted for TASC approval under the TA Guidelines (i.e. final version of 
the TA Operational Manual drafted in Annex 21 and other Guidelines if requested by the TASC). It is intended 
that partners will be eligible for TA once the investment has been formalized. Some other criteria could be 
anticipated, such as: (i) prevailing interest in advancing towards green and climate opportunities and products 
(FSPs)/clear social and environmental strategy and plans to strengthen and expand its operations (Innovative 
Companies); contribution form partners; etc. For the cross-cutting projects that will indirectly benefit the FSPs, 
Innovative Companies and/or the SMEs/smallholder farmers non investees could be eligible (such as actors 
in the value chains or other agents at meso level). 
TA Implementation (see details in Annex 21. Draft of Ta Operational Plan): 
The TAFM will channel the GCF funds to the final beneficiaries through service providers. The TAFM will 
select and manage reputable service providers as needed to deliver TA services and execute the agreements 
with the relevant service providers. 
TA will be delivered through the provision of grants that cover:  
• 
Consultant Services, i.e., hiring experts and consultants for tailor-made technical assistance for 
investees, SME, smallholder farmers and ecosystemic partners Institutions. 
• 
Trainings: evaluate, train and establish induction programs with green finance related subjects. 
• 
Goods or capital expenditures, i.e., hardware and other goods that the beneficiaries may need, caped 
to a % of the total project cost. 
COFIDES Contracting Procedures, as part of the TA Guidelines, will regulate apply to the selection of 
consultants. The purpose of the Contracting Procedures is to describe all the steps and applicable rules 
required to undertake procurement procedures from the very first steps to the award of contracts with Service 
Providers.

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams) 
Kuali Fund-GCF expects to build a portfolio of about 12-18 investees in the beneficiaries’ countries57. Each 
investment is expected to range between EUR 3.5 to 20 million with an average of EUR 8 million58. The 
average investment size is expected to be higher for FSPs than for innovative companies. 
As already explained, the following instruments are expected to be used: 
• 
Debt-like investments (approximately 60-70% of portfolio) for FSPs with a maturity of 3+3 years, 
with automatic renewal in the case certain milestones (financial or social) are met, or 6 years with the 
option of calling back the capital if certain conditions are not met. All investments will be made in local 
currency and any foreign exchange risk will be offset through swaps. Innovative companies might be 
also financed by debt. 
• 
Equity: approximately 30-40% of portfolio for investing in innovative companies. 
o Mezzanine investments, such as royalty financing or participatory loans. Expected holding 
period: 5-6 years. 
o Growth-capital equity investments with a relevant minority stake (less than 20%), a board 
seat and a clear and/or pre-negotiated exit. Expected holding period: 6-7 years. 
The investment period of the Funded Activity is 5 years, and most investments are expected to be deployed 
over the first 4 years, implying 3 to 6 investments per year.  Kuali Fund-GCF resources will be used for 
business expansion of the investees and not for refinancing. Investments will be recorded at Fair Value 
Through Profit/Loss (FVTPL), according to IFRS 10 Exception to Consolidation. The implementation period 
for the TA will be the 10-12 years of activity of Kuali Fund-GCF.

SICAR) and a Special Purpose Vehicle incorporated as a Securitization Vehicle (SV), together “Kuali Fund59”, 
which are two different vehicles with a separate legal set-up60:

- 
Kuali S.C.A., SICAR-EuSEF:  a corporate partnership limited by shares (Société en 
commandite par actions) qualifying as a société d’investissement en capital à risque (SICAR) 
pursuant to the Luxembourg 2004 Law and a Social Entrepreneurship Fund (EuSEF) pursuant 
to the EuSEFR61. A SICAR is an investment company in risk capital that was designed for 
investments in private equity and venture capital. The SICAR is an externally managed 
Alternative Investment Fund (AIF) and, therefore, the Shareholders have a passive role and 
commit to drawdowns upon requests meeting certain formal requirements.  
- 
Kuali SV: It will be a special purpose vehicle constituted under Luxembourg Law as a 
Securitization vehicle to accommodate the contribution of the Spanish Agency for International 
Cooperation and Development. This SV, regulated by the Luxembourg securitization law, will 
not be authorized, and supervised by the Commission de Surveillance du Secteur Financier 
(CSSF), but by making pari-passu co-investments with the SICAR, its activity will be subject 
to the same rules and requirements of operation and transparency as Kuali Fund, which will 
be authorized and supervised by the CSSF. The sole shareholder of the SV will be the SICAR 
General Partner.  
Kuali SV will be managed by GAWA Capital Partners, and its sole shareholder will be the General Partner of 
the Kuali SICAR. Kuali SV will issue senior notes to FONPRODE and subordinated notes to Kuali SICAR, 
whose participation in the SV will be limited to 10% of FONPRODE’s contribution. Kuali SV will not be 
authorized and supervised by the Commission de Surveillance du Secteur Financier (CSSF), but by making 
pari-passu co-investments with the Kuali SICAR, its activity will, de facto, be subject to the same rules and 
requirements of operation and transparency as the SICAR, which must be authorized and supervised by the 
CSSF. The first loss contribution from EU and GCF, and also the resources in concessional conditions from 
FONPRODE will enable to attract private investors’ participation, as this catalytic contribution will provide a 
function of de-risking the private investments:

63 For more details check Kuali Fund – Manual of policies and procedures. 
- 
The Investors Advisory Committee (IAC) is a body comprised of representatives of Kuali Fund’s 
Investors that have made large commitments (the “Member Investors”) and have requested to be part of 
the IAC. COFIDES shall be a member as well. The number of Member Investors shall be a minimum of 
two and a maximum of eight. One or more observers with no voting rights may be invited. Member 
Investors shall vote on a per capita basis (and not on the size of their respective commitments). The IAC 
shall act on an advisory capacity and shall not decide on the investments. Investments will be identified 
in line with the investment guidelines and requirements included in the Private Placement Memorandum 
(PPM) and the applicable legal documentation. 
In addition, another relevant body will be the Strategic Advisory Group, composed by experts in climate 
change, climate finance, emerging markets, SME, smallholder farmers and indigenous people financing 
agriculture, private sector development and geographical expertise. The Group can make recommendations 
to the Fund Manager on business development strategy for Kuali Fund, purely of an advisory and non-binding 
nature. It will meet on an ad-hoc basis. 
COFIDES will manage the GCF and the EU Proceeds for both investors and the TA.  GCF requirements will 
be further secured through a Side Letter signed between COFIDES and the Kuali Fund. The Fund Manager, 
in coordination with the General Partner, may enter into agreements with certain investors granting specific 
rights (the Side Letters) not included in the Private Placement Memorandum. The Side Letter will be 
formalized as long as the Commitment Agreement (which formalizes the committed contribution to Kuali 
Fund) and will include all the specific requirements that a given Investor (e.g. GCF) obliges Kuali Fund to fulfil 
(e.g. the relevant obligations under the AMA and FAA). Once any financial need is requested by Kuali Fund 
(for either new opportunity, reinvestment, fees…), the General Partner will issue COFIDES, as AE for the EU 
and the GCF, the relevant capital call. COFIDES will review such request and, if agreed, will disburse the 
relevant amount into the Kuali Fund banking account.  Based on Kuali Fund-GCF’s budget and its investment 
estimations, COFIDES will progressively request the GCF to disburse its commitment. The GCF would then 
transfer the requested amount to a COFIDES bank account. According to the TA resources, COFIDES will 
manage the costs related to Communication, which include website design and maintenance, domain and 
logo design, the launching events, promotion and other types of communication and visibility action. For audit 
and evaluation purposes, COFIDES will probably hire an external consultant according to COFIDES 
procedures.  
In summary, the Subsidiary Agreements that COFIDES as AE will put in place for the implementation of this 
Proposal are: i) in the case of the equity component, the Private Placement Memorandum (PPM), the 
Commitment Agreement, and the Side Letter; ii) for the TA component, the Fiduciary Agreement. 
The Executing Entity of Kuali Fund-GCF will be GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC, SA, in its role as sole owner 
of the General Partner (a Luxembourg private limited liability company specifically constituted to set up Kuali 
Fund). The Fund manager (i.e. GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC, SA), as Executing Entity, will establish a 
rigorous investment process with an emphasis on adherence to the investment objectives. The Manager will 
use a disciplined review of potential investments using a standard framework and consistent set of criteria63. 
Investment decisions will only be taken after intensive due diligence, review, and discussion.  
Regarding climate specific knowledge the Investment Committee members have 13 years of experience in 
investing in FSPs and technology companies. The green and climate aspects of the investees have been 
taken into account with several of them already deploying green products. For example, the Technical 
Assistance of the Huruma fund has financed the development of a solar water pump product in Panama, 
targeted to smallholders that currently use diesel pumps. This has allowed the IC members to learn about 
the opportunities and challenges related to climate finance.

Besides, Gawa Capital and its Co-CEOs are initiators and active members of the “microfinance” climate

In addition, a Senior Climate E&S Expert and E&S Analyst with substantial experience on climate lending 
and microfinance will be hired before the initial investments. They will play an important role, offering more 
in-depth technical expertise to the IC and ensuring that adequate baseline, monitoring and verification 
protocols regarding mitigation and adaptation are in place. The investment team will also be expanded with 
2-3 new positions aligned with past experiences.  
The E&S Analyst will also have expertise in gender. The Gender Officer will report to the Investment 
Committee whenever an investment is to be approved and provide gender information to decision-making 
committees. After the investment approval process, GAWA will call the investor commitments and will be 
responsible to ensure that the funds will be adequately allocated in the relevant approved concept (investee, 
expenses, fees). GAWA will be also responsible for the monitoring of the investees.  
Tracking Shares Methodology and distribution waterfall 
Kuali Fund-GCF will distribute the available cash using a "tracking shares" methodology.  Distribution for the 
Funded Activity will be separated and will come from the eligible operations. 
Given the geographic restrictions of each of the Kuali Fund’s first loss investors (EU India, EU LATAM and 
NOL countries), the Kuali Fund SCA SICAR-EuSEF will have different share classes to address the 
geographical constraints of its investors.  
(i) 
 
B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages) 
GCF participation will be essential for Kuali Fund-GCF for several reasons.  The funds will be used to support 
the two sectors in Latin America which are the most vulnerable and potential contributors to reduce CO2 
emissions: the MSME sector as well as the Agricultural sector. 
• 
SMEs are the backbone of the emerging markets economies, representing the vast majority of 
businesses. Estimates suggest that small businesses contribute to 60-70% of total industrial 
pollution 64. Therefore, the ecological transition should be built around decarbonizing MSMEs to 
gradually include environmental practices in their business processes, rethinking the production 
model and adapting the use of energy in companies. Making SME greener will lower production costs 
as a result of lower uses of energy and other resources and it will also enhance their access to export 
markets and to international value chains which are under increased pressure to mitigate the climate 
footprint of their operations. 
• 
Agricultural sector is responsible for 30% of global emissions and farmers are the most vulnerable 
segment of society to the consequences of climate change. Recent research shows that climate 
change is already impacting smallholder farmers activities in Latin America. As a result, 32% of 
smallholder farmers in the region are experiencing food insecurity and producers of essential products 
for the region such as maize and coffee are experiencing drops in productivity as a result of climate 
change.65 
On a positive look there is an increasing number of nature-based solutions that, in addition of providing 
substantial mitigation benefits reducing the carbon footprint of MSMEs and farmers, are also contributing to 
building resilience among these vulnerable segments. When a company invests in a mitigation solution it

=== PAGE 56 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 52 
 
 
 
 
 
66 EU total contribution for First Loss investments will be EUR 12 million for Kuali Fund. 
makes its operations more resilient to climate change. Similarly, many of the nature-based adaptation 
solutions that can be implemented by farmers regenerate the soil producing carbon sequestration. 
The Kuali Fund-GCF aims at ensuring that the MSME and agricultural sector can easily implement these 
solutions by facilitating financing and advisory by MFIs and other financial service providers. These 
institutions have the needed capillarity to deliver the climate-smart solutions to the hands of excluded farmers 
and businesses. The Kuali Fund-GCF will help the institutions to make their green transition so that they start 
offering or expand their financing of climate mitigation and adaptation solution to their clients.   
Apart from investing in MFIs and other feet-on-the-ground financial institutions, the Kuali Fund-GCF will also 
invest in fintech and insurtech companies that are providing financial service and insurance to both MSME 
and farmers in innovative ways, reducing operating costs and tapering new clients in new geographical areas 
in a very efficient way. These players are capturing in increasing market share of the MSME and farmers 
financing market and will be invested by the Kuali Fund-GCF to start offering climate finance solutions to their 
clients. 
In addition, Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest in innovative companies which produce products and services 
related to mitigation and adaptation. Some examples include alternative energy sources such as solar roof 
systems, energy efficient machinery, adaptive/integrated water resource management, climate related 
decision-making services, efficient cold chains or cooling systems and new farming inputs adapted to new 
climate conditions. These solutions are often implemented by climate-tech and agri-tech companies. 
The GCF’s contribution to the first-loss tranche will have a catalytic role. GCF investment will provide a 
function of de-risking (first loss) which is essential to mobilize private investors, particularly institutional private 
investors. This cushion will address perceived risks by the private sector; hence, the proposed structure will 
allow Kuali Fund-GCF to catalyze private sector participation at scale. Besides, the GCF’s contribution will 
foster systemic change via market-based solutions, promoting sustainable private investment and leading to 
less and less need for currently vital, catalytic capital in the future. To this end, a first-loss tranche of EUR 
26.26 million (EUR 25 million provided by the GCF and the EUR 1.26 million by the EU66) is required. 
GCF’s investment will also provide, as a well-respected institutional investor, enormous added value, 
boosting confidence and acting as a stamp of approval to private investors. 
GCF participation will surely attract investors that may be completely new to Impact investing and/or Climate 
finance, giving additional security and boosting environmental awareness in the investing community. 
GAWA’s latest fund (Huruma Fund, focused on boosting financial inclusion and strengthening agri-value 
chains for smallholder farmers in developing and emerging markets) found that the majority of the EUR 90 
million committed by private investors came from investors who had never invested in impact investing or 
agriculture before. This interest was spurred by increasing awareness of the opportunity to boost social 
impact via investments, but also by key catalytic capital injected in the fund thanks to a similar structuring of 
blended finance. 
According to investees, once financial institutions have undergone the proposed transformation and managed 
to begin to cater for the SME and smallholder farmers / climate segment, they will continue doing so after 
Kuali Fund-GCF, creating a systemic shift of capital from financial institutions to SME and smallholder farmers 
/ climate opportunities as well. The participation of GCF in Kuali Fund-GCF will be crucial to the Funded 
Activity's viability and will foster the replication of the project among the countries in LAC. 
The TAF will be key to significantly increase the impact of the financing provided to the FSPs by supporting 
their adaptation to SME and smallholder farmers’ needs. The TAF will also allow improving the outreach in 
scale and depth of SME and smallholder farmers financing and ensuring the sustainability of the project.

=== PAGE 57 ===
B 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 53 
 
 
 
 
 
TA supported by the GCF, EU, FONPRODE and Kuali Fund itself (EUR 5.9 million) is essential to the 
transformation and is an integral part of what Kuali Fund-GCF is offering: investments + knowledge (TA). 
Without TA there would be no transformation, which is at the core of Kuali Fund-GCF strategy. The TA Fund 
is also a crucial component in order to secure the alignment of interest of all the parties involved and the 
highest potential impact of the investment.  
The GCF contribution will therefore strategically contribute to the sustainability of the action by improving 
innovative companies and FSPs’ capacities.  
For the above reasons, Kuali Fund-GCF would probably not be able to be raised without the participation of 
GCF.

67 Founded in 2009, GAWA Capital is the leading impact investor in Spain. GAWA Capital has directly reached 419,000 low-
income households through its investments, fostering their access to financial services and enabling families and 
businesses to thrive: 
- 256,000 families directly affected. 
- 83% female clients. 
- 50% rural clients. 
- EUR 204 million advised. 
- 52 impact investments, supporting 32 entities. 
- 9.6 million total end-beneficiaries. 
Since its inception, GAWA has pioneered the introduction of the impact investing asset class in Spain, having launched 
three funds, in addition to advising a third-party fund - Magallanes Impacto FIL. Each of these funds invest equity and debt 
in emerging countries, in companies focused on providing market-based solutions to key societal issues such as poverty. 
To date, GAWA has advised over EUR 204 million, with investments in 16 countries and a positive track record of 6.34% 
annual IRR to investors in GAWA’s first fund. 
Source: https://www.gawacapital.com/impact  
Kuali Fund-GCF will have a duration of 10 years, with a possible extension of a maximum of 2 additional 
years, subject to the approval of the Investors Advisory Committee. However, the objective of Kuali Fund-
GCF is that financial institutions will have built the right infrastructure and business model to provide green 
and climate loans beyond the life of Kuali Fund-GCF. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will return investors’ capital with a yield in a form of distributions which will take place as 
and when underlying investments reach maturity. Thanks to its extensive track record (50+ investments and 
no losses so far), the Fund Manager (GAWA Capital Partners SGEIC) has a strong pipeline67 in the region 
and carries out exhaustive initial due diligences in addition to close monitoring of each investee throughout 
the life of each investment, assuring the financial sustainability of the Funded Activity. The Fund Manager 
has developed sophisticated financial models which allow for the detailed follow up and management of 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s cash inflows and outflows. The careful selection of target investees, thorough analysis of 
the robustness of their business models, financial and operating environments, implies that the inability to 
provide targeted return is very low. 
Debt financing (including mezzanine transactions) is self-liquidating and may be rolled over once during the 
Investment period.  Debt investments have an average tenor of 6 years, so that at the end of Kuali Fund-
GCF's life (10 years) they have always matured. These operations are always backed by promissory notes, 
which in certain cases can be endorsed to another investor.  In the case of equity investments, from year 6 
onwards the manager starts planning the exit of the investment, having enough time (4 years) to sell trying 
to get the maximum profit. Equity investments’ exit options are evaluated and documented before making 
an investment and are a key decision point during investment evaluation. Several options are typically listed 
in the Shareholder Agreements, in order of priority including:  
- 
Right to sale on future capital expansion,  
- 
Initial Public Offers (IPOs) 
- 
Put option to the majority shareholder (the right, but not the obligation, to sell an asset to another 
party). 
- 
Tag-along rights (if the majority shareholder sells his stake, minority holders have the right to join 
the deal and sell their stake in the same terms and conditions as would apply to the majority 
shareholder).  
- 
Mandatory sale right with drag-along (if the majority shareholder sells his stake, minority holders 
are forced to join the deal). 
Any investment will require the implementation of gender policies of FSPs and innovative companies, aimed 
at ensuring non-discrimination and empowering women as employees and entrepreneurs. The specific 
gender interventions will make investees more inclusive and strengthen women capacities, knowledge, 
sustainable skills and self-confidence.

The GCF and EU contributions to the first loss tranche (EUR 25 million + EUR 1.26 
million) will help mobilizing private investors’ participation and reduce their 
perceived risk of the SME and smallholder farmers sectors. The GCF, EU, AECID 
and the SICAR itself will also contribute to the funding of the Technical Assistance 
(EUR 5 million, EUR 0.57 million, EUR 0.3 million and EUR 0.04 million each).

in Kuali Fund-GCF through Senior debt and EUR 1.8 million from the SICAR itself

million in equity. COFIDES and GAWA will invest in the Funded Activity to align 
their interests and perceived risks, but the amount is still to be determined.

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page) 
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building 
activities? 
Yes ☒    No ☐ 
C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology 
development/transfer? 
Yes ☒    No ☐ 
Kuali Fund-GCF provides capacity building to financial institutions to help them become “greener” 
and to lend to SMEs and smallholder farmers with the same goal. The main key objective of the 
project is to develop and strengthen the capacity at bank level to serve SMEs and smallholder 
farmers with a specific focus on climate lending, by implementing the following best practices/ 
technologies: 
- 
Innovative underwriting methodologies which make lending to SMEs and smallholder 
farmers faster and easier. 
- 
Financial products aim at financing assets that promote mitigation and adaptation. 
- 
Internal Environmental best practices.

=== PAGE 62 ===
C 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 58 
 
 
 
 
- 
Incentivizing climate lending to women led SMEs and women smallholder farmers 
(women led SME and women smallholder farmers are expected to represent at least 30% 
of the total beneficiaries). 
- 
More broadly, all activities supported by the technical assistance facility. 
The TAF is intended to cover the following types of capacity building activities: 
- 
Consultant Services, i.e., hiring experts and consultants for tailor-made technical assistance 
for FSP, Innovative companies and SMEs. 
- 
Training: evaluate prior experience or training in agriculture and establish induction and 
training programs with agriculture related subjects. 
- 
Goods and Services related, i.e., hardware, software and other goods that the beneficiaries 
may need, caped to a percentage to be determined. 
- 
Ecosystemic technical assistance (TA) that will indirectly benefit the FSPs, Innovative 
Companies and/or the SMEs/ smallholder farmers. 
Indeed, to maximize the Funded Activity impact in the agriculture sector, the TAF will support projects 
seeking improvements in markets for products, country-level infrastructure, and other activities that, 
in addition to tailored TA to investees, could be implemented under a Value Chain Development 
(VCD) and/or thematic approach. This will be done through the Cross-cutting TA. The Cross-cutting 
projects of TAF will address specific needs and market failures identified during the investment period 
of Kuali Fund-GCF. TAF should indirectly help the Funded Activity’s end beneficiaries, i.e., producer 
organizations, agribusiness SMEs (both Kuali’s direct investees) or smallholder farmers’ clients of 
FIs which are the investees. 
These activities could embrace specific programs at meso level addressing major common 
challenges for smallholder farmers (e.g., need of better extension Services, access to markets, 
resilience and climate smart agriculture, access to market information, etc.), as well as enhance 
better access to finance for women. The Cross-cutting activities could be channeled through many 
actors in the smallholder support community working at different levels of the value chain, always in 
close relationship and coordination with the Delegations and Government of those countries. 
In addition, Kuali Fund-GCF will support partnerships between FSPs (smaller traditional financial 
institutions and Fintech companies) to build a better offering to SMEs and smallholder farmers; and 
will also develop a methodology/technology to assess “Green readiness” through data analytics, 
machine learning, open banking, etc.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Kuali Fund-GCF aims at transforming Financial Institutions to better serve the unbanked SME and 
smallholder farmers segments with a specific focus on climate lending. As a result of better access to 
financing, SMEs and smallholder farmers will have access to more energy efficient equipment and vehicles, 
clean energy production such as solar panels or assets that allow entrepreneurs and smallholders to adapt 
to climate change. Kuali Fund-GCF will have a significant impact in terms of SMEs and smallholder farmers 
support, women empowerment and in the global fight against climate change, supporting a large number of 
SMEs, smallholder farmers and entrepreneurs in LATAM and avoiding 2,707,455 anticipated tons of CO2 
equivalent in 10 years expected lifetime.  
Adaptation and resilience to climate change will be assessed evaluating the change in vulnerability of 
farmers based on the IPCC methodology that considers vulnerability as a function of exposure, sensitivity 
and adaptive capacity. Climate information will be derive using available platforms to set up customized 
baselines and technology/practice adoption will increment their adaptive capacity68, 
To this end, Kuali Fund-GCF will support more than 12 FSPs and innovative companies financed to 
reach approximately 4,428 SMEs and 237,907 smallholder farmers. This would represent approximately 
0.05% of the population of the target countries of Kuali Fund-GCF. 
The Funded Activity expects to support smallholder farmers in financing assets that foster adaptation to 
climate change such as water solutions or farming inputs better adapted to new climate conditions – 
assuming an average loan of EUR 2,100. Hence, people benefiting from Kuali Fund-GCF’s funding in 
FSPs/innovative companies and potential SMEs/smallholder farmers will have greater access to funding, 
increased business opportunities, greater diversification of their activity, better technology employed, more 
sustainable practices and an increase in their quality of life. Also, the total population of these countries will 
benefit from the sum of tons of CO2 reduced and by increasing their resilience to the effects of climate 
change, in line with the commitment of the Paris Agreement. 
In addition to the above benefits, the potential impact of the transformation of the financial system in FSPs 
and innovative companies, SMEs and smallholder farmers towards greener and more innovative models will 
also reduce the financing gap in these final beneficiaries and between the traditional banking, favor the 
digitalization of beneficiary companies and integrate these final beneficiaries into the value chains thanks to 
their greater competitiveness, which will contribute to strengthening the sector and the economic activity at 
the macro level. 
Moreover, the flow of climate finance towards smallholders will help filling up their financing gap, will result 
in resilience to climate change by this vulnerable population, increase in their productivity and their 
household income. 
Finally, gender targets have been established considering existing data regarding the gender finance gap. 
Kuali Fund-GCF propose to target at least 30% women-owned SMEs and women smallholder farmers to 
enhance the engagement of women in accessing financial services and participating in capacity building and 
training programs. Additional activities are described in the gender action plan.
