D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Kuali Fund-GCF will be an innovative investment fund in the region, benefiting from the Fund Manager's 
long and successful experience in LAC. The benefits that the Funded Activity will bring to the SMEs and the 
smallholder farmers can be easily replicated in the sector by other smaller or new FSPs once the Funded 
Activity has divested, as Kuali Fund-GCF's main objective is to change the trend of business models in the 
financial intermediation towards a greener and more inclusive transition through good practices, processes, 
technology, and appropriate products. After this transformation process, the financial institutions invested by 
Kuali Fund-GCF will be greener and more inclusive so that they can continue with their environmental and 
social impact even after the investment is exited and possibly crowding in more Financial Institutions that 
have not been investees to launch new climate finance initiatives.  
In this regard, the contribution to paradigm shift of Kuali Fund-GCF will be channeled through FSPs and 
innovative companies. If FSPs implement appropriate environmental strategies and risk management 
systems with the support of Kuali Fund-GCF, then (i) SMEs will reduce their carbon footprint and (ii) 
smallholder farmers will increase their adaptative capacity to climate change, because (i) SMEs will invest 
in more energy efficient equipment and (ii) smallholder farmers will invest in assets that support climate 
smart agricultural practices to increase their productivity, increase the resilience of their communities and 
reduce their carbon footprint. From the Innovative Companies’ perspective, if these companies also 
implement these strategies and risk management system, then they will benefit from higher productivity, a 
reduction in their carbon footprint and an improvement of their resilience to climate change, because of the 
transformation of their processes and business models into a more sustainable and ecological models.  
This replicability is directly related to the potential for knowledge sharing and learning. The transformation 
that Kuali Fund-GCF will make in the target sectors will generate lessons learnt for the future development 
of the companies themselves and for new companies that want to incorporate these business models. With 
partnership from the GCF, this Funded Activity will demonstrate that the technology, processes and products 
that will be developed by the funded FSPs and Innovative Companies will favor an improvement in the value 
chain and in the business structure that is expected to have a high impact on the sector, favoring the 
resilience of SMEs and smallholder farmers towards the ecological transition. Moreover, the influence of 
private capital has the potential to support businesses that build climate resilience for smallholders, while 
also supporting overall growth of SMEs and smallholder farmers sectors. This will allow Kuali Fund-GCF to 
develop a strong track record in climate resilient financing and will enable to raise future, larger funds to 
invest in more companies that are aligned with the GCF’s impact goals. In this regard, by investing in for-
profit companies, GCF and other investors in Kuali Fund-GCF will be creating financially sustainable 
enterprises that continue to operate through internal profits and follow-on investment capital even after the 
Funded Activity has divested. The expected success of Kuali Fund-GCF, due to an experienced Fund 
Manager team and a huge financial gap with a high-potential demand of FSPs/innovative companies and 
SMEs/smallholder farmers, will build further expertise and a strong track-record in the climate resilient 
investment scope, which will enable Kuali Fund-GCF or other investors in the region to raise more money in 
the future and make more investments. 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s investments will develop, prove and scale innovative inclusive finance, reduce the impact 
of climate shocks on the incomes of SMEs and smallholder farmers. Success stories from such investments 
should attract further private sector capital into those same companies and other similar that arise to 
compete, which ultimately will serve to increase the scale and impact of the final beneficiaries.  
In this context, Kuali Fund-GCF will develop its investments within an adequate environmental and regulatory 
framework, thanks to the previous experience in the region of the Fund Manager and of the accredited entity. 
The potential investments will have significant impact within the specific identified countries. Such 
investments will create employment opportunities, local institutional knowledge that can be shared within

=== PAGE 65 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 61 
 
 
 
 
public and private sectors and strengthen local supply and distribution value chains required for scaling 
sustainable businesses.  
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
SMEs are the backbone of the emerging markets economies and contribute 60-70% of total industrial 
pollution. SMEs need to include environmental practices in their processes, which will lower production 
costs and enhance their access to international value chains. On the other hand, the agricultural sector is 
responsible for 30% of global emissions and farmers are the most vulnerable to the consequences of 
climate change. 32% of smallholder farmers in LAC are experiencing food insecurity and producers of maize 
and coffee are experiencing drops in productivity as a result of climate change. 
From the perspective of mitigation measures, preventing and reducing the emission of greenhouse gases 
into the atmosphere will make the impacts of climate change less severe. Kuali Fund-GCF will seek to reduce 
human interference with the climate system in order to ensure that food production is not threatened and to 
enable economic development in a sustainable manner. Securing affordable-climate friendly energy is key 
to support climate mitigation. Several initiatives are being developed to cut greenhouse gas emissions, 
adopting legislation to increase the use of renewal energy and improve overall energy efficiency. From the 
adaptation to climate change side, it is really important to invest in supporting farmers and communities to 
better manage unpredictable weather patterns and maximize the resources by connecting them with 
information, tools and services for growing hardier yields and raising livestock. This includes helping farmers 
diversify their crops and redesign their farmland to improve their productivity and protect the soil, so they are 
better equipped to continue growing food and earning income in the face of drought.  
Kuali Fund-GCF will be deeply committed to creating sustainable development in developing countries, as 
evidenced by previous activities and track record of both the Fund Manager and the Accredited Entity. The 
Funded Activity will always seek to cooperate with local partners with a high potential of sustainable and 
innovative growth. The collaboration with these local partners will create appropriate values and processes 
that provide a sustainable and durable model for the future. Each investment decision will be made on the 
basis of a triple bottom line methodology, considering the economic, environmental and social sustainability 
view: 
- 
From a global perspective, Kuali Fund-GCF will also promote greater competitiveness in the 
target sectors, a modernization of the regional financial sector and the contribution to economic 
growth in the target countries. 
- 
From an economic perspective, the benefits identified are: increase in the number of jobs, (over 
640,000 indirect jobs supported) economic growth of SMEs and smallholder farmers, greater 
access to financing of SMEs and smallholder farmers that have not traditionally been supported, 
the strengthening of small financial services providers that have a high innovative component, 
the reduction of the gap between traditional banking and these FSPs and innovative companies, 
the synergies that can be found with traditional banking and the increase in purchasing power of 
small businesses and families. Additionally, the flow of financing to the smallholders will result in 
an increase in agriculture resilience through climate adaptation, which will result in an increase in 
agriculture productivity. 
- 
From a social perspective, as a result of better financing, SMEs and smallholder farmers will be 
able to grow, increasingly engaging with international value chains, create more quality jobs and 
therefore increase income and purchase, which can result in better health, education and 
livelihood. The increase in smallholders’ productivity will result in higher household income for 
this especially vulnerable segment. The transformation that Kuali Fund-GCF will achieve will 
provide the FSPs/innovative companies and final beneficiaries with a greater knowledge and 
sustainable skills that will be useful after the Funded Activity’s investment. These processes will

=== PAGE 66 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62 
 
 
 
 
lead to an increase in competitiveness in the sector and better access to new technologies, 
making their business models more efficient. Women’s local organizations will be identified and 
engaged with in country targets to incorporate their knowledge on women empowerment. The 
Gender Action Plan will ensure that cross-cutting gender measures are integrated throughout the 
project. The participation of women into the productive sector has repercussions on economic 
growth and on a better distribution of income, since they are agents that promote the education 
and well-being of children, resulting in an increase in the human capital of future generations. The 
commitment in gender and the number of jobs supported will imply a co-benefit in women 
employment during and after the lifespan of Kuali Fund-GCF. 
- 
In addition, Kuali Fund-GCF will have a clear benefit in the environmental field. The Funded 
Activity will contribute to the mitigation objectives of the GCF through estimated savings of 
approximately 2,707,455 tons of CO2 equivalent, distributed over the life of the Funded Activity; 
and will also contribute the soil structure with the adoption of agro-ecological practices (such as 
improvements with crop residues through mulching, natural restoration of soil fertility, anti-erosion 
techniques through hedgerows or agroforestry, etc.). This will imply a clear co-benefit in the 
improvement of the air quality. 
D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
The climate gap in the LAC region is USD 2.3 billion. Large financial institutions in the region have been 
channeling green lending towards the financing of large renewable energy projects, large industrial power 
plants and large efficient building construction companies.  
However, smaller financial institutions are key to mobilize climate finance to markets underserved by large 
banks. These institutions are essential to channel climate finance towards SMEs and smallholder farmers. 
Nevertheless, capital doesn’t glow into these two segments naturally. As already mentioned, according to 
IFC69, the latest estimates show that the formal SME financing gap70 in LAC is estimated to be over USD 
900 billion – 3.1x the current level of SME lending-. Financing demand increases USD 745 billion if informal 
SMEs are considered. About 31% of formal SMEs in Latin America have unmet financing needs. 
Additionally, smallholders’ finance gap in the region is USD 20 billion71. 
Therefore, these smaller institutions need to transform their risk management systems, processes, 
technology, staff and products to be ready to mobilize green and climate products for SMEs and smallholder 
farmers in a sustainable and efficient way. 
Given the strong adoption of sustainability values among multinationals, the SMEs and the smallholder 
farmers that are proactive on sustainability issues and transparency are in a better position to gain access 
to new value chains and improve their business. Kuali Fund-GCF will therefore transform Innovative 
Companies and mainly FSPs sector with innovative and ecological practices that will transfer this new 
methodology and know-how acquired to the SMEs and the smallholder farmers, which will increase their 
competitiveness in the sector in a sustainable way. This may lead to an increase in opportunities for SMEs 
and smallholder farmers in global value chains. 
The financial institutions financed by Kuali Fund-GCF will support farmers by increasing their financial 
resources to build resilience against climate change (e.g. by improving the irrigation systems, buying more 
suitable seeds…). Smallholder farmers are often more vulnerable to impacts of climate change, especially 
women farmers. This population will need to rapidly adapt to new climate conditions by adopting climate 
 
69 “SME Finance Gap. Assessment of the shortfalls and opportunities in financing small and medium enterprises in emerging 
markets”. International Finance Corporation, Washington DC, 2017. 
70 The financing gap is defined as the amount of credit that would be needed to satisfy the demand of unserved and 
underserved formal SMEs. Unserved SMEs are those that do not have a loan but need one. Underserved SMEs are those 
that have a loan but still find access to finance to be an operating constraint. 
71 Pathways to prosperity 2019, Initiative for Smallholder Finance.

smart agriculture principle and equipment. It is estimated that 95% of the 15 million smallholder farmers in 
Latin America and the Caribbean are already experiencing negative impacts from rising temperatures, 
unpredictable rainfall and extreme weather events on crop yields, pest, and disease incidence, decreasing 
income generation and food security. Small farmers can also reduce their carbon footprint by using new 
equipment such as biodigesters and reforesting degraded areas. Financial institutions can play a key role in 
financing climate smart agriculture equipment to increase adaptation and promote mitigation. As a result, 
the Funded Activity may also contribute to poverty reduction. 
During earlier stakeholder engagement, the key factors identified were (please refer Annex 7): 
Lack of tools and knowledge of farmers to protect their crops from adverse climatic events. This has been 
integrated into Kuali Fund-GCF's proposal through the financing of climate adaptation financial products and 
technical assistance to entities to develop these green products and offer training to end customers on better 
agricultural adaptation practices. 
Lack of women's access to individual financial products, and mainly lack of access to green financial 
products. Through the focus on gender equity detailed in Annex 8, Kuali Fund-GCF will analyse the reach 
of the entities to women through individual products and propose an action plan to increase that reach. The 
design of a specific financial product for women may also be considered, if relevant. 
Lack of a permanent market for farmers to sell their products, or even very low prices received for the sale 
of their production. Kuali Fund-GCF includes in its technical assistance proposal a block of projects that are 
aimed directly at end customers, and which can help farmers to improve their access to markets and their 
product offering (try to sell value-added product variants) in order to make their product more attractive to 
potential buyers. 
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s mission is aligned with the political strategy of the LAC region countries, as evidenced in 
their national climate policies and National Determined Contributions (NDCs) targets submitted under the 
Paris Agreement. Kuali Fund-GCF will contribute to climate change mitigation goal and will provide 
adaptation benefits to climate change and to social development of FSPs, innovative companies, SMEs and 
smallholder farmers in the target countries. Every targeted country72 should promote the transformation of 
the financial system73 with greener practices and the flow of green and climate products to SMEs and 
smallholder farmers.  
COFIDES, as an international private-public company and as a European Development Financial Institution 
(EDFI), is highly committed to various International Climate Change mitigation and adaptation initiatives in 
LAC. The over 30 years of experience working with private sector and with institutional and multilateral 
organizations in sustainable, development and climate projects has also built a significant experience in 
international private sector project mobilization, facilitating the contact with international al national 
institutions based in target countries. Moreover, the Fund Manager’s previous experience and its know-how 
in development and sustainability projects in Latin America, such as Huruma, will be a key factor in the 
potential success of Kuali Fund-GCF.

BRAZIL 
 
The National Policy on Climate Change (2009) - PNMC, by means of Law no 12,187/09. In 
accordance with this law, Brazil will pursue voluntary actions for the mitigation of greenhouse gas 
emissions with a view to reducing its projected emissions by 36.1%-38.9% by 2020. The PNMC is 
implemented by means of several action and sectoral plans for the mitigation and adaptation to 
climate change, in compliance with the principles and provisions of the Convention:  
o Action Plan for the Prevention and Control of Deforestation in the Legal Amazon (PPCDAm 
in the Portuguese acronym). 
o Action Plan for the Prevention and Control of Deforestation and Burning in Cerrado 
(PPCerrado in the Portuguese acronym). 
o The Ten-Year Energy Expansion Plan (PDE in the Portuguese acronym)  
o Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for a Low Carbon Emission 
Agriculture (ABC Plan in the Portuguese acronym). 
o Sectoral Plan to Reduce Emissions in the Steel Industry. 
o Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change for the Consolidation of a 
Low Carbon Emission Economy in the Manufacturing Industry (Industry Plan). 
o Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change in Low-Carbon Emission 
Mining. 
o Sectoral Transport and Urban Mobility Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate 
Change (PSTM in the Portuguese acronym).  
o Health Sectoral Plan for the Mitigation and Adaptation to Climate Change (PSMC-Saúde in 
the Portuguese acronym). 
Several components of these plans, such as the use of renewable energies and the fight against 
deforestation, are also essential parts of the Brazilian sustainable development strategy. Submitted 
to the Convention as a Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Action (NAMA), the National Policy on 
Climate Change provides that the country will adopt voluntary actions to mitigate greenhouse gas 
emissions at the national level with a view to reducing its projected GHG emissions by 36.1% to 
38.9% by 2020. The goals achieved are recognized, including by non-governmental entities, as one 
of the most ambitious contributions undertaken by a single country to fight climate change. 
COLOMBIA 
 
2017 The National Policy for Climate Change - Plan Nacional de Adaptación al Cambio Climático 
(PNACC)- aims to incorporate climate change management into public and private decisions to 
advance in a climate-resilient and low-carbon development path that reduces the risks of climate 
change and allows opportunities to be seized. The long-term objective is for the country to be carbon 
neutral. 
 
The Colombian Low-Carbon Development Strategy (ECDBC) was launched in February 2012 as 
a tool to deliver the objectives of the National Development Plan 2011-2014 and the CONPES 
Document on Climate Change by promoting efficient low-carbon growth. 
 
National Disaster Risk Management Plan and the Development Strategy for the period 2015-
2025 (Decree No. 308) - the Plan has as a general objective to guide the actions of the State and 
civil society regarding risk awareness, risk reduction and disaster management in compliance with 
the National Risk Management Policy. It should also contribute to people's safety, welfare, quality of 
life and the sustainable development of the country. 
 
2017-2022 Indicative Action Plan of the Rational and Efficient Energy Use Programme 
(PROURE) defines objectives and targets for energy efficiency, actions and sectoral measures and

strategies for the achievement of goals and other provisions for the period 2017-2022. It replaces the

 MEXICO 
 
General Law on Climate Change in 2012, it specifies the different responsibilities of the Federation, 
Mexico's pledge under the Copenhagen Accord, in terms of committing the country to an emissions 
reduction target of 30% below Business As Usual (BAU) by 2020, subject to the availability of 
financial resources and technology transfer, as well as 50% GHG emissions reduction by 2050 
compared to 2000. The law was amended in 2014 to establish a tax on fossil fuels, and in 2016 at 
article 94 to frame a carbon market. The attached regulation sets the framework for the accounting 
of carbon emissions. The law was further amended in 2018 by Decree 13/07/2018, notably to initiate 
a national market of greenhouse gases emissions and to precise the contribution of Mexico within 
the scope of the Paris Agreement. 
 
The National Climate Change Policy includes a 10-, 20- and 40-year outlook for achieving climate 
change goals. Sets a target of 30% GHG emissions reductions from the base year. 
 
The Special Programme on Climate Change, -the Programa Especial de Cambio Climático 
(PECC), published in August 2009- sets Mexico’s long-term climate change agenda, together with 
medium-term goals for adaptation and mitigation. It incorporates the planning for each administration, 
considering specific programs, goals, and resources.  
 
State Climate Change Programs: incorporates the planning of each state, considering their specific 
competences, resources, and climate state level regulations. 
 
2008 Laws for Sustainable Energy Use and for the Use of Renewable Energies and Funding 
the Energy Transition encourage and regulate renewable energy and low-carbon electricity. 
 
2002 General Law for Sustainable Forest Development promotes a variety of initiatives to reduce 
deforestation and forest degradation. 
PANAMA:  
 
National Climate Change Strategy: One of the lines of actions leading to meet this objective is to 
reduce vulnerability of the communities with the highest exposure to adverse climate change effects 
through financial instruments and specific national funds for adaptation to climate change.  
 
Nationally Determined Contribution to the Mitigation of Climate Change (NDC) before the 
UNFCCC: The working focus outlines a set of actions which will allow Panama to responsibly 
decrease its vulnerability in the face of the adverse effects of climate change, by prioritizing and 
implementing specific adaptation measures.  
 
Governmental Strategic Plan (PEG) for 2015-2019: prescribes, among other measures, "to 
consider the battle against climate change and its effects as a fundamental focus of the governmental 
action, by stopping deforestation and restoring the vegetative cover to prevent desertification and by 
reducing the existing vulnerability through the development and application of adaptation and 
mitigation measures".  
 
Integrated Development Plan for Aboriginal Peoples of Panama: promotes actions related to 
political participation, monitoring of human development plans, conflict prevention, poverty reduction, 
access to information, among others.  
 
2008 Biodiversity National Policy Strategy and Action Plan: integrate the group of 7 national 
policies of the Participative Strategic Plan, as well as the Climate Change Policy.

EL SALVADOR: 
 
Updated Nationally Determined Contributions 2021: The document outlines a set of actions that 
will allow El Salvador to have a consistent strategy in order to face the adverse effects of climate 
change. The NDC includes three strategic areas: i) Ecosystems recuperation through social 
inclusion, ii) Highly degraded areas restauration trough resilient agroforest systems and sustainable 
agriculture and iii) Synergic development of physical and natural infrastructure. 
 
El Salvador Sustainability Plan: Published in 2018, This Plan focusses in identifying paths to create 
economic and social development taking into account the environment. The structure of the Plan 
followed consist in four thematic axes: i) Strengthening institutions regarding sustainability, ii) 
Transforming the productive sectors to efficiency and competitiveness, iii) Enhance sustainability 
education, iv) Holistic risk understanding in terms of climate change to develop an action plan. 
GUATEMALA: 
 
Nationally Determined Contributions Guatemala 2021: This document synthesizes the country´s 
context in order to understand why and how Guatemala aims to develop its sustainable development 
strategy. The document, after being updated, gives special emphasis to four specific sectors in order 
to generate mitigation impacts: i) Land use change of land use and silviculture, ii) Energy, iii) 
Agriculture, iv) Residues. It also provides an adaptation component aligned with the national 
adaptation plan (PANCC). 
 
National Adaptation Plan (PANCC): This Plan presents the different strategies Guatemala wants 
to develop. It is presented as the summary of the priority actions and precautions that citizens and 
institutions should take into account to confront the risk intrinsic in the climate change taking the 
IPCC report as reference. It focuses on the identification of sustainable development opportunities 
of vulnerable people protection. It focuses in the following six sectors: i) Coastal zones, ii) Agriculture, 
farming and food security iii) Forestry resources, ecosystems and protected areas, iv) hydric 
resources integrated management, v) Human health, vi) Infrastructures. 
 
Adaptation, Mitigation and Vulnerability Reduction Regulatory Law: Law primarily designed to 
fight climate change. It also guarantees the correct procedures to develop the PANCC and the NDC 
strategies. 
HONDURAS: 
 
Updated Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) 2021: The document has incorporated five 
key elements to guide the work to do: i) Social inclusion, ii) Greenhouse gases mitigation, iii) Climate 
Change Adaptation, iv) Financing, v) Report and verification mechanisms. The energy and the 
agriculture sectors are the two fundamental areas in which the country wants to reduce its emissions. 
 
 NAMA for a low carbon livestock sector: Published in 2018, this document shows the importance 
of the rural sectors in Honduras and the necessity of a quick transition to a sustainability path in order 
to stabilize the way of living of thousands of families. 
• 
Honduras Climate agenda: Published in 2016, it provides an institutional and programmatic 
framework to develop the National Adaptation Plan and the National Mitigation Plans. 
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Thanks to the first loss tranche provided by the GCF and the EU, Kuali Fund-GCF expects to mobilize a 
substantial amount of private capital. The GCF investment will have a de-risking effect (first loss) for private 
investors. The resources of the GCF and the EU will encourage the participation of private investors, who

=== PAGE 71 ===
D 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 67 
 
 
 
 
will see their investment risk reduced, so the proposed structure of Kuali Fund-GCF will allow catalyzing 
private sector participation. The de-risking resources will represent 22.2%75 of the total investment. 
Concerning the return of capital to First Loss Shares, GCF would be integrated into Class E Shares and will 
receive 100% in proportion to their Capital Contributions until distributions equal four percent (4%) per annum 
above their capital committed to Kuali Fund-GCF, compounded annually, on their aggregate Capital 
Contribution.  
The minimum concessionality of GCF resources is justified as it is the necessary level of leverage for the 
private investors to obtain a minimum return in order to make the investment attractive. Likewise, it also 
ensures the viability of the project and ensures an adequate return for institutional investors. 
The efficiency and effectiveness of the GCF resources, considering the total financing and mitigation/ 
adaptation impact, will be adequate since every ton of CO2 would cost 9.21 EUR (less than the average in 
201976: 23 EUR). 
Moreover, the Portfolio Impact Score methodology designed by the Fund Managers fosters a positive 
environmental and social performance, since a good performance in this field within the global SME and 
smallholder farmers portfolio ensures a higher carried interest for the Fund Managers. 
According to the available technologies and practices, the Funded Activity will use best of art Impact 
management techniques, by supporting banks to implement best practices in terms of integrating technology 
in their business model, such as: 
- 
Open banking. Kuali Fund-GCF will ease the banking transactions for the clients. 
- 
Synergies between FSPs and traditional banking. 
- 
Innovative credit rating methodology leveraging machine learning. 
- 
Fostering banks to lend money to SMEs and smallholder farmers so they can buy the best energy 
efficient equipment.

82 Kuali Fund-GCF assumes a distribution of the total EUR 119.5 million among different sectors and technologies that are related to the investable universe and pipeline. For 
more details and specific data see Annex 22 and CO2 Calculation Excel File. 
  Other externalities to be considered refer to: 
· Technology – The state of available technologies regarding Energy generation, low emission transport and Buildings, cities, industries and appliances, and their prices and 
implementation costs might vary over time.  
· Economic – international financial conditions like inflation, interest rates and bank risk appetite.  
· Political - Commitments and targets regarding climate mitigation and adaptation 
· Legal and Regulatory - Central banks regulations regarding green and climate finance. Taxonomy development.  
· Social – Uptake and popularity of Nature Based Solution, Regenerative Agriculture among smallholder farmers. 
83 CO2 target emission reduction are calculated considering the specific technologies financed in every sector. The emissions are calculated in relation with a baseline scenario: 
Relative Emissions = “With” Project Emissions (Wp) – “Without” Project Emissions, or Baseline Emissions (Be). The baseline scenario (or “without” project scenario) is defined 
as the expected alternative means to meet the output supplied by the proposed technologies. Baseline emission and project emissions are calculated using technology and 
country-specific electricity emission factors. Besides Kuali fund uses assumptions around unitary costs, efficiency gains and capacity factors to construct the “with” project and 
baseline emissions levels, also considering the specific lifespans of the different technologies. Moreover, in order to calculate GHG emissions avoided/removed, the Kuali fund 
will use the Guideline for a Harmonised Approach to GHG Accounting elaborated by the technical Working Group of the International Financial Institutions (IFI TWG) of The 
United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the GHG Protocol Agricultural Guidance in the case of the Agriculture and Forestry sector. For more 
details and specific data being used see Annex 22 and CO2 Calculation Excel File.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 97 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 93 
 
 
 
 
 
Kuali Fund-GCF represents a significant initiative to address current environmental and climate challenges. With the aim of ensuring effectiveness 
and transparency in action implementation, a comprehensive Monitoring Plan has been developed to span the entirety of the Fund's lifespan. This 
plan not only seeks to fulfill regulatory obligations but also aims to maximize the positive impact of the Funded Activity. 
- 
Annual ex-post third party verifications: 
These verifications will be carried out by a third-party validation and verification body. The Programme will appoint a third-party agency to verify the 
GHG emission reductions generated by Kuali Fund investments. By conducting these independent verifications, Kuali will be able to report GHG 
reductions to GCF in an open, transparent, and consistent manner during the project implementation. 
Monitoring: 
The plan encompasses a rigorous project monitoring process to ensure the efficient execution of funded activities. Examples of monitoring activities 
include: Establishment of key performance indicators (KPIs) to measure progress in implementing each project; Periodic reviews of progress reports 
provided by project stakeholders; On-site visits to directly assess the status of projects and address any identified issues. 
Additionally, the AE will undertake the following requirements as monitoring, reporting and evaluation covenants: 
• 
Contractually require, monitor and enforce that the FSPs receiving funding under the Funded Activity apply such amounts towards meeting 
the agreed portfolio targets described in the paragraph below; 
• 
Ensure that subsequent disbursements (whether by way of a capital call or a disbursement under the relevant debt instrument or otherwise) 
from Kuali Fund-GCF to an FSP are based on evidence of the FSP having met the agreed portfolio targets during the Ramp-up Period, where 
such portfolio targets per FSP are based on the baseline studies and proposed by the Kuali Fund-GCF;  
• 
Contractually require, monitor and enforce such requirement, that the verification of targets shall be based on annual sub-loan reporting to 
the Kuali Fund by each FSP and certified by an independent verifier, which shall inform the Kuali Fund-GCF on the type (number and volume) 
of loans that are climate aligned and other loans (which shall form part of APR referred in paragraph above).  The climate alignment aspect 
must be based on the Eligibility Criteria  and the relevant Study referred in Clause 13.I (h). of the Term sheet;  
• 
Ensure that the targets referred to in paragraph above are verified by an independent verifier based on terms of reference and the approach 
agreed between the Accredited Entity and the Secretariat; 
 
- 
Impact Assessment: 
Impact assessment will be an integral component of our monitoring plan. Examples of activities include: Analysis of collected data to assess the real 
impact of interventions in terms of emission reduction and social benefits; Surveys and stakeholder consultations to gather valuable feedback on 
perceived impact at the local level; Comprehensive assessment reports to be shared with all stakeholders, fostering transparency and accountability.

=== PAGE 98 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 94 
 
 
 
 
 
The AE (COFIDES) will monitor the EE’s (General Partner or GAWA) in two different scopes: (i) ESG performance and compliance, and (ii) impact 
reporting both on an annual basis and by the corresponding evaluations. Furthermore, the General Partner is available on an on-going basis for any 
questions the AE may have regarding Kuali Fund-GCF assessment and impact reporting/methodologies. 
The value of the EE’s impact indicators should be based on reliable source documents, such as latest audited annual reports or consultant reports. 
If none of those are available, then data coming directly from client may be used. The information will be referred for verification purposes to the 
original source including the name of the document, the year of the report, the page number, etc. and saving the document in a permanent folder for 
the rest of the fund life. To verify data, Partnerships for Development (area in COFIDES in charge of the GCF accreditation and identification, analysis, 
monitoring, evaluation and all issues related to GCF) will check this monitoring information internally in collaboration with E&S department, legal 
department and internal audit department. For measuring the impact data correctly, EE will develop an impact measurement tool to enable portfolio 
companies measure its GHG emission reduction; enhancing capacity of portfolio companies to measure climate impact. GAWA will be in charge also 
of any update in the monitoring framework, reporting and verification of GHG emission of any portfolio company of Kuali Fund-GCF. 
GAWA Capital is focusing not only on measuring its environmental and social impact, but also to actively managing the investments in order to 
maximize the potential environmental and social impact. To do so, the Fund Manager have integrated a social performance management system to 
the investment process.  
The Impact Score will be measured at the beginning of each investment during the social and environmental due diligence. It will be measured and 
monitored on a quarterly basis. After the exit of each investment, the Investors Advisory Committee will select an independent social auditor that will 
measure the Audited Impact Score. An Audited Impact Score will be obtained as the average of all the investee’s Audited Impact Score. This Audited 
Impact Score will determine half of the potential carried interest of the Fund’s managers. 
The Fund Manager shall report to the Shareholders on a quarterly and on an annual basis.  
The quarterly reports shall be delivered to Shareholders 45 days after the end of each quarter and shall include, at least, the following information: 
- 
NAV of the Funded Activity and by share class and unaudited financial statements. 
- 
Economic and markets information of the investee countries. 
- 
Information about the investee portfolio and breakdown by geographies. 
- 
Financial performance of investees as well as ESG metrics and gender metrics (including, if available, the reporting indicators). 
- 
Impact score evolution.

=== PAGE 99 ===
E 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 95 
 
 
 
 
 
 
92 https://www.marketplace.spglobal.com/en/solutions/ilevel-software-(1480715a-1925-49f3-8b63-4e88178a2300)  
- 
Information 
about 
the 
investments in pipeline. 
-

and

to

-

On an annual basis, the Fund Manager 
shall deliver to the Shareholders:

-

following year. 
- 
E&S Annual Report including 
gender issues and GAP 
implementation. 
- 
TAF Annual Report. 
 
The aggregated quarterly and annual reports are constructed by the sum of individual reports at the investees level. Data will be captured and 
recorded through the iLEVEL92 software a tool that streamlines data collection, portfolio monitoring, analytics, valuation, and reporting. At the Due 
Diligence and initial investment phases (sometimes with the help of dedicated consultants that will guide the institution) specific Climate lending 
eligibility requirements, CO2 baselines, CO2 mitigation calculations, Adaptation baselines and all expected targets will be embedded in the software.  
During the Due Diligence (DD) process, GAWA Capital conducts a detailed environmental due diligence with the 
objective of assessing (i) investee environmental adaptation using a tool called Green Index which is part of the 
Cerise SPI4 tool and (ii) transformation potential of the investee to better serve/include SMEs and the smallholder 
farmers. All the strengths, weaknesses and areas identified for improvement will be included in the Environmental 
and Social Due Diligence report, which will serve as an initial input for the TA Steering Committee to explore 
potential TA interventions that could be implemented in agreement with the investee’s management. 
Legal documents will include social covenants (e.g. implementation/further development of environmental 
policies; etc.) as well as key impact-related goals (e.g. new SME or smallholder farmers clients to be reached 
with green lending, new products to be launched, etc) which the investees commit to achieving during the life of 
the investments. In addition, goals will be set that the investment needs to achieve. These goals will be linked to 
the calculation of carried interest for the General Partner. Goals will refer to the number of SMEs or smallholder 
farmers to be reached and to transformative metrics both referring to green adaptation of the investee and to 
SME/smallholder farmers adaptation of the investee. 
The monitoring system follows the evolution of the environmental and social metrics and of the implementation 
of agreed TA projects, revised quarterly. The investee will need to fill out a social monitoring form and there will 
be a monitoring call with management to understand recent developments of TA projects. The Fund will also 
monitor the compliance with environmental and social covenants included in the investment agreements. The 
result is reported quarterly to investors. 
When generating pipeline, GAWA always seeks Investees with the potential of servicing the SME and the 
smallholder farmers segment and with willingness to become “greener”: opportunities in potential investees 
which target and/or plan to expand their operations in the SME and smallholder farmers sectors and which are 
committed to improve their environmental practices.

F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT 
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)  
Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), sanctions, 
prohibited practices, and other risks that might prevent the project/programme objectives from being achieved. Also describe the 
proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.  
For probability: High has significant probability, Medium has moderate probability, Low has negligible probability 
For impact: High has significant impact, Medium has moderate impact, Low has negligible impact 
Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as well as 
fraudulent, coercive, collusive, and obstructive practices 
Selected Risk Factor 1 
 
Category 
Probability 
Impact 
Legal 
Medium 
Medium 
Description 
 
Mitigation Measure(s) 
The top-down due diligence approach starts with country selection. Kuali Fund-GCF will select target 
countries analyzing the political, macroeconomic and social situation and the risk of political intervention in 
the financial system. 
 
Political intervention in the financial sector can bring new regulation that may hinder the development 
of financial inclusion and, more specifically, the SMEs and the smallholder farmers finance. Before 
investing in any country, GAWA Capital conducts a detailed analysis and due diligence of the political, 
economic and regulatory risks and screens out countries that are politically unstable, prone to transfer 
and convertibility risk. Part of the analytical review entails: (i) discussions with sovereign rating analysts, 
(ii) meetings with multilateral representatives, (iii) reviewing country reports and (iv) discussions with 
embassy officials and other relevant third parties (including government bodies). Emerging economies 
are more exposed to volatility of macroeconomic events such as recession, inflation or government-
related defaults which, as a result, can negatively affect the financial and agricultural sectors. Commodity 
prices can also strongly impact the economy and further exacerbate currency volatility. This risk is 
mitigated in two ways: 
• 
Top-down investment approach, picking countries which are less likely to suffer macroeconomic 
events and/or are more resilient to shocks. Extensive macroeconomic/political analysis is carried 
out before making an investment. 
• 
Picking companies that have the financial strength to be resilient to a potential shock. For 
example, companies that are well capitalized and have demonstrated the ability to raise capital 
in difficult times (e.g. owned by shareholders that can inject more capital if needed) or which are 
profitable and use these profits to strengthen their capital base. 
 
Concerning foreign exchange risk, thanks to its partnership with hedging providers, Kuali Fund-GCF 
can lend in local currency and hedge to EUR for all debt investments. In equity investments, where 
hedging is not an option given the lack of a clear exit date, we invest in local currency after careful 
consideration of the historical performance of the currency against the EUR and after a thorough analysis 
of the currency perspective. 
 
Another risk has been identified regarding possible economic turndowns due to global fluctuations or 
specific crisis like COVID-19. At a lower level, credit risk would be also related to specific risks linked to 
the SMEs and the smallholder farmers sectors or even the credit/financial intermediary risk.  
Political instability (Transfer restriction, expropriation, currency convertibility, war and civil 
disturbance, economic turndowns).

Finally, a thorough scoring model has been developed, providing the maximum percentage of Kuali Fund-

regulatory and market indicators.

Mitigation Measure(s) 
Kuali Fund-GCF will seek investments with a low risk profile activity in order to create a portfolio with an

accredited entity in developing markets. There will be also a diversification policy in the portfolio investments 
in order to mitigate this risk, that will be also lowered by the TA facility (with a thorough selection process 
and the Fund Manager expertise) in the SMEs and smallholder farmers’ adaptation process. 
Credit risk is extensively analyzed during the on-site due diligence. In addition to this, there will be a quarterly 
monitoring review where credit risk parameters are closely followed and a thorough analysis is carried out if 
portfolio quality worsens or any covenant is broken. If the situation persists, an additional on-site due 
diligence may be arranged to visit affected branches and assess the situation.

Mitigation Measure(s) 
No activities will be undertaken in any UNSC sanctions regimes. 
No individual or entity that is listed on any UNSC sanctions list will be involved in any manner with the project 
or its activities, either as a counterpart, implementer or beneficiary. 
There is no intention to distribute or disburse to beneficiaries, either directly or indirectly, cash, vouchers, 
commodities or other items of value. 
An integrity channel has been set up to enabling the report of complaints and allegations of impropriety, 
wrong-doing or other related issues to the projects or its activities. The channel is publicly available (both in 
Spanish and English) in the following link: https://www.cofides.es/integridad. 
Regarding jurisdictions that are listed by the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) such as Panama, a 
reinforced due diligence will be carried out, in line with the requirements of COFIDES’ AML/CFT procedure. 
The specific activities declared by the potential investee and the identification of the holder will be closely 
looked at.

Mitigation Measure(s) 
The due diligence will include a social and an environmental risk analysis that will cover SMEs and 
smallholder farmers specific risks (i.e. Client Protection Principles, financial consumers’ rights) including 
special needs of vulnerable groups such as indigenous peoples if any risk or harm is estimated to be possible 
(see more details in Annex 6). Regarding the implementation risk, an adequate and thorough selection 
process together with the previous experience of the senior managers would mitigate the potential lack of 
experience in managing direct operations with Fintech companies. 
The operational and fraud risk is mitigated via field audits performed during the on-site due diligences, 
analysis of investees’ full operating manuals, in addition to random checks.  
For corruption related risk, there will be background checks on the key individuals of the FSP and 
innovative companies, including the OFAC list, analyzing the existing shareholders and their affiliations and 
monitoring information from Transparency International and other corruption-focused information providers. 
Regarding the asset-liability management, it will be mitigated by an initial ALM analysis and the 
correspondent monitoring of the investee’s liquidity position. 
Another risk in technology and innovation of Fintech has been identified, which will be mitigated by 
the support on technology use, both in the field and in the head offices, recommending potential technology 
vendors if needed. Kuali Fund-GCF will benefit from the Technical Assistance facility, that will act as a strong 
mitigant of some of these risks. TA will be used to help investees in improving aspects such as technology 
adoption or product improvement. 
Finally, as for the business cycle of the investees, it is important for FSPs to adjust their sourcing 
requirements to the cash model of their loan products’ cycles. In the same line, it is essential to adapt their 
loan products to the business cycle of their clients’ productive activities (i.e. longer tenor for agricultural 
loans, amortization consistent with harvests). The Fund Manager analyses the cash flow models of 
invested FSPs and may advise the management in achieving the optimal funding structure.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
RISK CATEGORY 
Kuali Fund-GCF (and Kuali Fund) is categorized as I-2, though investments mostly will be rated as I-3. The 
Funded Activity is not intended to finance or invest in FSP/ innovative companies with exposure to high 
Environmental and Social (E&S) risk sectors according to IFC standards. FSP/ Innovative Companies are 
expected to trigger Green Climate Fund E&S Standards in their Environmental and Management Systems 
(ESMS). GAWA will support this through capacity building (by means of its Technical Assistance (TA) 
facility). As for Innovative Companies (IC), the risk associated is C. 
ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT INSTRUMENTS 
GAWA has developed an operational ESMS according to the Green Climate Fund E&S Standards (See 
Annex 6). It is integrated into Kuali Fund-GCF’s own investment process and is focused on the E&S 
assessment of the FSPs/ Innovative Companies with a risk-based approach. In this regard, the E&S due 
diligence and monitoring of the clients will be based on the project life cycle and will comprise the following 
stages: 
STEP 1: SCREENING AND CATEGORIZATION 
Kuali Fund-GCF Portfolio and the expected project activities will be screened by the investment analyst 
against IFC exclusion list to ensure no activity on this list will be financed. 
The E&S Officer will carry out the E&S risk categorization according to the ICF Categorization List. 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s investments in FSP will normally fall into the I-3 category (low risk) and the I-2 category 
(medium risk). As for Innovative Companies (IC), the risk associated is C or B, rated according GCF Risk 
Categorization Ratings (being A -high, B-medium; C-low) for direct investments.  Potential investments that 
fall on the A category for ICs or I-1 category for FSPs will be screened out and will not be considered for 
further analysis and investment. 
The Investment Officer will include in the Preliminary Investment Memorandum the conformance with the 
exclusion list and the E&S Officer will include the key E&S issues, the proposed investee E&S risk category, 
the proposed scope of the ESDD and any anticipated E&S requirements. 
STEP 2: E&S and SEAH DUE DILIGENCE (ESDD) 
During the due diligence, Kuali Fund-GCF’s team will gain an insight into the nature and scope of the FSPs/ 
innovative companies’ operations. An E&S performance due diligence is conducted in parallel with the 
business due diligence process when evaluating a potential investee.  
An E&S due diligence and an on-site visit is conducted in parallel with the business due diligence process 
when evaluating a potential investee. This ESDD shall be carried out by the investment team in collaboration 
the E&S Officer.  
The level of ESDD actions and requirements depend on the E&S risk of a potential FSP/ innovative 
companies. In all cases, the management of the E&S risks -and the E&S risks associated to its investments- 
of every FSP/innovative company will be evaluated. According to the E&S level of risk, an E&S Action Plan 
(ESAP) will be established in order to strengthen the ESMS of the FSP/ Innovative Company and it will have

=== PAGE 105 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 101 
 
 
 
 
to be accomplished along the loan tenure. In this stage, the TA could be used to address the ESAP(please 
refer to Annex 6). 
A particular case within social risks is SEAH risk management. The ESDD will include an assessment of the 
situation and the need to include actions and requirements (from training, zero-tolerance of SEAH 
Guideline’s code of conduct, policy and management system and resolution of incidents, GRM, among 
others) in a survivor-centered and gender-responsive manner. The objective will be avoided, and where 
avoidance is impossible, mitigate the risks of SEAH to people impacted by Kuali Fund-GCF. 
Despite being a very novel risk issue during due diligences, the SEAH risk forecasted for the project rated 
low-medium risk based in the AE experience in microfinance and SMEs investments (according to UN criteria 
-UN Protocol on Allegations of Sexual Exploitation and abuse involving IP, 2018- for considering SEAH high-
risk activities the Funded Activity will not take place in high risk environments such as camps and shelters 
and involve nor the Funded Activity management company neither the FSP and ICs having direct contact 
with children. The aspect to be considered as relevant is the fact that the subprojects (FSP and IC) will take 
place in countries where sexual and gender-based violence is prevalent, therefore medium rate is present). 
It is required that every single subproject/investee company (FSP and IC) has a SEAH risk diagnosis and a 
SEAH plan accordingly to the satisfaction of AE and based on the SEAH risk assessment tool developed by 
the GCF’s Secretariat. 
SEAH risk management is far from being systematized and understood in the corporate world. This implies 
that for the management of SEAH risks in this program, the first measure that will necessarily have to be 
established is a specific staff training plan with a top-down flow (Kuali Fund-GCF to FSPs/Fintech 
companies/investors). It will cover: 
1. 
The fund management company will (i) acquire the necessary knowledge and skills in this area, 
including general aspects and country specificities (ii) adopt an explicit commitment to combat the 
SEAH and in favour of zero-tolerance of SEAH, and it has to be reflected in a code of conduct and a 
management system. 
2. 
The E&S officer will include in the contracts with the underlying entities, clauses of commitment to 
Kuali Fund-GCF Zero-Tolerance policy and the implementation of an ad-hoc management system, 
which may be the same as that of Kuali Find for the better centralization of data and its subsequent 
evaluation or based in the GCF SEAH risk assessment tool. 
3. 
Within this contractual framework, the underlying companies of Kuali Fund-GCF shall undertake to 
receive training in the field of SEAH, to increase their capacities transversally in the organization and 
to appoint a person responsible for making that commitment materialize (an E&S officer or a Human 
Resources Officer). 
4. 
The EA will be reviewing and monitoring all these acts: Kuali Fund-GCF's commitment and capacity; 
incorporation of clauses in contracts with underlying companies; training and assignment of 
managers in the underlying companies; aggregate and integrated monitoring by the Funded Activity 
of all these issues in an annual report that will reflect actions taken, incidents, resolution of the same 
and lessons learned. 
The management system will not only be preventive to avoid SEAH but inclusive survivor-centered and 
gender responsive as well.  
Additionally, the GRM of each underlying entity will be adopted not only for E&S claims but also to receive 
SEAH complaints in an anonymous way.  Kuali Fund-GCF will be informed quarterly about SEAH incidents 
and actions taken. This information will be processed and included in the annual E&S report to the AE. 
Besides, the AE will also be available to receive and process SEAH complaints in its Program mailbox. 
Those complaints will be evaluated by the AE Sustainability and Impact Division.

=== PAGE 106 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 102 
 
 
 
 
Kuali Fund will use several tools/guidelines to support this process: IFC Performance Standards, IFC EHS 
Guidelines, Alinus/SPI4 with Green Index module and Global Map of Supply Chain Risks in Agro-Commodity 
Production, UN SEAH Policies and Protocols (UN Protocol on Allegations of Sexual Exploitation and abuse 
involving IP), among others.  
The result of the ESDD will be documented in an ESDD Report which will include all the strengths and 
weaknesses detected in the FSP/innovative company, in addition to the areas identified that could be 
improved with TA interventions. 
STEP 3: E&S Conditions of Investments 
The Investment officer and director of the Fund Manager will negotiate and agree with the FSP/ innovative 
company the E&S provisions and investment conditions in the term sheet and investment agreement. These 
consist of standard E&S terms applicable to all Fund investments, and specific conditions identified during 
the ESDD of a proposed investment (ESAP with SEAH requirements will be included in the legal 
documentation).  
The Funded Activity’s Fund Manager should ensure that any conditions of investment tied to disbursement 
of funds, as specified in the investment agreement, are met before disbursing funds. 
STEP 4: TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE 
The ESDD will help identify potential TA projects with regard to the green readiness, E&S risks management 
and gender approach integration of FSPs and Innovative companies. 
Examples of Green Readiness projects: 
- Implementation of green products directed to the MSME or smallholder farmers such as loans to finance 
more efficient new machinery or equipment or climate smart agriculture loans. 
-Training people on climate finance, including product specialists, risk officers, loan officers and man. For 
more details about the Technical Assistance Facility and its use please refer to the Kuali Fund TA Operations 
Manual. 
STEP 5: MONITORING AND REPORTING 
The Investment officer and E&S Officer should actively supervise and monitor compliance with the E&S 
covenants included in the investment agreements and the E&S performance of each of its portfolio 
investments. For this purpose, FSPs/ innovative companies will fill out an E&S Performance Report on a 
yearly basis. It will include at least the following: 
• 
Portfolio breakdown by industry sectors, E&S risk category and product lines, and sample ESDD 
reports carried out by I-1 investees. 
• 
Cases of non-compliance with E&S requirements 
• 
Serious E&S accidents or incidents related to a transaction, including SEAH information. 
• 
Information on the implementation of any changes to the E&S Management System. 
 
Besides, Kuali Fund-GCF will prepare a E&S monitoring report for all investors on an annual basis with 
aggregated information of the FSPs/ innovative companies: 
• 
E&S risk category of each investee.

G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 103 
 
 
 
 
• 
Major E&S performance related activities per investee.  
• 
Evolution of E&S covenants and E&S Action Plan, if applicable. 
• 
Evolution of the Green Impact Score (GAWA customized metric that measures social impact and 
green readiness based on SPI4 and other indicators) of each investee and on an aggregated basis. 
• 
Evolution of SEAH measures, incidents, and their solution 
• 
Status of implementation of Technical Assistance projects by each investee and underlying 
investments, if applicable. 
GRIEVANCE MECHANISM 
The grievance mechanism ensures the right to be heard for complainants who feel affected by Kuali Fund-
GCF’s financed operations in order to enable resolution of disputes and assist the Funded Activity in drawing 
lessons learned for current and future operations. Any person or group may lodge a complaint and it can be 
submitted in any language by letter, e-mail and/or web form (e-mail and web to be determined). Kuali Fund-
GCF will require all investees to have a GRM in place in which they compile all complaints and follow up 
their resolution.  
DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION 
After the ESDD of a potential investee and before the financial legal agreement is signed, the E&S Officer 
will disclose an “Executive Summary E&S Assessment” (includes the E&S category of the investee and a 
brief description of the identified E&S risks and mitigation measures to be implemented) in the Funded 
Activity’s webpage according to the requirements of GCF’s Disclosure Policy. 
CAPACITY IN TERMS OF HUMAN RESOURCES 
Senior Management 
The CEOs of GAWA Capital as representatives from the upper management are responsible for Kuali Fund-
GCF’s overall commitment to E&S objectives during the whole investment process. In particular, the CEOs 
establish the Funded Activity’s E&S requirements and conditions for investees, as defined herein. In cases 
of unresolved E&S issues or non-compliance associated with a transaction that cannot be resolved by the 
Investment Officers, Senior Management determines the appropriate course of action to follow to reduce the 
financial institution’s potential exposure to E&S risk, which may include taking legal action against the 
client/investee. 
E&S Officer 
The Technical Assistance Manager of GAWA Capital will act as the E&S Officer of the Kuali Fund-GCF. The 
E&S Officer is responsible for developing and implementing the ESMS as well as for communicating with 
senior management on E&S issues and concerns. 
The E&S Officer is responsible for developing and updating the procedures and documents that are part of 
Kuali Fund-GCF ESMS. This person also evaluates the E&S risks at the portfolio level and provides 
assistance to Investment Officers in evaluating and monitoring the E&S performance of clients/investees. 
The E&S Officer in coordination with the Gender Officer will be involved in the solution of all E&S claims 
(including SEAH) received through the Funded Activity’s GRM. 
The E&S Officer will also lead the communication with the Members of the Technical Assistance Committee 
and, when necessary, with the management of the TA implementation partners.

=== PAGE 108 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 104 
 
 
 
 
The E&S Officer will receive annual training of E&S issues, including SEAH in order to keep up to date and 
will integrate and share the updated knowledge with the investees. Given the multi-country nature of the 
programme, the training will need to collate cross-cutting material subjects with countries E&S specificities. 
Investment Officer   
The Investment Officer is responsible for following the procedures of the ESMS at the transaction level. They 
discuss and negotiate possible E&S mitigation measures with the client/investee. 
The Investment Officer is also responsible for evaluating the E&S risks at the level of individual transactions 
and make a recommendation to the Credit/Investment Committee on whether to proceed with a transaction. 
The Investment Officer ensures that the Funded Activity’s E&S requirements are incorporated in legal 
agreements for each transaction. The Investment Officer may advise if a client’s/investee’s non-compliance 
with E&S clauses constitutes a breach of contract and is considered an Event of Default under the terms of 
the legal agreement that requires follow up by Senior Management. 
Investment Committee   
The Investment Committee is responsible for deciding if E&S risks are acceptable to Kuali Fund-GCF overall 
exposure to risk before proceeding with a transaction. 
G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
Kuali Fund-GCF aims at improving access to financial services with a special focus to support women 
entrepreneurs. In addition, specific gender interventions through TA funding will enable for more empowered 
women participation. The gender assessment is built on quantitative data and qualitative evidence derived 
from a desk review of existing literature and secondary databases. It includes general data about women in 
LAC as well as country-specific ratings and information.  
Target countries have made substantial progress in recent decades, particularly in the topics of access to 
education and health services and have a solid legal framework. However, social norms limit the practical 
impact of the legally recognized rights of women. Gender gaps are still large in areas such as access to 
employment, entrepreneurship, representation in informal work, assets’ property and control, access to finance 
and distribution of household and caring tasks. Gender-based violence continues being a major social issue in 
all countries. An external consultant will be hired to carry out country assessments. 
Women in LAC has a strong history of entrepreneurial activity as the female/male led TEA ratio shows: 0.84, 
the highest in the world. Like other regions, women entrepreneurs are often driven more by necessity than in 
the case of men. For this reason, supporting them via financing and other mechanisms gains further 
importance. On top of that, over 60% of women are stuck in the informal sector, lacking access to financial, 
human and social capital, key constraints for the growth of their business. These constraints are exacerbated 
by weaker levels of financial literacy and lower legal ownership of assets or guarantees. However, women 
have shown to be responsible and loyal financial clients with higher aversion toward indebtedness than men. 
 
Regarding agriculture, a high proportion of rural women employed in this sector are also unpaid family workers 
which puts them at a disadvantage. Gender gaps accentuate the vulnerability of women to the impacts of 
climate change, limit their adaptation capacity and reduced their potential contribution to reduce emissions of 
greenhouse gases.

=== PAGE 109 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 105 
 
 
 
 
The project will mainstream gender equality and women’s empowerment at the Funded Activity level, the FSPs 
and Innovative Companies’ level and their portfolio. Women’s local organizations will be identified in each 
country and consultations with them will be conducted to incorporate their knowledge and views in the 
implementation of the project. Institutions and initiatives to address gender-based violence will also be 
identified in the target countries and dissemination of information on gendered violence as well as resources 
available in each country will be included. A gender policy will be required in the FSPs and innovative 
companies for both internal and external dimension. A gender approach management system has been 
integrated in the ESMS to evaluate gender issues and implement an action plan. To ensure that gender equality 
is addressed, specific covenants will be introduced in loan agreements.  
 
Tracked indicators include, among others, the number of trainings about business skills and financial literacy 
and % of participation in capacity building activities (see Gender Action Plan for further information). 
 
Regarding human resources, a Gender Officer who will receive periodic training will monitor the investee’s 
compliance with the gender action plan and will be responsible for developing and implementing the gender 
management system. An E&S officer with expertise in gender will also be hired and work in coordination with 
the gender officer. In addition, gender related issues will be part of the calculation of an environmental and 
social performance score which will be linked directly to carried interest of the fund managers – if objectives 
are not achieved, the fund manager will earn a lower performance fee. Annual report will include information 
on the implementation of the Gender Action Plan. 
 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
Kuali Fund-GCF will apply best practices in its financial management and procurement practices. The Funded 
Activity will benefit from the extensive experience in fund management of the Fund’s Manager, GAWA Capital 
Partners SGEIC S.A., in addition to the support of external service providers such as a Fund Administrator, 
Custodian, Auditors, Legal and Tax advisors, amongst others. The Fund’s Administrator will be in charge of 
book-keeping and preparation of the quarterly NAV and Annual Accounts of Kuali Fund, supported by 
documentation from the Fund Manager. The Funded Activity will undergo annual external audits, to be realized 
by a Big-Four company, again supported with documentation and evidence from the Administrator and the 
Fund Manager. 
A detailed Financial Model enables the Fund Manager to oversee all operational and investment cashflows to 
ensure Kuali Fund-GCF’s adequate financial management. The Funded Activity’s Financial Model will include 
the consistent logging of cashflows in line with real bank cash movements and balances, whilst projecting the 
future cash inflows/outflows, thus enabling the Funded Activity to maximize return of capital/dividend payments 
to investors whilst ensuring the timely payment of all service providers. 
Disbursements will be carried out on an investment-by-investment basis, involving a Capital Call procedure 
following the IC approval of each investment. Capital Call notifications are sent out to investors by the 
Administrator and funds enter the Kuali Fund’s Investor Account from each investor, to be then transferred in 
their totality to Kuali Fund’s Transaction Account (as per industry best practices) for disbursement. Prior to any 
disbursements, both Kuali Fund’s Administrator and its Custodian must have received all pertinent 
documentation backing the transaction. In the case of debt financing, a disbursement schedule will be 
established. In the case of capital subscription, a Commitment Agreement for every investor will be made and 
this will be accompanied by the corresponding request for resources through a capital call93. Commitments 
from Component I (loans and equity for investments) and commitments from Component II (grants for TA) will 
be separate and there will be therefore different calls for either financing capital and TA capital. 
 
93 As long as COFIDES will be a limited partner, COFIDES will not provide any no objection to the disbursements. COFIDES 
will ensure that the process will comply with the legal documentation that will be established the eligibility criteria and the 
rights granted to investors.

=== PAGE 110 ===
G 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 106 
 
 
 
 
To ensure the financial sustainability of the Funded Activity’s investments, close monitoring of each investee 
is carried out by the Fund Manager’s investment team and CRO. Monitoring is typically carried out on a 
quarterly basis, involving the review of a detailed Reporting Factsheet to be filled out by investees, Financial 
Statements and the holding of quarterly monitoring calls with the management of the investee in addition to 
relevant third parties. Country risk is managed on-the-go, with the team keeping actively informed of political, 
macroeconomic and sectoral developments to ensure the sustainability of the investee’s operating 
environment. Monitoring is up-stepped to a higher frequency where relevant (for example, in COVID-19 
situation, Fund Manager’s monitoring of current investees has been heightened). 
The procurement of the different service providers necessary for the correct functioning of the Funded Activity’s 
operations (e.g. Administrator, Custodian, Auditor, Insurance, Technical Assistance partners such as 
Consultants, etc.) will entail the hiring of renowned and trusted specialists in each field, which will be selected 
following thorough market research and the evaluation of the most appropriate provider for each task. This 
evaluation is based on cost, adequacy and scope of services provided in each provider’s proposal. In the case 
of partners (such as external consultants) selected for the implementation of the Technical Assistance Facility, 
the process will involve a tender procedure, with decision-making in the power of the Technical Assistance 
Steering Committee during at least the first year of Kuali Fund-GCF’s life. COFIDES’s approved Procurement 
and Consultant Guidelines reviewed and accepted by the Fund will apply under these grants. Procurement will 
be carried out by Grant Recipients themselves and procurement plan will be agreed with the Grant Recipient. 
COFIDES will carry out constant monitoring of the project. COFIDES will elaborate monitoring reports for the 
Spanish Cooperation through AECID and progress reports for the EUR and also for the GCF to ensure the 
proper use of its resources. GAWA will conduct frequent on-site missions during the investment period and 
also during the monitoring phase and will report to COFIDES and maintain constant communication between 
COFIDES and the management team. COFIDES will also receive information from each Investment Committee 
of the Funded Activity. Likewise, Kuali Fund will have independent evaluations and annual audits. It will also 
have an ESG auditor to assess the portfolio's environmental and social performance. 
Finally, thanks to the joint experience of GAWA with COFIDES in GFIF Fund and Huruma Fund (where 
resources from COFIDES, FONPRODE, and the EU through COFIDES are invested), a highly developed 
knowledge of the procedures of these types of programs has been achieved. In particular, COFIDES' 
experience in reporting and compliance with EU regulations is also essential for the proper use of GCF 
resources. 
G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal  
Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for 
all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme 
funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the 
redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of 
the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, 
segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.  
Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information. 
☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be 
disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence. 
☒ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not 
be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity 
is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes: 
• 
full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an 
explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and
