=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 128 
 
 
 
 
Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP230 
Proposal name: 
Kuali Fund-GCF 
Accredited entity: 
Compañia Española de Financiación del Desarrollo (COFIDES) 
Country/(ies): 
Brazil, Colombia, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, 
Panama 
Project/programme size: 
Medium 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Overview 
1. 
Latin America is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its exposure to acute climate 
events, chronic climate risks and limited adaptation and mitigation capacity.1 High exposure to 
climate events and limited adaptation and mitigation capacity make some countries especially 
vulnerable. Adaptation investments are insufficient partially due to increased public debt levels 
and a weak domestic revenue base.  
2. 
Agriculture is an important economic sector and source of employment in Latin 
America, especially in rural areas. The agriculture sector’s structure and scale vary significantly 
between countries in this region.2 Countries with an extensive agricultural industry sensitive to 
climate change are especially vulnerable. South American states have a significant agricultural 
sector, which has been hit hard, especially by severe droughts. Climate change damages farm 
assets such as livestock shelters, and farmers incur losses related to lower crop yields. 
Smallholder farmers are more affected and exposed to climate risks as they lack the instruments 
and know-how to increase their resilience. 
3. 
The region’s financial service providers (FSPs) are indirectly exposed to climate change 
since a significant proportion of the lending portfolio is in sectors exposed to climate risks, for 
example the agricultural sector.3 At the same time, FSPs are crucial for shifting investments 
towards increasing climate resilience and greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation activities. 
4. 
Micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises constitute the majority of enterprises and 
generate around 60 per cent of formal productive employment in “the Americas”. The 
Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) has identified that Latin 
American small and medium-sized companies have a higher productivity gap than their peers in 
other world regions.4 Like smallholder farmers, small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) 
have less ability than large companies to deal with the effects of climate change due to limited 
resources and know-how. 
5. 
The programme realizes investments and provides technical assistance (TA) in seven 
countries: Brazil, Colombia, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico and Panama. The 
 
1 European Investment Bank (EIB), 2023, Climate Risks for Latin America and the Caribbean. 
2 Inter-American Development Bank, 2021, Landscaping the Agritech Ecosystem for Smallholder Farmers in Latin 
America and the Caribbean. 
3 EIB, 2023, Climate Risks for Latin America and the Caribbean. 
4 OECD, 2019, Latin America and the Caribbean 2019: Policies for Competitive SMEs in the Pacific Alliance and 
Participating South American Countries.

=== PAGE 129 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 129 
 
 
 
 
programme (Kuali Fund) may also undertake activities in other countries of the region, as well 
as India, but this is outside the scope of the funded activities.5 The funded activity is thus named 
“Kuali Fund-GCF”. This assessment is therefore of the Kuali Fund-GCF. 
6. 
Kuali Fund-GCF shall increase the adoption of low-carbon technologies and improve the 
resilience to climate change of involved countries by supporting small financial service 
providers to green their portfolios and finance investments of smallholder farmers and SMEs in 
activities which increase climate resilience and reduce GHG emissions. The fund will also 
directly support companies which provide climate solutions with the aim of growing climate-
relevant products and services available to farmers, which could then in turn be financed 
through FSPs. 
7. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will fund an expected 13 FSPs to build a “climate portfolio”. More than 
90 per cent of the fund shall be used for this purpose, while the remaining part is to be used to 
invest in innovative companies, as indicated in the response to question 13 raised by the 
independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) to the accredited entity (AE). FSPs shall provide 
loans to SMEs (around 20 per cent of the total fund) and farmers (about 80 per cent of the fund). 
Loans to SMEs provided through FSPs are expected to be, on average, EUR 18,000 for a tenor of 
two years to finance assets or activities such as solar rooftop panels, high-efficiency heat pumps, 
insulation, low-carbon transport, energy-efficient machinery, etc. Loans to smallholder farmers 
given through FSPs are expected to average EUR 2,100 with a one-year tenor for supporting 
activities such as tree plantation, composting, farmland restoration, farm irrigation systems, etc. 
Debt investments in FSPs will usually have a maturity of two to three years and are renewable 
for the same amount of time. All debt investments of the Kuali Fund-GCF will be in local 
currency, with offsetting of foreign exchange risks through swaps. The fund has targeted returns 
from debt investments in FSPs of 4–7 per cent in euros after the cost of hedging. 
8. 
The primary purpose of this programme is to green small financial service providers. A 
successful implementation of this approach allows for a transformational change and an influx 
of private capital into climate investments. The progression of FSPs will be measured with the 
Green Index 3.0,6 a framework to assess the performance of FSPs in green-inclusive finance. 
FSPs shall grade their performance using this index over time due to the investments made by 
the Kuali Fund-GCF. FSPs will start to offer their clients financial and non-financial green 
products. The Kuali Fund-GCF allows FSPs a ramp-up period to build their climate portfolio with 
an initial loan to FSPs for two to three years and a second loan over the same period and 
intermediate monitoring of the climate portfolio. The Kuali Fund-GCF will require the FSPs to 
build 50 per cent of the climate portfolio by the end of the third year and 100 per cent by the 
end of the following three years.  
9. 
Kuali Fund-GCF will also invest around 10 per cent of its resources in agritechnology 
and climate technology companies with innovative solutions in mitigation (e.g. rural solar 
energy providers, biodigester manufacturer companies, electric vehicle charging networks) and 
in adaptation (e.g. farm-to-business mobility supply chains, smart farming), with an average 
ticket size of EUR 4 million using primarily equity or quasi-equity investments basically with a 
tenor of six years, on average. Two investments are expected for the Kuali Fund-GCF. The fund 
has target returns of 15–20 per cent per annum for equity investment projects in innovative 
companies. 
10. 
Around 50 per cent of investments are expected in adaptation and resilience-focused 
technologies and 50 per cent in mitigation technologies. 
 
5 No-objection letters were not received by the accredited entity for other targeted countries. The total Kuali Fund 
aims to achieve a volume of EUR 300 million working in 14 countries of Latin America and the Caribbean and in 
India.  
6 See: The Green Index: An innovative tool to assess green inclusive finance performance of FSPs | e-MFP

=== PAGE 130 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 130 
 
 
 
 
11. 
GAWA Capital Partners, a private limited liability firm specializing in impact investment 
management services, registered as an alternative investment fund manager and supervised by 
the Spanish Exchange Commission, will act as a fund and TA fund manager and is the executing 
entity (EE).7 GAWA Capital has 14 years of experience and EUR 220 million in assets under 
management across four funds investing in various Asian and Latin American and Asian 
countries in microfinance, SMEs and agriculture. The Kuali Fund-GCF will have an investment 
advisory committee comprised of large investors, but with a purely advisory role, and a 
strategic advisory group to make recommendations to the fund manager of a strictly advisory 
nature.  
12. 
The EUR 128.2 million project has a financing source of (i) EUR 25 million from the GCF 
as equity with a tenor of 10 years and an equity return of 3.9 per cent; (ii) A GCF grant of EUR 
7.8 million for TA and monitoring; (iii) EUR 1.26 million from the European Union as equity 
with the same conditions as GCF; (iv) EUR 17.6 million from the Spanish Agency for 
International Development Cooperation (AECID) as senior loans with a tenor of 10 years and a 
3.75 per cent cap; (v) EUR 1.8 million from the Kuali Fund, indirectly from AECID, as a 
subordinated loan with the same conditions as the AECID direct loan; (vi) EUR 1 million in 
senior equity from GAWA Capital; (vii) EUR 72.8 million in private investor senior equity at 10 
years with 6.5 per cent interest; (viii) EUR 0.57 million in grants from the European Union; and 
(ix) EUR 0.3 million in grants from AECID and EUR 0.04 million in grants from Kuali Fund, 
indirectly from AECID. The GCF and European Union contributions to the first-loss tranche (EUR 
26.26 million) will help mobilize private investors’ participation and reduce their perceived risk 
of the SME and smallholder farmer sectors. It is expected that Compañia Española de 
Financiación del Desarrollo (COFIDES) and GAWA Capital will invest in the programme to align 
their interests and perceived risks. 
1.2 
Impact potential  
 
 
 
 
 
  Scale: Medium 
13. 
The programme claims an adaptation impact of 242,000 direct and 642,000 indirect 
beneficiaries (0.28 per cent of the total population). The GHG mitigation impact is estimated at 
2.71 million tonnes of carbon dioxide (t CO2) over the lifespan of the technologies and 
investments. 
14. 
Direct beneficiaries are smallholder farmers and SMEs which receive a loan from FSPs. 
Indirect beneficiaries include the household members of the beneficiary. The calculation of 
beneficiaries is based on (i) the number of loans extended by FSPs to SMEs and farmers, 
assuming 100 per cent of loans are relevant to climate resilience; (ii) an average loan size per 
group, and (iii) an average tenor per group with a fund investment period of six years. 
Beneficiary numbers will be lower (higher) if the average loan size is higher (lower), the tenor is 
longer (shorter), and the share of climate-relevant loans is lower.  
15. 
Beneficiary numbers are considered to be overstated in the original funding proposal 
for the following reasons: (i) Investments in SMEs are for mitigation activities, such as 
insulation or solar hot water with no direct resilience impact. The beneficiary number 
calculated in this area is thus deemed incorrect. (ii) It is assumed that 100 per cent of loans are 
given for climate investments, while in the funding proposal (page 35), it is stated that the 
climate portfolio of FSPs will be 50 per cent by the end of year three and 100 per cent by the end 
of year six. Thus, on average, only 50 per cent of loans would be given with a direct climate 
impact. Therefore, beneficiary numbers of farmer-related loans need to be divided by two to 
account for the gradual portfolio build-up of climate investments.  
16. 
The mitigation benefit is calculated based on 50 per cent of the investment targeted 
towards mitigation and an estimated average cost per t CO2 avoided based on a calculation of 
 
7 Compañia Española de Financiación del Desarrollo (COFIDES) is also an EE with an amount of EUR 0.4 million.

=== PAGE 131 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 131 
 
 
 
 
total CO2 mitigated and total investments made. Calculations are based on typical investment 
projects, differentiating between types of projects and sectors (buildings/industrial, energy 
generation and access, transport, and forestry and land use) and between investments from 
FSPs to SMEs and from FSPs to farmers, as well as investments in climate technology 
companies. Approx. 83 per cent of mitigated CO2 is based on forestry and land-use change (50 
per cent from tree plantations, albeit having less than 5 per cent of investments), followed by 14 
per cent for buildings and industrial appliances, 2 per cent for energy generation and less than 1 
per cent for transport. Around 60 per cent of the calculated mitigation impact is due to 
investments in innovative firms, and 40 per cent is due to loans from FSPs. 
17. 
The mitigation benefit is considered to be overstated in the original funding proposal 
due to calculations being based on the total Kuali Fund, which has a different distribution of 
investments, with a higher share going to innovative firms with a higher GHG impact per 
invested capital compared to loans channelled through FSPs. The total Kuali Fund invests 24 per 
cent of the total fund in innovation companies, while the Kuali Fund-GCF invests less than 10 
per cent. The mitigation numbers of the Kuali Fund-GCF are lower than initially estimated based 
on a 10 per cent share of investments in the Kuali Fund-GCF 8 going to innovative firms and 90 
per cent being channelled through FSPs.  
18. 
The impact calculations for energy generation follow a standard methodological 
approach using the grid factor per country based on the methodology and dataset as published 
by the UNFCCC / International Financial Institutions. For buildings and energy generation, an 
assumption is made that 20 per cent (e.g. for insulation or industrial appliances) to 60 per cent 
(e.g. for light-emitting diodes (LEDs)) of electric energy can be saved. It also assumes a capacity 
factor of 10 hours per calendar day, or 40 per cent, which means, on average, double shifts. 
These assumptions are not deemed as conservative. However, the impact of these assumptions 
on the total projected GHG mitigation impact is minor, as the share of energy generation in the 
total mitigation impact is only 14 per cent. 
19. 
The impact calculations in the transport sector are overly optimistic and overstate 
emission reductions for this sector due to (i) usage of assumptions concerning efficiency 
improvements,9 which are very optimistic; (ii) baseline emissions of vehicles, which are very 
high and presumably correspond to old poorly maintained vehicles instead of comparing, as 
requested by GHG transport methodologies, a project alternative of a new vehicle with a 
baseline new vehicle; and (iii) assumption for electric vehicles of zero emissions instead of 
applying the grid factor per country and electricity usage. Using a more conservative approach, 
expected GHG reductions in this area are deemed to be 20–30 per cent of the amount suggested 
by the AE. However, the impact of these assumptions on the total projected GHG mitigation 
impact is marginal as the share of transport in the total mitigation impact is less than 1 per cent. 
20. 
The approach used to determine agricultural, forestry and land-use impacts is based on 
total hectares (has) financed using the GHG Protocol Agricultural Guidance and impacts per 
activity per ha based on the EX-ACT tool of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United 
Nations (FAO). GHG reductions of 3–24 tonnes per ha of land are expected per annum, 
depending on activities. Other sources10 used by other GCF-financed or to-be-funded 
programmes estimate impacts for the same activities as ranging from 0.07 to 4.7 t CO2/ha. 
Project Drawdown GHG data, as one example, shows two tonnes per annum for bamboo 
cultivation, while the AE assumes 24 t CO2 per annum. For tree plantations, 24 tonnes per ha 
were considered, while other sources show 3.3 tonnes (see Tree Plantations (on Degraded 
Land) | Project Drawdown). The emission factors therefore do not seem to be on the 
 
8 The AE indicated in its written response to question 13 raised by the independent TAP that indicatively, EUR 7.5 
million out of EUR 119.5 million (i.e. 6.3 per cent) shall be invested in innovative companies. 
9 For example, 50 per cent of fuel savings using hybrid vehicles, while standard figures are in the order of 20–30 per 
cent. 
10 For example, https://www.jswconline.org/content/jswc/71/3/68A.full.pdf; see tables 2 and 3.

=== PAGE 132 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 132 
 
 
 
 
conservative side but are rather optimistic. The AE considers, in general, an impact period of 20 
years for agriculture, forestry and land use based on FAO EX-ACT guidelines. However, the 
investment period is one year for the average credits and six years for innovative companies. 
Various areas targeted, such as the usage of organic fertilizers, are reversible if no further loan 
is given, and others, such as terraces and rainwater reservoirs, require continuous maintenance. 
A more conservative approach also used by other GCF-funded projects is to assume the impact 
only over the invested period, that is a maximum of six years. 
21. 
Multiple agricultural investments, for example in forestry, have potential issues of 
permanence. This is not discussed nor accounted for in calculations. Therefore, mitigation 
numbers need to be taken with caution as they reflect, to a certain extent, avoided and 
sequestered emissions, which have the risk of not being permanent. 
22. 
Based on the above considerations, the independent TAP estimates a more realistic 
adaptation and mitigation benefit for this programme: 121,000 direct beneficiaries, 321,000 
indirect beneficiaries and a GHG mitigation impact of 0.95 million t CO2 avoided with the remark 
that the GHG mitigation impact is partially based on sequestered emissions with the risk of non-
permanence. 
23. 
The programme allows for some investment areas which can be considered to lock in 
fossil technologies, such as investments in fossil-efficient trucks or hybrid vehicles. 
Technologies have moved fast in this area, and investments in such vehicles have long 
permanence. Electric vehicles in trucking are also already cost-competitive in most markets. 
Climate investments should avoid continuing investments into outdated technologies to achieve 
a maximum climate impact and achieve the target countries’ decarbonization goals. 
24. 
Neither the EE, GAWA Capital, nor the fund management’s designated investment 
committee members have relevant experience in assessing climate impacts of climate 
investments or projects. This endangers the potential impact of the programme. GAWA Capital 
has, however, ensured the independent TAP that relevant expertise will be brought on board. As 
this is considered critical not only for the programme impact but overall, for programme 
success, independent TAP approval is conditional upon fulfilment of this point.  
25. 
The project interventions include investing in innovative companies and FSPs. While the 
former has higher immediate GHG mitigation impacts per invested euro, the latter interventions 
green the FSPs, presumably resulting in continued ongoing lending of FSPs in climate-friendly 
investments, thus multiplying the impact of the initial investment. The indirect implications of 
this thus favour investments in FSPs and not in innovative companies. 
26. 
The programme aims to green FSPs. Greening their lending practices has a potentially 
high multiplication impact, resulting in private capital flowing into this sector and multiple 
instances of follow-up lending by the greened FSPs. Thus, the medium and long-term indirect 
impact is considered high or very high.  
27. 
Appropriate monitoring of the achieved impact in terms of resilience and mitigation is 
critical to ensure that investments achieve their desired impact and realize corrections during 
programme implementation by avoiding investments with low impact and strengthening those 
with high impact. FSPs must also be provided with simple-to-manage monitoring tools to track 
the climate performance of their investments. 
28. 
Overall, the independent TAP considers the project to have a medium impact. The short-
term or direct impact is regarded as a medium. The long-term indirect impact is viewed as 
potentially high, conditional upon successfully integrating climate-relevant know-how into 
programme execution. 
1.3 
Paradigm shift potential 
 
 
               Scale: Medium-High

=== PAGE 133 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 133 
 
 
 
 
29. 
The Kuali Fund-GCF targets FSPs. The greening of FSPs and their shift towards financing 
investments with favourable climate impact is paramount for moving large amounts of capital 
from the private sector towards investments with an adaptation and/or mitigation impact. 
Small FSPs in Latin America have, at the moment, limited exposure to and experience with 
climate-related lending, and unlocking this potential is a crucial paradigm shift.  
30. 
The investee companies are intended to be financially sustainable enterprises that 
continue operating after project termination. Successful companies have the potential to attract 
additional investments, thus expanding their impact. Successful loans from FSPs and exits of the 
fund in innovative enterprises could provide an essential signal to private investors concerning 
the profitability and risk of investing in this area, thus supporting a paradigm shift. 
31. 
The Kuali Fund-GCF intends to transform small FSPs to adopt and implement green 
strategies. The status and progression of the FSPs will be measured with the Green Index 3.0. 
This is considered an appropriate approach to assess the progression of the FSPs in 
accommodating climate-related investments in their portfolio. The gradual build-up of the 
portfolio in the climate change arena is also considered proper, as FSPs are new to the topic. 
Together with grant-based TA, this incentivizes FSPs to make the required adjustments. The 
intermediate assessment of target achievement in year three provides the required pressure to 
stay on track. 
32. 
The paradigm shift is achieved through greening the loan portfolio of FSPs. The area of 
direct investment in companies which provide climate solutions is less transformational. Also, 
with only a few investments expected in this area (two currently planned investments), no 
critical mass is achieved, and no transformational impact can be predicted. Investments in 
individual enterprises can be justified to diversify risks and increase the fund’s profitability, 
which are both critical to attracting private investors. However, investments in this area should 
be limited to allow for a clear focus on FSPs.  
33. 
High-quality TA and capacity-building on climate lending will be crucial to achieving the 
target of greening FSPs. The TA strengthens the FSPs and is considered essential and 
acknowledged for attaining high climate impact and reducing risks to FSPs. GAWA Capital 
Partners and the fund management’s designated investment committee members have an 
excellent background and experience working with financial institutions and impact 
investments but lack the critical climate focus. This can and must be brought on board to ensure 
the transformational potential is achieved.  
34. 
Ecosystem issues and failures are also significant barriers to greening FSPs. The AE has 
not yet identified these at the country level. The TA includes an analysis of the ecosystem and 
activities to remove identified barriers. A further strengthening of this area and collaboration 
with other initiatives would result in a more substantial impact on the macro level, which is also 
considered critical to achieving a transformational impact. Knowledge-sharing with other FSPs 
and organizations in this space will be essential to entice other FSPs to engage in climate 
lending and spread out from investments in individual FSPs to the larger market (for example, 
160,000 fintech companies (finance and insurance area) are registered in Brazil alone).11   
35. 
Based on the above, the independent TAP considers the proposed investments in FSPs 
linked with TA to be critical to green the financial sector, which is crucial for the massive 
deployment of private capital towards climate-related investments. This will be a 
transformational change. However, the effort of this programme alone will be insufficient to 
achieve such change in all the targeted countries; nevertheless, it is a critical first step. Focusing 
on FSPs, collaborating with other institutions in the same sphere and good outreach with a high-
quality knowledge management system are essential to ensure and strengthen the 
transformational impact. The rating given is thus medium to high. 
 
11 Brazil: number of finance and insurance companies | Statista.

=== PAGE 134 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 134 
 
 
 
 
1.4 
Sustainable development potential  
    
             Scale: Medium 
36. 
The project is aligned with several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1 (No 
poverty), SDG 2 (Zero hunger), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 5 (Gender equality), 
SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (Decent work 
and economic growth), SDG 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), SDG 13 (Climate action), 
and SDG 15 (Life on land). 
37. 
Environmental co-benefits are related to organic farming and reduced fertilizer use, 
reduced nitrogen and chemical run-off, efficient water usage, sustainable land use with less 
post-harvest loss, and reduced pressure on land. On the mitigation side, environmental co-
benefits are reduced energy usage, which can again translate into fewer pollutants. 
38. 
Economic benefits are achieved through investing in businesses with services that will 
improve the incomes of smallholder farmers and SMEs in the host countries. Farmers can 
improve their incomes through crop diversification, improved efficiency using fewer input 
materials, increased productivity and yields, and improved sales prices of agricultural products 
through enhanced quality of products and market information. SMEs and invested companies 
can improve their revenues and profits and position themselves with products and services 
with a high growth potential. 
39. 
Social benefits are realized through improving the livelihood of farmers by increasing 
crop productivity (through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture) and incomes, resulting in 
enhanced quality of life. The project also expects to create around 1,600 jobs over the life of the 
fund. 
40. 
The project seeks to invest in and support agribusinesses and SMEs through a gender 
lens. It also provides TA to the invested companies and FSPs to enhance their gender expertise.  
41. 
Overall, the project’s sustainable development potential is considered medium. It can 
make contributions in the area of gender and have positive social, economic and environmental 
impacts. 
1.5 
Needs of the recipient  
 
                          Scale: Medium-High 
42. 
The countries included are vulnerable to climate change, and many have a significant 
agricultural sector that is especially exposed to climate risks. FSPs are exposed to climate risks 
through lending, among other things, to the agricultural sector, which has high climate risks. 
Also, SMEs experience above-average exposure to climate risks due to their lack of adaptive 
capacity. 
43. 
Many countries involved have significant and persistent fiscal deficits, which have led to 
public debt accumulation, crowding out private sector borrowing, and posing macroeconomic 
risks. Limited access to credit results in low levels of private investment. High-level government 
borrowing from the financial sector crowds out private sector financing and has kept the 
financial system from extending finance to riskier and vulnerable sectors like agriculture or 
SMEs. It also limits government financing in the area of adaptation to climate risks.  
44. 
The independent TAP considers the recipient’s need medium to high.  
1.6 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
    Scale: Low-medium  
45. 
The programme is consistent with the nationally determined contributions (NDCs) of 
the involved countries, which is unsurprising in itself as the programme intends to cover two 
large sectors of the economy (agriculture and SMEs), adaptation and mitigation, and a broad 
range of technologies. These technologies, as well as the sectors, are included in the NDCs.

=== PAGE 135 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 135 
 
 
 
 
However, the funding proposal submitted does not show if the NDCs or country policies and 
strategies specifically include FSPs for achieving climate targets.   
46. 
The no-objection letters (NOLs) of the seven countries included (Brazil, Colombia, El 
Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico and Panama) have been provided. The programme is 
designed for 14 countries in Latin America, the Caribbean and India, but it has not (yet) received 
an NOL from the other 8 countries. 
47. 
The project did not undergo formal stakeholder engagement. The formal stakeholder 
engagement strategy shall be realized once the programme is operational. This is also related to 
the fact that the FSPs have not yet been selected, and the direct beneficiaries have not yet been 
determined. This is acknowledged, and the funding proposal has a procedure which includes 
formal stakeholder engagement at a later stage. However, it would have been helpful to engage 
multiple FSPs, and not only the ones already working with the AE and the EE before or during 
programme formulation to better understand their needs, constraints and possible support for 
such a programme. This is also critical to ensure replication of efforts and to create a knowledge 
management platform that is useful to the invested FSPs and others. 
48. 
The independent TAP therefore assesses country ownership as low to medium.  
1.7 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
                         Scale: Medium 
49. 
Kuali Fund-GCF has a blended finance structure. The European Union and GCF 
contributions to the first-loss tranche (EUR 1.26 million from the European Union and EUR 25 
million from the GCF) are essential given the risk perspectives of investors and enable the influx 
of private capital to the agriculture and SME sectors. The AE intends to establish a EUR 119.5 
million returnable fund with a 10–12-year duration. 
50. 
The European Union (EUR 1.26 million in equity and EUR 0.57 million in grants) and 
AECID (EUR 17.6 million in loans, EUR 1.8 million indirectly through Kuali Fund-GCF and EUR 
0.3 million in grants) commitments have been approved, and legal drafting is being finalized. 
COFIDES is expected to invest EUR 5 million from its balance sheet and GAWA Capital is 
expected to invest EUR 1 million as equity. The targeted private funding is EUR 72.8 million 
from private investors, including from COFIDES as equity. Current investors in the Huruma 
Fund managed by GAWA Capital have expressed interest in investing, including large 
institutional investors from Europe and the United States of America and several prominent 
family offices. Considering the challenging market conditions and high interest rates, the target 
for the overall Kuali Fund-GCF of reaching private investment of EUR 213 million might not be 
achieved. In that case, the total fund would be smaller than expected. To ensure that private 
capital is not spread out over the entire Kuali Fund-GCF, leaving the GCF with a higher share of 
equity than envisaged, the GCF disbursements should be tied to achieving the private sector 
capital inflow targets.  
51. 
The AE requests EUR 25 million in equity capital from GCF with a 3.9 per cent equity 
return. This vehicle, and GCF’s anchoring investment, should enable the fund to raise EUR 17.6 
million in senior loans, capped at 3.75 per cent from AECID, EUR 1.8 million in subordinated 
loans from Kuali Fund-GCF (indirectly from AECID), EUR 1 million in equity from GAWA Capital 
and EUR 72.8 million as equity from private investors with a 6.5 per cent equity return. GCF 
participation is considered material to provide de-risking in attracting additional capital. Thus, 
the GCF equity investment could be leveraged by nearly factor 4, a high leverage factor. At the 
moment, it is, of course, not yet guaranteed if the programme will achieve its private investment 
target. A staged or tranched investment of the GCF with clear achievement targets of private 
sector financing is required to ensure that the leverage ratio is not significantly lower than 
expected.

=== PAGE 136 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 136 
 
 
 
 
52. 
The fund expects to raise EUR 8.7 million in grant finance for the TA fund and data 
collection, EUR 7.8 million from GCF and EUR 0.9 million from the European Union and AECID. 
GCF grants could thus be leveraged with a low rate of 0.1. 
53. 
The total investment amount is EUR 128.2 million, of which EUR 32.8 million is from 
GCF, with an expected co-financing ratio of 74 per cent and a leverage factor of 2.9. This is a high 
leverage ratio and a high co-finance ratio. The positive aspect of co-finance is that the large 
majority is actual private capital and does not come from multilateral development banks or 
government/semi-government financial entities. GAWA Capital has experience and a proven 
track record with their previous Huruma Fund in attracting private capital, which shall also be 
used for this programme.   
54. 
Kuali Fund – GCF is a for-profit fund. This can potentially increase the impact, 
replicability and sustainability and bring efficiency and effectiveness to investment decisions. 
55. 
Based on 50 per cent of the finance used for adaptation and the updated number of 
beneficiaries, the GCF cost per direct beneficiary is EUR 136. Based on 50 per cent of the 
mitigation finance and the updated GHG mitigation income, the GCF cost per t CO2 avoided is 
EUR 17. Both figures are considered reasonable and have a good cost-benefit relationship.  
56. 
The fund and TA fund manager, GAWA Capital, is a private limited liability firm 
specializing in impact investment management services with considerable experience in fund 
management and the agricultural and SME sectors working through FSPs. GAWA Capital has 
performed in multiple countries in Latin America. The targeted investments in FSPs are 
essentially existing clients from a previous and ongoing GAWA Capital fund (Huruma Fund). 
Neither the fund manager nor the four designated members of the investment committee have 
relevant climate or climate finance experience. GAWA  Capital has, however, committed to 
bringing a senior climate expert on board. 
57. 
The overall independent TAP assessment concerning efficiency and effectiveness is 
considered medium. The co-finance ratio, if achieved, is high. To achieve this level, safeguards 
must be implemented to prevent the GCF from funding the programme with a higher share than 
anticipated. The involvement of GAWA Capital and COFIDES is also deemed essential to ensure 
the alignment of interests. The inflow of private finance into the fund is considered very 
favourable. The cost per direct beneficiary and tonne of CO2 mitigated are relatively low but are, 
as with any programme, dependent on the final projects financed.  
II. 
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
58. 
The independent TAP considers the funding proposal to be in line with the GCF’s 
strategic targets. The independent TAP notes that the programme aims to invest in FSPs in the 
agriculture and SME sectors, which are considerably affected by climate change and face serious 
climate risks. Greening financial service providers is critical to achieving a large-scale 
deployment of private capital towards climate investments. Greening the portfolio of small FSPs 
is paramount to completing a transformational impact and moving investments from one-time 
projects towards structural changes in the financial system. GAWA Capital, as an EE, has 
experience attracting private capital and working with FSPs and, indirectly, smallholder 
farmers. Investments are expected to happen in the field of adaptation and mitigation. This 
novel programme approach, the financial experience background of the managing entity, the 
significant impact and especially the transformational nature of this programme proposal 
warrant approval. 
59. 
The independent TAP also recognizes some significant deficiencies in the funding 
proposal, including (i) the current lack of climate experience in the management team; (ii) the 
deficient and non-conservative calculations regarding GHG impact and the number of 
beneficiaries; (iii) incomplete documentation within the funding proposal; and (iv) the risk of

=== PAGE 137 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 137 
 
 
 
 
losing focus and spreading out scarce capacities by investing in geographical areas outside Latin 
America and the Caribbean and by focusing too many efforts on investment companies.  
60. 
Nevertheless, the independent TAP ultimately decided to provide an endorsement with 
conditions (see para. 61 below). Factors which tip the scale for endorsement of this funding 
proposal are (i) the relevance of the programme itself and its innovative nature; (ii) the 
experience of the managing entity with FSPs, SMEs and the agricultural sector in target 
countries; (iii) the limited size of the funding request with a high leverage ratio; and (iv) the 
amount of design that has already taken place. We think it is imperative to provide a path 
forward and consider that the weak points identified can be amended with the conditions listed 
below. 
61. 
Therefore, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal subject to the following 
conditions: 
(a) 
Condition to be met prior to the effectiveness of the funded activity agreement 
(FAA): Delivery by the Accredited Entity to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory 
to the GCF Secretariat, of the disbursement schedule under the FAA for the GCF 
Reimbursable Funds and the GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds, with the amount and 
schedule for each disbursement tied to milestones in securing private capital for the 
Kuali Fund-GCF, such disbursement schedule to set out the breakdown in terms of 
amount per disbursement and the equity/grant split as agreed between the Accredited 
Entity and the GCF Secretariat (the “Disbursement Schedule”).   
(b) 
Condition to be met prior to the effectiveness of the FAA: Delivery by the Accredited 
Entity to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, that GAWA 
has contracted a senior climate expert (acceptable to the GCF Secretariat) with 
specialisation, preferably in adaptation and agriculture and experience in Latin America, 
whose role is to provide approval authority on the climate validity of Kuali Fund-GCF 
investments (the “Senior Climate Expert”).   
(c) 
Condition to be met prior to the effectiveness of the FAA: Delivery by the Accredited 
Entity to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of 
evidence that GAWA has subscribed for shares in the Kuali Fund-GCF in an amount of 
not less than EUR 1 million.  
(d) 
Condition to be met prior to the effectiveness of the FAA: Delivery by the Accredited 
Entity to the GCF, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of 
evidence that the Accredited Entity has subscribed for shares in the Kuali Fund-GCF in 
an amount of not less than EUR 1 million. 
(e) 
Condition to be met prior to each disbursement under the FAA: Unless otherwise 
agreed by the GCF Secretariat, delivery by the Accredited Entity to the GCF, in a form 
and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of evidence that the amounts 
requested by the Accredited Entity in the Request for Disbursement are consistent with 
the Disbursement Schedule. 
(f) 
Condition to be met prior to the first disbursement under the FAA: Delivery by the 
Accredited Entity to the GCF, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, 
of a stakeholder review report, setting out the outcome of a formal stakeholder review 
conducted by the Accredited Entity, such review to include, at minimum, FSP providers 
of the different Host Countries, government entities (at minimum from the agricultural, 
industry, environmental and finance ministries), SME and farmers associations. 
(g) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the GCF 
proceeds and the Co-financing are used exclusively in the Host Countries. 
(h) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the Kuali 
Fund-GCF (i) invests at least 80% of its capital into FSPs; and (ii) invests no more than

=== PAGE 138 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 138 
 
 
 
 
20% of its capital in innovative companies. For the avoidance of doubt, the “capital” of 
the Kuali Fund-GCF means the aggregate of the commitments of the Class E Shares and 
the relevant Co-financing. 
(i) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the Kuali 
Fund-GCF ensures that (i) each FSP builds a balanced investment portfolio in adaptation 
and mitigation; and (ii) if an FSP’s combined adaptation and mitigation investments fall 
below 30% or the ramp up period target as set by Kuali Fund for that FSP, whichever is 
higher, of total invested monies by the Kuali Fund-GCF in that FSP, no further 
disbursements are made by the Kuali Fund-GCF to that FSP until that FSP meets  the 
threshold of 30%. The threshold value is set at the level of the FSP's aggregate portfolio. 
(j) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the Kuali 
Fund-GCF does not invest more than 30% of its capital into any single country. 
(k) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the Kuali 
Fund-GCF does not invest in fossil fuel vehicles, including, without limitation, any 
investment in hybrid, plug-in hybrid, or “efficient” fossil vehicles, even if operated with 
biofuels (including gaseous as well as liquid fossil fuels). 
(l) 
Covenant to be included in the FAA: The Accredited Entity shall ensure that (i) a 
Senior Climate Expert is engaged by GAWA/Kuali Fund-GCF at all times; and (ii) the 
Senior Climate Expert is a member of the Kuali Fund-GCF’s investment committee with 
the same decision rights as other investment committee members.

Impact potential 
1. Realistic Adaptation and Mitigation Benefits: 
 
ITAP suggestion of a more conservative approach for impact calculations due to potential risk 
of overestimation it is acknowledged and accepted. An important upside to the committed 
targets will remain a potential scenario as some current and recommended assumptions might 
change in the future.  (More than avg. 50% climate portfolios, relevant number of IC clients and 
suppliers as beneficiaries, etc.) 
 
Besides, the highlighted importance of monitoring for achieving impact in terms of resilience 
and mitigation it is shared by the AE and the EE. Technical Assistance resources will play a key 
role on catalysing and ensuring the equipment of FSP with the proper tools.  
 
Paradigm shift potential 
The Fund manager, GAWA Capital, has committed to hiring a climate expert to complement 
their sound expertise and background in the financial inclusion sector and impact investment 
with the required climate expertise.  
The intermediate three-year assessment is directly linked to the agreed requirement for FSPs 
to build 50% of their climate portfolio by the end of year three. Moreover, GAWA Capital has 
extensive experience in working with FSPs. In the case of Kuali GCF, the covenants will be 
defined to ensure that FSPs’ interests are aligned with those of Kuali GCF’s.  
Following the previous experience with Huruma Fund (www.fondohuruma.com), Kuali GCF is 
committed to knowledge sharing. Furthermore, part of the TA resources will be dedicated to 
identifying specific barriers at country level, although GAWA Capital has already a sound field-
knowledge of nearly all the countries where Kuali GCF is going to be deployed.  
 
Sustainable development potential 
No Comments

=== PAGE 140 ===
GCF/B.38/02/Add.10 
Page 140 
 
 
 
Needs of the recipient 
One of the key outcomes of COP28 was the need to promote a joint effort by governments, 
international financial institutions and the private sector to align financial flows with global 
climate goals. 
As a result of growing adaptation finance needs and stagnating flows, the current adaptation 
finance gap is estimated at US$194-366 billion per year1. Simultaneously, adaptation planning 
and implementation appears to have stagnated. This has severe consequences, especially for 
the most vulnerable. 
Kuali will help mobilise private capital to finance the climate transition. In particular, it will 
support the adaptation of MSMEs and smallholder farmers by helping FSPs to transform 
themselves to be able to offer adapted products and solutions. 
 
Country ownership 
As the ITAP itself stated Kuali Fund is alligned with the nationally determined contributions 
(NDCs) of the host countries.  
AE consideres that the Country Ownership is higher than the level assesed by the ITAP for the 
following reasons: 
• 
Kuali will focus on SMEs and it will support agriculture sector, specially on adaptation, 
as well as mitigation with a comprehensive range of technologies. These technologies, 
as well as the sectors, are included in the NDCs.  
• 
In order to obtain the Non Objection Letters, the proposal was not only assessed by the 
NDA, but also underwent a validation process involving various ministries and 
institutions in all the countries concerned.  
• 
Host countries have assessed the proposal in relation to their NDCs and policies, and 
have found Kuali's strategy for achieving climate objectives (i.e. support for the FSP) to 
be relevant and in line with their climate strategy. 
• 
As ITAP acknowledges, Kuali has established a process that includes formal 
stakeholder engagement once implementation has begun. Knowledge sharing is part of 
Kuali's value added, as explained above, and technical assistance and capacity building 
on climate finance will be the tools to achieve this. 
 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
GAWA Capital and COFIDES have a long track record of working in similar blended finance 
structures where alignment of interests is a critical condition.  
The participation of AE and EE is granted because of their fiduciary duties, responsibilities, and 
economic interests. 
The funding request is small and the leverage ratio is high.

AE Comments on the deficits in the funding proposal highlighted by the ITAP 
(i) 
the current lack of climate experience in the management team;  
As mentioned above, the Fund manager has committed to hiring a senior climate expert to 
complement the management team with the required climate expertise. 
(ii) 
the deficient and non-conservative calculations regarding GHG impact and the number of 
beneficiaries;  
ITAP suggestion of a more conservative approach for impact calculations due to potential 
risk of overestimation it is acknowledged and accepted.  
(iii) 
incomplete documentation within the funding proposal;  
Full package (with agreed term sheet) has been provided to GCF and it has been reviewed 
and cleared by the Secretariat.  
(iv) 
the risk of losing focus and spreading out scarce capacities by investing in geographical areas 
outside Latin America and the Caribbean and by focusing too many efforts on investment 
companies.  
We consider this risk to be fully mitigated as the Kuali Fund-GCF will invest exclusively in 
Latin America.  
Alignment of interests is ensured by the minimum co-financing rate negotiated for the 
investment of GCF funds, which gives the fund manager a clear incentive to invest in 
countries eligible for GCF commitments. 
The experience, track record and capacity of both AE and EE in financing these activities in 
the region should also be highlighted.  
Conditions requested by ITAP has been accepted.

GCF GENDER ASSESSMENT & ACTION PLAN – KUALI FUND-GCF 
1. GENDER ASSESSMENT
1.1. Introduction 
This Gender Assessment and the resulting Gender Action Plan has been prepared following the “Gender 
Analysis/Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan Templates” 1 and the guiding questions listed 
therein. The Kuali Fund-GCF gender analysis/assessment is built on quantitative data and qualitative evidence 
derived from a desk review of existing literature and secondary databases, introduced as in body and footnote 
citations on this document. 
The Kuali Fund-GCF objective is to promote the transformation of the Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) financial 
sector into a more inclusive and greener industry by fostering systemic adoption of climate best practices by smaller 
financial institutions, including Microfinance Institutions, Cooperatives or other Non-Bank Financial Institutions and 
companies with innovative climate solutions (Innovative companies)2 so that they start serving green lending products 
to Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) and smallholder farmers in participating countries (Brazil, Colombia, 
El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Panama,). 
There is ample literature about gender issues in MSMEs and smallholder farmers in some of the target countries and in 
LAC as a whole. Consequently, the gender assessment is divided in two main chapters. Chapter 1.2 focuses on a general 
overview of gender issues regarding MSMEs and smallholder farmers in Latin America and Chapter 1.3 provides a 
gender analysis/assessment for the specific target countries with an emphasis on national gender strategies/policies 
and gender issues in specific areas like entrepreneurship and agriculture.  
The Kuali Fund-GCF gender targets are considered to be ambitious and of significant contribution. These targets have 
been defined in general terms, not for each country in specific. Nonetheless, the targets have been structured according 
to GAWA Capital’s current investments including most of the countries of this multi-country proposal. 
1 Accessible online under: https://www.greenclimate.fund/document/gender-assessment-and-action-plan-annex-8-funding-proposals  
2 Companies that already offer products/services related with mitigation and adaptation, including “Agri-tech” and “Climate-tech”.

Looking at GDI, disparities in development between men and women are more pronounced in Guatemala which 
belongs to group 3 that comprises countries with medium equality in HDI with absolute deviation of 5–7.5 percent. 
Then, El Salvador and México belong to group 2 which comprises countries with medium to high equality in HDI 
achievements between women and men (absolute deviation of 2.5–5 percent). The rest of target countries have 
high equality in HDI achievements between women and men (absolute deviation of less than 2.5 percent). 
Regarding the GII which reflects inequality in achievement between women and men in three dimensions: 
reproductive health, empowerment and the labor market, all target countries range between the 62-119 positions 
 
 
3 The GDI measures gender gaps in human development achievements by accounting for disparities between women and men in three basic dimensions of 
human development—health, knowledge and living standards using the same component indicators as in the HDI. The GDI is the ratio of the HDIs 
calculated separately for females and males using the same methodology as in the HDI. It is a direct measure of gender gap showing the female HDI as a 
percentage of the male HDI. 
4 The GII is an inequality index. It measures gender inequalities in three important aspects of human development—reproductive health, measured by 
maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rates; empowerment, measured by proportion of parliamentary seats occupied by females and proportion of 
adult females and males aged 25 years and older with at least some secondary education; and economic status, expressed as labour market participation 
and measured by labour force participation rate of female and male populations aged 15 years and older. The GII is built on the same framework as the 
IHDI—to better expose differences in the distribution of achievements between women and men. It measures the human development costs of gender 
inequality. Thus, the higher the GII value the more disparities between females and males and the more loss to human development. See Technical note 4 
at http://hdr.undp.org/sites/default/files/hdr2020_technical_notes.pdf for details on how the Gender Inequality Index is calculated. 
5 The index benchmarks national gender gaps on economic, education, health, and political criteria (see Figure 1), and provides country rankings that allow 
for effective comparisons across regions and income groups 
6 Countries are divided into five groups by absolute deviation from gender parity in HDI values. 
7 World Economic Forum. Global Gender Gap Report. 2021

=== PAGE 144 ===
3 
 
out of a total of 189 countries. Guatemala shows the biggest level of inequality. Mexico shows the lowest level of 
inequality.  
The Global Gender Gap of the World Economic Forum (WEF) benchmarks national gender gaps on economic, 
education, health and political criteria, and provides country rankings that allow for effective comparisons across 
regions. Western Europe is once again the region where the gender gap is smallest (77,6%), placing it ahead of North 
America, which has closed 76,4% of its gap, Latin America and the Caribbean (72,1%), and Eastern Europe and Central 
Asia (71,2%). The East Asia and the Pacific region (68,8%) is just ahead of Sub-Saharan Africa (67,6%), while South Asia 
has closed 62,7% of its gap and is ahead of the Middle East and North Africa, the region with the lowest performance 
(61,6%) 
Looking at the four subindexes, regional performance is well positioned in terms of Health and Survival (97,6%) and 
gender parity in Educational Attainment (99,7%). Regional performances on the Economic Participation and 
Opportunity subindex  (64,2%), however, vary within a wider range. Political empowerment gaps (27,1%) are also large 
in many countries of the region.  
All target countries in LAC region have ratified the CEDAW, Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination 
against Women as well as the “Convention of Belem do para” Interamerican Convention on the Prevention, punishment 
and eradication of  violence, except Colombia which is adhered to this last one. In relation to ILO Conventions, all 
countries have ratified Core Conventions 100 on equal remuneration for work of equal value and 111 on discrimination 
(employment and occupation). Regarding maternity protection, two countries (El Salvador and Panamá8) have ratified 
number 183 which is the most advanced in rights and protection for women and two countries have ratified number 
103 (Brazil and Guatemala). Two countries have ratified Convention 156 on workers with family responsibilities (El 
Salvador, Guatemala). Finally, two countries have ratified Convention 190 on violence and harassment at work (México 
and Panama9) 
1.2.2. Specific Gender Analysis by country 
1.2.2.1. Brazil 
From a strictly legal standpoint, men and women are fully equal in Brazil. The Citizens' Constitution of 1988 declared 
women equal to men in all legal respects, explicitly stating in Article 5 of Title II that "men and women have equal rights 
and duties under the terms of this Constitution." Women, Business and Law Index scores 85, with legal constraints 
found in the areas of workplace (law does not mandate equal remuneration for work of equal value) and assets (law 
does not prohibit discrimination in access to credit based on gender)10. Besides, 91.7% of legal frameworks that 
promote, enforce and monitor gender equality, with a focus on violence against women, are in place according to UN 
Women. 
The Ministry of Women, Family and Human Rights (MMFDH) has the purpose to implement, promote, and protect 
human rights, civic rights, and the rights of children, adolescents, women, families, the elderly, and the disabled. 
The OECD Development Centre’s Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) 11 which measures discrimination against 
women in social institutions taking into account laws, social norms and practices that restrict women’s access to

The road to equality is still a long one for women and even more difficult if the woman involved is black, brown, or 
mixed race and lives outside of the urban centres in the South and Southeast Regions.

Brazil is fairly egalitarian when it comes to providing education and health rights for men and women but continues to 
have large gender gaps within the workforce and government representation. Gender gap in management positions 
and in top management is 26% and 19% respectively.  
Some representative indicators show for example that the proportion of women aged 20-24 years old who were 
married or in union before age 18 is 26.2%. Also, the adolescent birth rate is 58.6 per 1000 population as of 2016.  
UN Women also states that in Brazil, only 43.8% of indicators needed to monitor the SDGs from a gender perspective 
are available, with gaps in key areas such as violence against women. In addition, many areas such as gender and 
poverty, women’s access to assets including land, physical and sexual harassment, and gender and the environment 
currently lack comparable methodologies for comprehensive and periodic monitoring.

Regarding the program specific areas of intervention, Brazil has 16 million MSMEs, being the largest contributor in the 
region to total enterprises13.  MSMEs form an essential part of the Brazilian economy, accounting for 98.5% of all legally 
constituted companies, for 27% of GDP, and for 41% of the total payroll14. Out of 49 economies, Brazil rank 8 in Total 
Early-stage Entrepreneur Activity (TEA), 7 in Established Business Ownership (EBO) and 5 in informal investment. There 
is a significant gender gap in both TEA (23% male: 17.2% female) and EBO (14.2% male: 6.8% female)15 
Despite their importance to economic growth, MSMEs and smallholder farmers suffer more from credit constraints 
than larger companies, since they usually have less collateral to secure a loan and are more likely to default than large 
companies. This situation is often exacerbated by the lack of financial planning and training of the manager. 
Women account for 31% of the total entrepreneurial population in Brazil, but for 47% of individual micro-entrepreneurs 
(MEI), suggesting that women-owned businesses are on average smaller than men-owned businesses. Other studies 
also confirm that Brazilian businesses owned by women are less growth-oriented, export-oriented, innovative and 
profitable than those owned by men16.  It has been estimated that if women were to start growth-oriented businesses 
at the same rate as men, 5.8 million more jobs would be created in two years17  
In terms of policies, there is a common perception in Brazil that special programmes for women entrepreneurs are not 
needed since all programmes are open to everyone. However, a lack of explicit exclusion is often not enough to build a 
culture of full inclusion in which women enjoy the same opportunities as men. This is also true for other 
underrepresented groups in the entrepreneurial population, such as Afro-Brazilians, migrants, and refugees. 
The finance gap in LAC is the second largest after East Asia region, mainly driven by Brazil ($0.6 trillion). This large 
financial gap is especially relevant for women, around 45 percent of women-owned SMEs identify access to finance as 
a major constraint in operating and growing their businesses18.  
According to OECD SIGI, regarding access to productive and financial resources, Brazil scores 16.219, better than other 
target countries of the Fund such as El Salvador (22.1) and Colombia (33.7). According to an OECD study20, in Brazil, the 
gender gap in financial knowledge, one of the main barriers for inclusion, is 8 points, similar to financial behaviour, 
understood as budgetting, setting goals, choosing financial products and using independent advice, where the gap is 7 
points. Meanwhile, there is also a relevant difference in financial attitudes in favour of women, with 52% showing 
positive attitudes towards the longer term compared with 49% of men. In terms of formal education, Brazil has 
experiences of interest in the framework of the national financial education strategies21.

According to a study of the Interamerican Development Bank (IDB) about leadership in microfinance institutions22, 50%  
of these institutions in Brazil have a female general manager and 20,83% of women in senior management positions.  
Despite provisions protecting women's land ownership rights, local customs often infringe on the enjoyment of this 
right in the case of inheritance or after divorce. Also, many women in rural areas lack birth certificates or identity cards, 
which prevents them from accessing land titles. Evidence shows that the entire financial architecture of lending, 
especially in relation to microcredit, has not taken into account the needs of women, particularly poor women and 
women at the intersection of multiple discriminations23. 
Regarding climate change, Brazil is the fifth-biggest emitter of greenhouse gases, an important agro-exporter and also 
home to the Amazon Basin ecosystems that provide essential services for many people both regionally and globally. 
Brazil’s Amazon deforestation and the agriculture sector are increasingly main contributors due to the expansion of 
cattle-ranching, soy cultivation, illegal gold mining and logging. 
Climate change effects can be felt notably in such a dynamic environmental and socio-economic system. Important 
impacts can therefore be expected from both global price changes and local yield changes. Previous studies on the 
impacts of climate change on agriculture in Brazil have found potentially severe impacts, with farms losing on average 
23 percent of their land values by 2060 in the worst climate scenario24 
Brazil has an unusually large share of female-headed households compared to other countries in Latin America25 (34,8), 
but they are mainly concentrated in urban areas. In rural areas it is 18% and they are less likely to be vulnerable due to 
reasons such as higher levels of per capita income which are also more diversified. This could be because in those 
households there are several income earners, women are older and so have grown-up children and since they earn less 
money in equivalent jobs, women tend to have more than one main source of income. Female-headed households 
should therefore be better able to absorb the impacts of climate change regarding decreasing yields and increasing 
prices of agricultural products. However, according to IFAD26, family farms headed by women have performed worse 
than average in reducing poverty over recent years and rural women suffer more the constraints of a social environment 
marked by discriminatory practices 
 
1.2.2.2. Colombia 
 
Colombia has a robust normative system and institutions to support the rights of girls and women. The country’s 1991 
Constitution recognises women’s equality and outlaws gender-based discrimination. Particularly, the High Presidential 
Office for the Equality of Women was created to monitor policies on gender equity. Besides, Colombia has introduced 
over the years vigorous legislation to promote equality like the law requiring that women candidates comprise at least 
30% of party electoral lists that has been crucial for obtaining higher representation of women in public institutions.

The National Development Plan 2018-22 includes an entire chapter on women’s rights based on three dimensions: the 
economic, the political and physical integrity, which includes violence against women27. 
Despite a good legislative framework, in practice, girls and women still endure discrimination, the most prominent on 
women participation in the labour market and their economic development in general. Women, Business and Law Index 
scores 84.4, with legal constraints found in the areas of workplace (law does not mandate equal remuneration for work 
of equal value) and pension (periods of absence from work due to childcare are not accounted for pension benefits)28. 
Particularly important is the exclusion of rural women in the processes of social and productive order that leads to 
equitable and sustainable rural development.  
An important factor affecting women in Colombia has been the civil armed conflict of recent decades. An estimated of 
8.8 million people have been registered as victims of the armed conflict. The majority are victims of forced 
displacement, of which about 80% are women and children and 87% are from rural areas. Furthermore, 15.8% of the 
women displaced because of the armed conflict have stated that they were also victims of sexual violence.

remains low. OECD SIGI scores 23.5 and a significant 19% of population think that a men should have more right to a 
job than a woman and that men makes better business executives. Only 19% of firms have women in top management 
positions.

Regarding the program specific areas of intervention, MSMEs and smallholder farmers represent an important part of 
the Colombian economy. In 2017, according to the National Department of Statistics (DANE), MSMEs and smallholder 
farmers contributed to 40% of GDP, generated 80% of employment and constituted 90% of the country's productive 
sector. 
According to figures from the Bogotá Chamber of Commerce, of the total number of entrepreneurs in the capital, 38.7% 
of legal representatives are women. Figure that can certainly be estimated to be much lower on other areas of the 
country. Often the need for economic subsistence is the main reason for entrepreneurship, accompanied by the 
aspiration to balance family and work responsibilities and to achieve professional development. 
In Colombia, the historical gaps in entrepreneurship by gender have shown a decreasing trend and the ratio of male 
entrepreneurs to female entrepreneurs it close to 1. Despite this data, women face significant barriers when it comes 
to participating in the formal economy. The perception of gender roles, the sexual division of labour and the lack of 
access to financial capital are some of the factors that condition women's access to the productive sphere. 
Women entrepreneurs are characterized by participating in fragile business initiatives, not very intensive in job creation 
and in branches of activity with few entry barriers (requiring little initial capital, intensive in labour, etc.). Thus, women 
entrepreneurs operate in highly competitive sectors that are more vulnerable to changes in the economic context. 
According to the GEM report, with respect to ventures, the extractive sector is the one with the lowest participation of 
women, with 2.3%, and the consumer-oriented sector is the most prevalent, with 57.4%, followed by that of 
transformation, with 20.5%. 
The degree of innovation and internationalization of women's business initiatives is equally low, their presence in high-
impact sectors is low. The influence of a culture and social structure articulated around gender differences, which 
condition their attitude and self-esteem, represent greater difficulties for them. Despite reporting high educational 
levels and social skills comparable to those of men, companies created by women tend to have a slower size and 
expansion than those created by men. 
Business financing in Colombia was rated with a score of 3.5 on a 5.0 scale (GEM Colombia, 2022), which indicates that 
Colombian entrepreneurs do not have sufficient sources of financing such as angel investors, equity capital, bank debt, 
public subsidies, venture capital, crowdfunding, etc. The gender gap in perceived entrepreneurship skills remains at 
around 10 points over the last five years and in terms of no fear of failure is closed to 4 points in 2022.

=== PAGE 150 ===
9 
 
Out of 49 economies, Colombia rank 2 in TEA, 34 in EBO and 34 in informal investment. The proportion of adults starting 
or running a new business is highest in five Latin America & Caribbean countries, being Colombia one of them29. 
Moreover, it is one of the seven countries in which one in five women were starting or running a new business. Female 
new entrepreneurship has moved in line with the males, so a small gender gap has persisted. Regarding the levels of 
TEA and EBO by gender, the gap is 4 and 2.2 points respectively. 
The country has no legislation prohibiting discrimination in access to credit30. Colombia scores 33 regarding access to 
productive and financial resources31, and there is a 6 point gap in ownership of land32. There are differences in access 
and use of financial products between women and men. Gap in access is 6.6 and in use is 3.8. Access to credit product 
also show a gap of 3.3. In addition, in all forms of credit, the average amount of money disbursed is higher for men than 
for women. Women aged 18-25 and in rural areas are the groups with a lower level of access to financial products33.  
According to a study on female leadership by the Interamerican Development Bank, 54,5% of microfinance institutions 
have a female general manager and 51,4% of women in senior management positions, the best figures among the target 
countries of the Kuali Fund-GCF, together with Panama. 
Regarding the rural context, women are commonly involved in unpaid agricultural jobs and housekeeping due to strong 
gender roles in rural family structures. Therefore, their entrepreneurship activity tends to move towards sectors other 
than the traditional ones where they find space for growth and that enables them to have economic independence. 
Due to internal displacement and the tendency of rural men to migrate to urban areas, the number of female-headed 
households has increased in the last 10 years. As a result, women must shoulder the responsibilities of both domestic 
and productive activities. Their poverty rate is 52.3% compared with 44.7% of households headed by men. Regarding 
extreme poverty rate, is 29.3% and 20.5% respectively. Among the main barriers, it stands out their limited access to 
credit, paid work, land, and participation in decision-making; 31.3 % of agricultural workers are considered unpaid family 
help and these activities are often not reported as work34.  
The promotion of female entrepreneurship and companies run by women in agricultural activities, the increase in 
activities related to the secondary and tertiary sectors in the rural economy constitute an essential aspect of rural 
development policies to bring progress to the regions hardest hit by the conflict.The Second Biennial Update Report on 
Climate Change in Colombia35 built by IDEAM and UNDP states for the first time the importance of addressing climate 
change with gender perspective. In Colombia the main source of carbon emissions is linked to the agricultural sector, 
according to the latest inventory of greenhouse gases (IDEAM 2017), the emissions of this sector were of a 36%, above 
sectors such as transport (11%) and energy (10%). Rural women in Colombia constitute more than 48% of the registered 
rural population, and 36% of them are agricultural producers. The recognition of the central role played by women in 
aspects such as agricultural production, food security and resilience in communities to climate change are essential 
aspects for the promotion of sustainable and inclusive development 
From the Directorate of Climate Change, the "Proposal of Technical Guidelines to Promote and Strengthen the Gender 
Approach in Attention to the Establishment of the PNCC (2017)" was built, which is framed in the provisions of the 
national and international contexts, both in terms of gender approach as in relation to its incorporation in the 
management of climate change.

1.2.2.3. El Salvador 
El Salvador has implemented numerous legislative amendments in the last years regarding, among others, improving 
maternity protection, prevention of violence against women, reinforcing gender equality and non discrimination in the 
workplace including equal pay for equal work and a place free from violence36.  
The Salvadoran Institute for the Advencement of Women37  is the institution responsible for formulating, executing and 
monitoring compliance with the National Policy for Women. 
During the period 2016-2020 a National Equality Plan has been the main policiy tool with national scope but with 
regional and sectoral specificites. Institutional changes have been achieved and today more women are working at all 
levels of public service. In the executive branch, representation of women in decision-making positions increased from 
two female ministers in the period 2009-2014 to seven in the present presidential term (43,8%). In the municipal 
councils in 2021, 11% of mayor’s offices are women.  
Women, Business and Law Index scores 88.8, with legal constraints found in the areas of pay (law does not mandate 
equal remuneration for work of equal value), marriage, parenthood and pension. Access to assets are equal38.

Regarding employment, the IGUAL-ES programme has been rolled out to encourage businesses to establish egalitarian, 
inclusive and decent workplaces, which can be certified by a “gender equality seal”. Since 2018 employers have a duty 
to provide childcare facilities for their employees’ children between the ages of 4 months and 3 years for the duration 
of the workday.  
El Salvador scores 19.6 in OECD SIGI and a significant 27% of population think that a men should have more right to a 
job than a woman and that men makes better business executives. Gender gap in management positions is only 3 points 
but just 28% firms have women in top management positions.

To support the growing economy of microenterprises and small and medium-sized businesses, in 2019, the Legislative 
Assembly approved amendments so that 25% of the annual budget for procurement and contracts will be earmarked 
for goods and services provided by microenterprises and small and medium-sized businesses, with at least 10% reserved 
for purchases from businesses whose owner, majority shareholder or legal representative is a woman.  
The Women’s Entrepreneurship Programme promotes the entrepreneurship and economic empowerment of 
Salvadoran women. Women in El Salvador own 64% of microenterprises and 29% of medium and large enterprises39, 
which are also a more formal type of business. This offer an opportunity to train women so that their businesses grow. 
One of the objectives of the National Policy for Enterprise Development is to strengthen competitive and productive 
capacities from a territorial and gender perspective. 
Companies are located mainly in urban areas (91,13%). In the rural areas, entrepreneurship by women are more than 
double than those leaded by men. Both male and female MSMEs owners used their own resources as a primary source 
to finance their economic activity. For women owners, also stand out as important sources of financing community 
banks, NGOs, other cooperatives, moneylenders and seed capital granted by CONAMYPE40.  
Women have lower default rates. However, the lack of assets in their name are the main barriers to accessing formal 
sources of finance. Access to productive and financial resources scores 22.141. National data show that less than 30% 
of population has an account in a formal institution. The country has a National Policy for Financial Inclusion that has 
gender equity as one of the main goals. According to the National Survey of Demand of Financial Services  the gap in 
the use of at least one financial product, including savings, insurance, pensions and credit, reached 13 percentage 
points. There is no gender gap in relation to the use of mobile phones to make payments or transfers and the gap in 
relation to obtaining credit is also small (2%). In 2018 the Salvadoran Institute for the Advencement of Women in 
coordination with the Central Reserve Bank of El Salvador, conducted an assessment of the invisible barriers to women’s 
access to financial services with a view to prepare guidelines on financial inclusion from a gender perspective.
