=== PAGE 153 ===
12 
 
The IDB study on female leadership show that 20% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 50% 
of women in senior management positions.  
Measures have been taken in order to ensure women’s rights access to and control over land, housing and property, 
especially in rural areas. In 2018, the Salvadoran Agrarian Reform Institute revised and incorporated a gender 
perspective into its user service guidelines and provided information on Legislative Decree No. 719, which establishes 
land as a family asset. The Ministry of Agriculture has also taken steps to include women in its services (agricultural 
packages, TA programmes, trainings) but there is still a significant gender gap. 
Although the rate of rural poverty  decreased by 18% between 2000 and 2013, many rural people continue to live in 
poverty. Traditionally excluded groups such as young people, women, indigenous peoples and seniors are especially 
vulnerable. Young rural people are trapped between the lack of access to education, which makes it difficult to enter 
the formal labour market, and the lack of assets needed to launch a sustainable business. They are also among the main 
victims of crime and, as a result, many choose to migrate. They are reluctant to work in the agriculture sector because 
of its low profitability and the perception that agriculture is drudgery. 
The National Policy for Rural, Indigenous and Campesina Women was designed to guarantee human rights and promote 
the autonomy of rural women. Women and girls are 52,1% of rural population. 32.1% of households are headed by 
women (67.9% by men). However, they make up only 9% of the rural population engaged in agricultural activities and 
almost 30% as unpaid family workers. Most of the majority of these women, 75 %, declare themselves as economically 
inactive and 50.8 % have no income of their own, compared to 17% of men42.  
The illiteracy rate among women stood at 17.4 per cent in rural areas. In 2019, the rate of participation in the labour 
force was 59.1% in rural areas (36,8% women and 84,4% men). Data regarding wages show a gender gap: the average 
monthly salary at the national level was $344.29, with women earning $306.11 and men earning $373.40 on average 
and in rural areas, women earning $213.23 and men earning $276.34 on average. 
El Salvador is located in one of the world's most vulnerable areas to the impact of natural hazards in the world. 2.2 
million people live in poverty and climate vulnerability in the 114 municipalities that make up the so-called “dry 
corridor”. The effects of climate change and environmental degradation are causing the displacement of thousands of 
people. The link between gender and climate change has been scarcely studied but there is consensus on the types of 
strategies that should be addressed.  In the case of adaptation measures, subsistence farming practices and rural water 
management are tasks often assigned to women. UN WOMEN highlights strategies such as empowering women 
through work in the areas of land, credit, agricultural inputs, technology, extension and training services to broaden 
their capacity and the sustainable use of natural resources43. 
 
1.2.2.4. Guatemala 
In Guatemala, the Constitution incorporates an article on the right to non-discrimination and the Constitutional Court 
upheld the exercise of this right in the workplace and the family, informed by a perspective of intersectionality and 
equality44. The Presidential Secretariat for Women (SEPREM) is the high level institution which depends directly from 
the President of the Republic. It is the advisory and coordinating body for public policies to promote the comprehensive 
development of women.  
 
 
42 UN WOMEN, 2020 
43 UN WOMEN, 2020 
44 UN CEDAW. Tenth periodic report. December 2021

=== PAGE 154 ===
13 
 
Guatemala, the biggest economy in Central America, is known for its fertile land, abundant natural resources and robust 
agricultural sector, which employs half of the population and accounts for 10% of the country’s GDP. However, the 
country has one of the highest inequality rates in Latin America and the lowest rate of women’s participation in the 
workforce in LAC. Rates of poverty, malnutrition and maternal-child mortality, especially in rural and indigenous areas, 
are some of the worst in the region. Additionally, chronic malnutrition affects almost one half of all children under the 
age of five, predominantly those living in rural areas (53%)45. Access to formal financial services and resources, health 
and education services are still limited. Interrelated territorial, ethnic and gender inequalities intensify women's 
constraints to full participation in development 
The National Policy for the Promotion and Comprehensive Development of Women have set three priorities for the 
period 2021-2024: education, eradication of violence against women and girls and women’s economic development. In 
2021 the strategy for the implementation of the 2020-2029 National Plan for the Prevention and Erradication of 
Violence against Women was adopted. In 2017, the Ministry of Labour and Social Security presented a protocol for 
addressing and preventing workplace harassment against women working in both public and private sectors. 
Women, Business and Law Index scores 73.8, with legal constraints found in the areas of workplace (the law does not 
prohibit discrimination in employment and there is no legislationon sexual harassment), pay (law does not mandate 
equal remuneration for work of equal value), marriage, parenthood and entrepreneurship (law does not prohibit 
discrimination in access to credit). Access to assets are equal46.

include gender equity as priority area, with the aim of ensuring that women have access to fairly paid and productive 
work under conditions that ensure respect for their freedom, security and human dignity.

Regarding entrepreneurship, out of 49 economies, Guatemala rank 1 in TEA, 5 in EBO and 2 in informal investment. 
Women in Guatemala own 40.37% of microenterprises and 16.27% of small and medium enterprises47. The proportion 
of adults starting or running a new business is highest in five LAC countries, being Guatemala one of them and is one of 
the seven countries in which one in five women were starting or running a new business48. In the levels of TEA and 
EBO by gender, the gap is 2.6 and 3.9 points respectively, scoring better than other countries of the project. The 
percentage of adults starting or running a new business in Guatemala has actually increased over the pandemic cycle, 
from 25% in 2019 to 29% in 2022, although the level of EBO has gone the other way, from 15% in 2019 to 12% in 2022. 
There were more than two adults starting a new business for every adult running an established business in 2022, with 
men slightly more likely than women to be running those new businesses. Guatemala is an example of a country with 
low levels of human development and high levels of TEA. Consumer services made up 78% of startups.  
The Women’s Economic Development Act facilitates access to economic and productive resources as well as 
technological services to implement projects for the economic empowerment of women. As at December 2020, 
statistical data show that women have limited access to the financial system and when they do, they, in general, receive 
financing in lower amounts and with higher interest rates than men, even though women have a lower default rate. 4 
in 10 loans were for women, the interest rate applied was 21.2 (19.4 for men), while the default rate was 1.8 in the case 
of women and 2.6 of men49. Through the Fund for the Development of MSMEs, during the period 2017–2021, 35.1 % 
of loans was granted to women. Guatemala scores 28.4 in access to productive and financial resources. In spite of equal 
ownership rights to immovable property the gender gap in ownership of land reaches 26 points50. 
According to the IDB study on female leadership, 38% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 
33% of women in senior management positions.

In 2019, women represented 8.3 per cent of employers in the agricultural sector. The procedure to facilitate access to

The Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Food is empowering women by providing them with training and technical

supplies in order to improve household finances. Between 2017 and 2021, 60.9% (538,014) of the beneficiaries of the 
various activities implemented under the Ministry’s institutional programmes were women. In order to advance equal 
opportunity in the rural areas it is critical to target women producers and it is also a significant driver of improved 
nutrition at home51. Exclusion and racism have generated various forms of structural, legal and institutional violence 
and discrimination, particularly those living in rural areas.  
Guatemala is one of the 20 countries in the world that are most affected by the severe impacts of extreme climate 
events. Some studies have shown that losses caused by natural disasters affect directly to more vunerable people, old 
age, women and children living in poor rural areas.  
 
1.2.2.5. Honduras 
Honduras is a middle-income country of Central America that is facing persistent poverty, insecurity and socio-economic 
inequality. Poverty rate reached 66% in 2016. The country has one of the lowest Human Development Index of the 
region, specially due to the low expectancy life and gross national income per capita. 
Since 2015, Honduras has adopted several laws to protect women’s rights such as the Wage Equality Act, the Credit for 
Rural Women Act, regulations for the implementation of the principle of parity on electoral lists, regulations to monitor 
investigations of violent deaths of women and cases of feminicide, among others. Likewise, Honduras has also adopted 
policies against racism, on trafficking with persons, cities for women and sexual and reproductive health52.  
The National Institute for Women is implementing the Second National Gender Equity and Equality Plan 2010-2022 
through the Gender Units and the municipal offices for women. The Plan was developed with wide participation of 
women's movements, civil society, and municipal governments and has had the support of international agencies such 
as AECID, UNFPA and UNDP. 
Women, Business and Law Index scores 75, with legal constraints found in the areas of pay (law does not mandate equal 
remuneration for work of equal value), marriage, parenthood and pension. Access to assets are equal53.

by providing training, mentoring and services for the digitalisation of businesses. 
According to UN WOMEN, the limited access to land and credit severely restrict women’s economic prospects and 
autonomy. Some women wishing to form their own businesses often cannot do it due to their need of capital or 
incapacity of complying with some requirements such as having a formal account. The National Banking and Insurance

compiled and promoting access to and use of financial services and products by women.

In addition, the agricultural sector has lost almost a third of its income over the past 20 years, partly due to the decline 
in prices for the country's export crops, especially bananas and coffee.55.  
Rural women and youth are particularly vulnerable, impacted by violence, migrations and poverty. 50% of rural 
population is composed of women but only 27% of this population is economically active. Women are involved in much 
lesser extent in agricultural activities with limited access to land or financing. There is an invisibility phenomenon of the 
contributions women do in agriculture hidden by the domestic tasks they usually perform. Rural women combine 
agriculture activities (mostly related to subsistence production and taking care of nutrition with their households) with 
other the economic activities. In spite of having achieved greater educational results, opportunities have not increased 
for rural women and gender gaps have not changed. Strong patriarcal culture continues to prevent rural women from 
reaching their economic autonomy. Poverty in rural areas has affected the women-headed households in a 
disproportional manner 
Initiatives related to the empowerment of rural women have included the adoption of the Act and regulations on credit 
for rural women and the launch of the microenterprise credit line “Working Capital for Women”. In 2019, the National 
Institute for Women advised the Ministry of Natural Resources and the Environment on the creation of the Gender 
Equity and Environment Strategy with a view to eliminating gender inequalities related to the environment and 
encouraging women’s participation in the development, implementation and evaluation of environmental policies and 
programmes for sustainable development. 
The country is prone to natural disasters. Climate events such as droughts increase women’s vulnerability. Decreases in 
the quantity and distribution of rainfall lead not only to failed crops, but also to fewer employment and income 
opportunities, price increases, and changes in the consumption patterns of the most vulnerable population. Therefore, 
the most impacted families are landless producers or those with small plots dedicated to subsistence farming, day 
labourers and women heads of households56. With regard to the active inclusion of women in the design and 
implementation of climate change policies, the School for Equality and Empowerment of Rural Women offers advice 
and support to rural women. The initiative is designed to develop skills and promote equality and equity in the use, 
conservation and preservation of the environment and natural resources. 
 
1.2.2.6. Mexico 
Mexico is the second-largest economy in Latin America. Despite being a large, upper-middle-income country, Mexico 
continues to have high rural poverty levels and wide social and economic disparities.   
The country has made significant progress in the achievement of women’s rights and gender equality, especially in key 
areas at federal level: strengthening of national laws to ensure women and men equality; strong gender institutionalism 
and increased public resources earmarked for gender equality. The most prominent legislation at the federal level to 
promote gender equality is the National Constitution57, the Federal Law for the Prevention and Sanctioning of

on advancing substantive equality around 6 strategic goals (economic autonomy, domestic work, health rights, violence, 
decision-making and safe environments). Important milestones recently achieved were the Political-Electoral Reform 
that elevated gender parity to a constitutional level for candidacies to Federal and State Congress.  
Despite the progress made, gender equality in Mexico faces a series of structural challenges characterized by an 
unrelenting gap between formal and substantive equality in all areas of development. Besides, a cause for concern is 
the escalation of generalized violence in several target areas of the country, showing patterns of human rights violations 
and impunity at all levels, in particular enforced disappearances, torture, arbitrary detention, as well as attacks on 
human rights activists and journalist men and women alike.

Regarding entrepreneurship, smallholder farmers and MSME, there are 4 million SMEs, of which 97.4% are 
microenterprises that represent 12.4% of total gross production and employ 47.2% of the workforce64. Altogether 
account for 99.7% of all private enterprises, 62.6% of private-sector employment and 35.2% of national total gross 
production. 
Out of 49 economies, Mexico rank 18 in TEA, 49 in EBO and 42 in informal investment.The national entrepreneurship 
context substantially worsened over the pandemic period in Mexico. The score fell from 4.7 in 2019 to 4.1 in 2020 and 
3.9 in 2022, in part due to be heavily dependent on tourism. There is not a significant gender gap in entrepreneurship 
in TEA (13.8% male: 12.1% female) nor EBO (2.2% male: 1.0% female)65, scoring better than other countries of the 
project. 
According to the IDB study on female leadership, 33% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 
27% of women in senior management positions. 
Mexico is one of the most populous countries in LAC, with more than six million microfinance borrowers, the largest 
number in the region with nearly 5,000 FSPs but has the smallest loan portolio  which implies a small average loan size 
provided mainly through the group lending approach, the dominant model in the country to reach low-income 
entrepreneurs and non-entrepreneurs66. Group lending is a good entry point for the unbanked into the formal financial 
sector but it does not serve well microenterprises as they grow. Since 80% of Mexico microfinance borrowers are 
women, moving to an individual lending model will affect women’s access to finance. There seems to be a big potential 
to grow the financial services sector in Mexico. 
In 2018, the average interest rates varied according to the amounts of the loans and the size of the borrowing company. 
For large companies, the average interest rate was approximately 11.78%; for SMEs, the average was 17.70%. In recent

In a recent study by Value for Women on women-led small and growing businesses67 found that women-led SMEs are 
smaller than those led by men, which limits their economic potential. The percentage of business led by men with 
profits over $4 million MXN ($222,000 USD) is twice that of women-led. The study also found that in Mexico 70% of 
women were looking for finance. Yet, according to this report, many Mexican women-led businesses (42%) have never 
applied for credit due to high interest rates and aversion to indebtedness but also due to lack of appropriate credit 
products. 93% of the sample said they believe that there is a lack of financing opportunities available to and accessible 
for women–led businesses specifically, while very few (14%) reported knowing about opportunities for financing that 
exist for women-led businesses. 
Socioeconomic disparities are more marked in rural areas, where 61% of the indigenous population is extremely poor, 
compared with only 19% of the non-indigenous rural population. Although there is no gender gap in the poverty rate 
(44% of women versus 43% of men), women experience higher levels of deprivation in terms of food, healthcare and 
education. Most poor women live in rural areas68  
Mexico's agriculture sector is facing increasing vulnerabilities and a degraded natural resource base. Rural people are 
constrained by limited human and social assets, inadequate production-related investments and value-adding 
infrastructure, scarce financial resources and services, weak public-sector technical assistance and poor access to 
markets. These constraints result in low productivity, depressed consumption and incomes, and poverty. 
A key factor in the sector’s performance is a lack of smallholder access to financing. Although Mexico has various 
national development banks, access to rural finance remains a challenge. Women face additional obstacles such as 
access to land and inputs and restrictions on decision-making in the home. The insertion of women in productive 
activities requires considering and knowing the role that perform in society, especially taking into account the increase 
in their responsibilities, due to the migration of man69.  
Low-income households are increasingly dependent on women's work. Increasing their income requires access to 
microcredits, productive inputs, training and access to better markets. The incursion of women into productive activities 
has repercussions on economic growth and in a better distribution of income, since they are agents that promote the 
education and well-being of children, resulting in an increase in the human capital of future generations. 
Crops that are key to the smallholder economy, such as coffee and seasonal white corn, are frequently exposed to 
shocks such as climate-related events or market imperfections. The country also faces vulnerability to climate-related 
disasters exacerbated by inappropriate agricultural practices70. 
 
1.2.2.7. Panama 
 
In Panama, the basis for gender equality is the Political Constitution of the Republic, whose article 19 establishes that 
“there will be no privileges or discrimination based on race, birth, social class, sex, religion or political ideas”.  According 
to UN Women, in Panama, 83.3% of legal frameworks that promote, enforce and monitor gender equality, with a focus 
on violence against women, are in place.

An important milestone was the creation of the National Institute for Women (INAMU) by law No. 71 of December 23, 
2008 as a decentralized public entity that ensures compliance with legal instruments, international agreements ratified 
by Panama and other national laws and regulations related to equal opportunities for women. 
Despite recent good progress and a low level of disparity compare with other countries in Latin America, work still needs 
to be done in Panama to achieve gender equality. The National Strategic Plan 2020-202471 recognizes that there are 
still forms of discrimination against women and highlights several subjects to be improved. According to INAMU, major 
issues that affect women in Panama relate to unequal employment opportunities (only 39% of productive work force 
are women), unequal salaries, domestic violence and femicide. At national level, disparity of labor income between men 
and women is 0.61.  Panamanian government is committed to the implementation of the Public Policies for Equality of 
Opportunities for Women and has designed an Action Plan for the equality of opportunities for women 2014-2024. 
Major challenges remain in the area of interinstitutional coordination and the assignment of a specific budget line for 
gender for the implementation of the policies as well as investment in entrepreneur cooperatives financing, microcredit 
programs, education related to rural development, environment management and climate change impacts72. 
Women, Business and Law Index scores 79.4, with legal constraints found in the areas of pay (law does not mandate 
equal remuneration for work of equal value), marriage, parenthood, entrepreneurship (law does not mandate 
discrimination in access to credit based on gender) and pension. Access to assets are equal73.

and as productivity levels sectors increase, the participation of women is getting smaller74.

Women in Panama own 12,44% of microenterprises and 11.04% of small and medium enterprises75. Out of 49 
economies, Panama rank 3 in TEA, 28 in EBO and 8 in informal investment. The proportion of adults starting or running 
a new business is highest in five LAC countries, being Panama (together with Guatemala) one of them. Panama is one 
of the 10 economies which had more than one in 20 of their adults both starting or running a new business and 
expecting to employ an additional six or more people in five years time. There is a significant gender gap in 
entrepreneurship in TEA (31.2% male: 24.6% female) and EBO (7.9% male: 3.7% female)76 
MSMEs and smallholder farmers represent 96% of all companies in Panama, generating 45% of total employment 
according to AMPYME. Also, around 51% of all MSMEs and smallholder farmers are concentrated in Panama capital and 
more than 57% of microenterprises are led by women. 
Among the main obstacles that women entrepreneurs face can be mentioned the access to credit. It is necessary to 
finance the seed capital and make more flexible credit conditions when it comes to loans to women entrepreneurs. In 
addition to this, there is a limited supply of microfinance services that does not supply the great demand for it 77. 
Other potential barriers that can be mentioned are that most regulatory standards are designed in a gender-neutral 
way. Women tend to feel less prepared, and businesswomen report not having sufficient information on the procedures 
for business registration and compliance with other rules. Besides, traditional gender roles continue to 
disproportionately assign family and domestic responsibilities to women78. 
According to the IDB study on female leadership, 51% of microfinance institutions have a female general manager and 
55% of women in senior management positions. 
More than 50% of the population lives in urban areas which reflects that the rural development has significantly been 
neglected. The rural population is estimated to be composed of 626,000 women y 688,000 man79. Women mostly 
contribute to agriculture production and the design of strategies to confront climate changes but are not considered 
key actors in the elaboration of local, national or international strategies. According to INAMU, climate change impacts 
such as lack of access to water or degradation of the land, unequally affect woman’s rights as well the women’s health.

1.3. MSME and smallholder farmers overview  
The Latin America & Caribbean continent represents ca. 8.4% of the world’s total population80, yet is the third largest 
region in terms of number of MSMEs. The continent is home to 27.5 million MSMEs, of which 26.2 million are 
microenterprises and 1.3 million are SMEs81. In fact, out of 50 countries surveyed by the “Global Entrepreneurship 
Monitor, 2019-20 Report” (GEM Report), the top 6 countries in terms of Total Early-Stage Entrepreneurial Activity (TEA) 
were in Latin America, four of which form part of this multi-country proposal (Brazil, Panama, Guatemala and Colombia). 
The region showed an average TEA of over 24%, compared to ca. 12% in both Africa and in Asia & Oceania and ca. 10% 
in Europe & North America – indicating the importance of MSMEs in Latin America’s populations’ daily livelihoods. The 
MSMEs bring life to LAC economies, they represent 99,5% of all companies, create 60% of the total employment and 
generate around a 25% of the regional GDP82.  
Women in the region have a strong history of entrepreneurial activity, which can be seen via the high female/male-led 
TEAs ratio for Latin America: 0.84 in comparison with 0.72 for the countries surveyed in Africa, 0.69 for those in Asia & 
Oceania and 0.67 in Europe & North America. Despite this, Latin American women entrepreneurs are often driven more 
by necessity than in the case of men – a seemingly general standpoint for women worldwide, as the GEM Report’s 
findings suggest. Out of 50 countries surveyed, in 36 countries more women than men cited that their main motivation 
for working as an entrepreneur was ‘to earn a living because jobs are scarce’. Furthermore, in each of the 8 countries 
surveyed in Latin America (of which 6 are relevant to this multi-country proposal), whilst both men and women cited 
this reason as their main motivation for working as an entrepreneur, the % of women agreeing with this statement in 
these countries was 7% higher than males (88.2% and 81.3% respectively). In this regard, supporting women 
entrepreneurs in their activities via financing and other mechanisms gains further importance given the fact that limited 
other options are available. 
In 2019, women represented 50.8% of Latin America’s total population, yet only 41% of the region’s total employed 
population. In line with this, the labour force participation rate of men (77% in 2019, according to ILO data as of Jun-
20) is significantly higher than that of women (52%), the latter of which has only increased slightly since 2010 (51%). 
Despite the region’s high rate of women participation in entrepreneurial activities in comparison with other 
geographies, many women are stuck in the informal sector, lacking access to financial, human, and social capital – key 
constraints for the growth of their businesses83. Over 60% of women in the Latin America & Caribbean region operate 
in the informal sector, unable to grow out of the informal economy due to a lack of women role models in business, 
social/cultural constraints, and greater domestic responsibilities.  
 
Financial inclusion 
Latin America is the region with the second highest level of failure in women-led businesses in the world, with women 
entrepreneurs highlighting financing constraints as the main reason for business discontinuation (as opposed to poor 
profitability, in the case of men). Whilst access to microfinance for women is easier, women are often not able to access 
more “sophisticated” products that provide the appropriate terms and conditions that will enable their 
microenterprises to grow into more established SMEs. Increasing women’s entrepreneurs’ access to capital is therefore 
key in ensuring the sustainability of MSMEs and smallholder farmers and boosting female livelihoods, whereby for Latin 
 
 
80 World Bank data. 
81 “International Finance Corporation. 2017. MSME Finance Gap: Assessment of the Shortfalls and Opportunities in Financing Micro, Small, and Medium 
Enterprises in Emerging Markets. Washington, DC. © International Finance Corporation. https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/28881  
82 OECD/CAF (2019), Latin America and the Caribbean 2019: Policies for Competitive SMEs in the Pacific Alliance and Participating South American 
countries, SME Policy Index, OECD Publishing, Paris, https://doi.org/10.1787/d9e1e5f0-en

=== PAGE 165 ===
24 
 
America a financing gap worth USD 5 billion has been identified for women-led micro-businesses alone, and an 
additional shortfall exits worth USD 93 billion for women-led SMEs84.  
Women have shown to be responsible and loyal financial services clients, whilst BCG analysis found that as much as 
USD 2.5-5 trillion could be added to global economic growth if women and men participated equally as entrepreneurs85. 
Women clients can contribute to reduced client attrition and portfolio risk, and to increased fee income, thanks to 
women’s responsible behaviour towards loan repayments but also to their interest in savings and other complementary 
products that financial institutions can offer86. As per Global Banking Alliance findings, women are 16% more likely to 
save than men, whilst women also show higher aversion toward indebtedness than men.  
Several barriers limit in general the financial inclusion of women. On the supply side, there are physical barriers (related 
to travel and time costs to a financial institution, insecurity and restrictions to mobility and social interactions outside 
the home), economic barriers (related to lack of autonomy and decision-making power about income and expenses as 
well as less labour opportunities) and eligibility barriers related to greater difficulty in obtaining an official 
documentation and collateral, direct and indirect discrimination by the financial institution (based on biases, conscious 
and unconscious, about women’s roles) or even the need of permission or signature of husbands. On the demand side, 
women often have less self-esteem in their skills and capabilities, less levels of financial literacy and can even have less 
confidence in financial institutions87.  
On top of these constraints, it is also very relevant to take into account social norms and their influence on the different 
barriers identified. Gender norms are a part of social norms and are defined as “collectively held expectations and 
perceived rules for how individuals should behave based on their gender identity”88. In LAC region, overload of unpaid 
work, domestic and agricultural activities as well as patriarchal norms about the role of women limit their autonomy in 
social and economic fields, especially in rural areas and for indigenous women. The Gender Social Norms Index89 show 
that the % of people with no bias against gender equality in areas such as politics, economic, education, intimate partner 
violence and women’s reproductive rights worldwide is 14% of women and 10% of men. Data for target countries are 
10,50% in Brazil, 8,60% in Colombia and 12,30% in Mexico. 
The project will implement measures to take into account those barriers and will set targets to reach women-owned 
SMEs and female smallholder farmers which were previously unbanked. Compilation of sex-dissagregated data, 
trainings on gender equality and women empowerment (GEWE), gender and SEAH policy and action plans implemented 
at every FSP and business skills and financial literacy sessions, which will include concepts such as business 
management, innovation for business expansion, strategic decision-making, financial management, cash flows, savings 
investment and credit, will help to challenge constraints in the women’s participation. As a minimum, 5% of total 
attendees will be indigenous women and 5% afrodescendant women. 
The project will also identify opportunities and challenges of targeting women as clients such as the design of financial 
products that target women and vulnerable populations. Outcomes of this activity will depend on FSP and country 
context since every FSP will design the financial products that best adapt to women’s realities and needs.

Participants in the financial system need to be aware of and understand how gender norms limit women’s access and 
use of financial services even in aspects where the law has been changed but endure as a social norm90. Barriers and 
social norms will be taken into account in the trainings implemented as part of the GAP aimed at supporting other 
activities and their effectiveness. 
Consultants hired will make emphasis on specific barriers of each country and partnerships with women’s organisations 
will also help to ensure a more enhanced knowledge about women’s situations and influence other stakeholders.  
Several activities in the GAP (e.g. 2.3, 2.6, 3.1, 3.2) are aimed at enabling women get the funding they need. 
 
Agriculture and rural areas 
Regarding agriculture, women play a key role in food production, nutrition, and natural resources management. 
According to FAO, women provide around 20% of all agricultural employment. However, there are specific issues that 
make women entrepreneurs more prone to inequality compared with their male counterparts, such us: land rights, 
productive resources, unpaid work, employment and decision making. 
In the case of rural Central America, inequality is deepened by the structural consequences of urban-centric bias in the 
distribution of strategic investments for national development. According to a study by ECLAC on women in the 
agricultural sector, it reveals that in 2010 women constituted only approximately 28% of the rural economically active 
population in Central America. A high proportion of rural women employed in agriculture are unpaid family workers 
which puts them at a disadvantage, as they do not receive financial reward for their activities.  Increasing the income 
of these women means raising the resources of more than 40% of poor rural households in Central America where they 
are the household head. This would translate into improvements in education and nutrition of children, and in higher 
economic growth of the countries. 
Women are less likely to hold statutory land rights, and, when they do own land, their plots are often relatively small 
due to many customary land rights systems. The need of collateral and asset-based lending implies that women have 
to own and control assets to unlock finance. Collateral requirements are generally much stricter for women, and 
customary laws and social beliefs favor men and put rural women at a great disadvantage. Being deprived of access to 
key assets and employment directly impacts access to credit, making it more difficult to acquire production inputs and 
technologies that would enable them to increase their productivity. This is also a missed opportunity since developing 
good products and services tailored to the specific needs of women entrepreneurs, financial institutions could capture 
an underserved market and boost the entrepreneurial potential of an entire generation. 
Moreover, they often are expected to provide unpaid farm work, and bear a disproportionate burden of care and 
reproductive roles within the family and community. 
As said above, the project will implement measures to take into account women’s barriers in providing guarantees and 
will set targets to reach women farmers. Awareness about social norms will also be part of the activities implemented 
so those root causes of gender inequality are better known for all participants. 
 
Gender and climate change 
Women are also more vulnerable to the effects of climate change, as they spend more time and energy on activities 
related to their environment. The United Nations Development Program (UNDP) and the United Nations Environment 
Program (UN AMBIENTE) have identified 4 major areas that link the environment and poverty, including women and 
 
 
90 See note 44

=== PAGE 167 ===
26 
 
girls: direct dependence on natural resources, vulnerability to climatic shocks such as floods and landslides, health 
effects from the indiscriminate use of agrochemicals, substances that deplete the ozone layer or poor waste disposal. 
These effects can increase gender inequality, reduces women's ability to be financially independent, and has an overall 
negative impact on the social and political rights of women, especially in economies that are heavily based on 
agriculture91. As a result, women and girls find themselves with less time for education, income-generating activities, 
and participation in community decision making processes, further entrenching unequal gender relations. 
Global studies show that 70% of consumer decisions are made by women. However, in decision-making in general, 
women do not achieve positions of power on an equal basis with men. This applies to issues of climate change and 
biodiversity. Women must participate in decision-making processes regarding access, use and control of natural 
resources, as well as in the equitable distribution of the resulting benefits. 
Public environmental policies for the conservation of biodiversity and climate actions, among others, must incorporate 
gender equity, including access to training, education, access to tools or mechanisms for entrepreneurship.  
Currently, Financial Service Providers (FSPs) included in the multi-country proposal reach 40% women in general 
through products and services. Typical products provided are MSME loans, village banking, and in some cases, specific 
agricultural and education loans. In relation to health and education services, FSPs generally provide health capacity 
building, specifically during COVID-19 pandemic, and other education related to financial literacy and agricultural best 
practices. 
Women-owned MSMEs and women farmers reach by FSPs is much lower, thus, the Kuali Fund-GCF aims to contribute 
increasing their reach by FSPs and improving products and services offered. By achieving these improvements, it is 
expected that women end-clients will be empowered. 
The project will include an activity aimed at improving awareness about the potential to increase income, reduce CO2 
emissions and improve adaptation through green strategies financed by FSPs.

development and the activities that will be implemented as part of the GAP to address them, taking into account that 
all are interrelated.

1.4. Other aspects in the implementation of the Kuali Fund-GCF 
Country assessments will be updated and extended once Kuali Fund-GCF is approved and the countries are confirmed. 
The country assessments will be updated by external consultants prior to the due diligence. Moreover, the Gender 
Action Plan will be adjusted as the project progresses. Updated gender country assessments, including SEAH, and 
gender action plan will be resubmitted to GCF. 
Kuali Fund-GCF’s stakeholders92 include women smallholder farmers and women-owned SMEs as borrowers of FSPs in 
order to increase their participation in the proposed activities and ensure that their concerns are heard, taken into 
account and influence decisions that affect their wellbeing and livelihoods. Women’s organisations will also be identified 
in each country and consultations with them will be conducted to incorporate their knowledge and views in the

A gender gap assessment will be conducted in each investee which together with the country information and the 
insights of the women’s organisations, will be the basis for developing specific gender policy and procedure93. Gender 
Officer will report to the Investment Committee whenever an investment is to be approved and provide gender 
information to decision-making committees. Specific covenants regarding gender equality will be introduced in loan 
agreements with FSPs and ICs. The Gender Officer will monitor quaterly the investees gender and SEAH indicators and 
FSPs / IC will provide data on an annual basis as part of an ES performance report94. Finally, Kuali Fund-GCF will prepare 
an ES monitoring report for COFIDES with data and information on the implementation of the GAP.  
 
Human resources in GAWA for gender activities 
A Gender Officer will be appointed and an E&S Officer with expertise in gender will be hired and provide support to the 
Gender Officer. The Gender Officer will be in charge of integrating the gender approach across the full investment cycle 
(analysis, legal agreementas and monitoring) Gender Officer will also support Kuali Fund-GCF’s staff on gender issues 
at any time95. GAWA will support with specialized gender and SEAH training to these officers. Training will be also 
provided to the whole team to enhance competencies and knowledge in the areas of gender, SEAH and social inclusion. 
Moreover, external consultants can be hired to provide support to Kuali Fund manager and Gender Officer on the 
elaboration of Gender Action Plans of external and internal dimensions of investees as well as for the design of financial 
products adapted to women’s needs. Priority will be given to local expert consultants. 
Within FSPs, there will be the possibility of naming a focal person in gender issues, in order to execute the GAPs more 
effectively.

and barriers), equal remuneration for 
work of equal value, and a sexual 
harassment protocol. It will include 
mechanisms of monitoring and 
reporting
